Chipload 0.40.0 user guide

Chipload

Chipload is 2.5D CAM for CNC routers and mills: a drawing in, checked G-code out.

  • In: shapes drawn and edited in Chipload (lines, arcs, circles, rectangles, polygons, slots; offset, fillet, trim, union and subtract, arrays; the Compass to move, turn and copy), DXF and SVG drawings, text in built-in fonts, pictures traced into shapes, and 3D models (STL) and depth maps for reliefs. Drawn and imported shapes mix in one project, and the drawing exports as a DXF too.
  • Out: G-code for your controller, in mm or inches, through a post-processor: GRBL, LinuxCNC and Mach3/4 are built in, and you can write your own.
  • Operations:
    • profiles, spiralling down, with tabs, finishing passes, onion skins and dogbones;
    • ring pockets, with helix entry, rest machining and a finishing pass;
    • V-carving, and prisms: raised shapes with a ridge along their centre;
    • chamfers on any closed outline;
    • follow path, and flutes: grooves that ramp in and out;
    • fill patterns: lines, cross-hatch, rings, waves, and short fluted strokes;
    • mouldings: a profile (cove, ogee, bead…) swept along a path;
    • drilled and helically bored holes;
    • surfacing the stock;
    • reliefs: a 3D model (STL), a depth-map picture or a picture’s shapes inflated, carved from the top, with a rim, roughing and smoothing.
  • Checked: every program passes an independent checker before Chipload writes it. The checker is separate code that reads the G-code back and refuses rapids through the stock, cuts outside the machine, and other mistakes.
  • Seen before cut: a 3D view, a material-removal simulation, a playback slider, the cycle time, the chip load, and a setup sheet for the operator.
  • Scriptable: everything works from the command line too, with JSON output for scripts, and AI assistants can drive it.
  • mm or inches: the app shows lengths in either, and the G-code can be in either.

Testers with real machines wanted. Chipload’s programs pass its checker and many tests, and they cut wood on a GRBL router (a Shapeoko), but they haven’t run on LinuxCNC or Mach3/4 yet, nor in inches. If you have a CNC router or mill, please try it, starting with an air cut, and tell us how it went in an issue, also when it worked. The README says what to try and what to send.

Safety

CNC machines are dangerous. A wrong program can break tools, throw parts or swarf, damage the machine and injure people. The checker catches the mistakes it knows about, but it can’t know your clamps, your fixture, your tool or your machine’s real state. You are responsible for every program you run:

  1. Look at the toolpath: in Chipload’s 3D view and simulation, or a G-code viewer such as NC Viewer or CAMotics. Check the depths, the work zero, clamps in the way, the order of the cuts and what holds the part.
  2. Air-cut first: set the work zero above the stock (or raise Z) and run the program without cutting, with a hand on the stop button.
  3. Only then cut, and never leave a running machine unattended.

Chipload comes with no warranty (GPL-3.0, sections 15 and 16). Its licence and the licences of everything it is built with are under Help > About and licences, and chipload licenses prints them.

What Chipload doesn’t do

Chipload is meant to be lean. It leaves out what other tools do better:

  • machining driven by a 3D CAD model (features and depths from a STEP file): CAM connected to your CAD does that better. Chipload does carve 3D shapes from 2D input: reliefs from the top, and mouldings;
  • sending G-code to the machine (use your controller’s sender);
  • CAD beyond the shapes a part needs.

This guide

This guide is for Chipload’s version in the title.

  • Getting started: install Chipload and make your first part.
  • Concepts: what every operation shares: units, stock and zero, depths, sides, tools, and the checker.
  • One page per kind of operation, then tools, machines and posts.
  • Before cutting: problems, the simulation, times and the setup sheet.
  • Scripting and the generated reference pages, for every command and every field of the files.

Installing

Download Chipload from the releases page. For the app you need one file: the one named chipload-X.Y.Z-… for your system (below).

Chipload is two programs that read and write the same project files and make the same G-code:

  • the desktop app, in the files named chipload-X.Y.Z-…;
  • the command line chipload, for scripts and automation, in the archives named chipload-cli-X.Y.Z-…. You don’t need it to use the app.

Each release also has the user guide to read offline and SHA256SUMS.txt to check the files.

Linux

Download chipload-X.Y.Z-linux-x86_64.AppImage, make it executable and run it:

chmod +x chipload-*.AppImage
./chipload-*.AppImage                # the app; add a .chipload or .dxf file to open it
  • ARM64 (Raspberry Pi 4 or 5 with a 64-bit OS, ARM boards and laptops): take chipload-X.Y.Z-linux-aarch64.AppImage instead. The app needs OpenGL 3, which a Pi 4 or 5 has with its standard graphics driver. Not yet tried on a Pi: reports welcome.
  • Requirements: glibc 2.35 or newer (Ubuntu 22.04, Debian 12, Fedora 36 or newer, Raspberry Pi OS 12 “Bookworm”).
  • Command line: it’s in chipload-cli-X.Y.Z-linux-x86_64.tar.gz (ARM64: …-linux-aarch64.tar.gz). Unpack it and put chipload on your PATH.
  • File dialogs: these need an XDG desktop portal or zenity. Without them, use File > Open path… (and Import image from path…, Update drawing from path…), or give the files on the command line.

Windows

Download chipload-X.Y.Z-windows-x86_64-setup.exe and run it. On a Windows laptop with an ARM processor (Snapdragon), take chipload-X.Y.Z-windows-aarch64-setup.exe instead. It installs Chipload for you alone, without asking for administrator rights, into %LOCALAPPDATA%\Programs\Chipload (you can choose another folder), and then:

  • .chipload files open in Chipload with a double-click, and show its icon;
  • DXF and SVG files have Chipload under Open with; the program that opens them now stays as it is;
  • Chipload is in the Start menu.

To update, run the newer installer: it replaces the old version in place. To remove Chipload, use Settings > Apps. Your settings, tools, machines, materials and posts stay, for when you install it again.

The installer isn’t code-signed, so Windows SmartScreen may warn the first time. Choose More info > Run anyway.

  • Without installing: chipload-X.Y.Z-windows-x86_64.exe (…-aarch64.exe) is the app as one file, to run from anywhere, a USB stick too. Double-clicking a project won’t open it; use File > Open in the app.
  • Command line: the installer puts chipload.exe beside the app, in the folder above; run it from a terminal (PowerShell or Command Prompt) with its full path, e.g. & "$env:LOCALAPPDATA\Programs\Chipload\chipload.exe" --version. It comes only with the installer.

macOS

No release has macOS files yet. Until one does, build Chipload from the source (below). Once there: download chipload-X.Y.Z-macos-universal.dmg, open it and drag Chipload onto Applications. It runs on Apple silicon and Intel Macs with macOS 11 or newer.

The app isn’t notarised by Apple, so macOS refuses it the first time. Open *System Settings

Privacy & Security*, scroll down to the message about Chipload and choose Open Anyway.

  • Command line: it’s in chipload-cli-X.Y.Z-macos-universal.tar.gz. Unpack it and put chipload on your PATH. If macOS stops it, run xattr -d com.apple.quarantine chipload once.
  • Opening files: use File > Open in the app; double-clicking a .chipload file in Finder doesn’t open it yet.

From source

Install Rust with rustup, then:

git clone https://github.com/chipload/chipload
cd chipload
cargo build --release -p chipload-gui -p chipload-cli
# target/release/chipload-gui and target/release/chipload

New versions

The first time it starts, Chipload asks whether to check for new versions. If you say yes, it asks GitHub once a day, when it starts, for the latest release. That is one request: nothing about you, your computer or your projects is sent, though GitHub sees your network address as it does for any visit. When a newer version is out, the status bar says Chipload X is available; click it for What’s new… (the release’s page in your browser) or Skip this version. The same is under Help. Chipload never downloads or installs anything by itself: download the new version as you did this one.

Help > Check for new versions now checks at once, also with the daily check off, and says what it found. Change your answer in Preferences > New versions. Behind a proxy, set HTTPS_PROXY.

Your settings

Chipload keeps your tool library, machine profiles, styles (styles.json), own post-processors (in posts/), fonts for texts that you haven’t installed (in fonts/), app settings and the arrangement of the window’s panels (layout.json) in one folder:

SystemFolder
Linux$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/chipload/, usually ~/.config/chipload/
Windows%APPDATA%\chipload\
macOS~/Library/Application Support/chipload/
Anythe folder in the environment variable CHIPLOAD_CONFIG_DIR, if set

A project keeps its own copy of the tools, machine and post it uses, so it cuts the same on any computer, whatever that computer’s library holds.

Your first part

This walks through one part, from nothing to G-code: a plate of 160 × 100 mm with rounded corners and four holes, drawn in Chipload and cut out with profiles. The same steps follow in the app and on the command line. Starting from a DXF or an SVG instead differs only in the first steps (below).

In the app

  1. A new project: New project on the start screen; with a project open, File > New… (Ctrl+N) shows the same choices, then New project.

    • The new project dialog asks for the material, the board’s thickness and size, Z zero, the machine and the tools. Set the real thickness, 12 mm here: depths are measured from the board’s top. Leave the board on Fit the drawing: it grows round what you draw. Enter creates the project.

  2. Draw the plate: press R (or the rectangle in the ribbon’s DRAW group) and click two opposite corners on the canvas, anywhere. You draw on the canvas’s Drawing tab, where a new project opens (the canvas).

    • The rectangle stays selected, and its values are under Selection in the Properties panel on the right. Type the exact ones: centre 0, 0, length 160, width 100, corner radius 8.
    • Clicks land on the grid and snap to what is drawn, so values usually start out round.

  3. Draw the holes: press C, then type -65,-35 and Enter for the centre, and 4 and Enter for the radius. A typed value opens beside the cursor as you type (Exact values).

    • Enter starts the circle again: 65,-35, 65,35 and -65,35, each with radius 4.
    • Esc ends the tool. Every drawn shape can be changed later: drag its handles, or type its values under Selection. Drawing shapes has the other tools: lines and arcs, polygons, slots, offsets, fillets, arrays.

  4. Check the stock and the machine: Stock in the Browser on the left is the board you chose; Machine shows the machine. Clicking Stock shows the board’s own tab, Setup 1: the plate as it will be cut. Click Drawing to go back. Work zero is at the stock’s bottom-left corner (the Work zero row; drag it in the view to move it). Without a tool library and machine profiles, a project has the GRBL post, a safe height of 5 mm and a 6 mm endmill at 18 000 rpm.

  5. Select what to cut: drag a box from left to right round the whole plate: it selects every shape wholly inside it.

    • A click selects one shape, Ctrl+click adds one; right-click selects a whole layer.
    • Right-click a shape for Select similar (every circle of its diameter, or every shape of its size on its layer) or Select inside (the closed shapes within an outline: a part’s pockets and holes). With Ctrl held they add to the selection. On the command line, chipload shape similar plate.chipload 2 and chipload shape inside plate.chipload 1 print the shape ids, for op add … --shapes.

  6. Add an operation: Profile, in the ribbon’s OPERATION group.

    • Quicker: press S over the canvas, then P. The quick menu opens at the mouse with a key for each kind (P profile, K pocket, H holes, V V-carve, F follow path, C chamfer, L flute, E pattern, R prism, M moulding) and a digit for each of your styles. A kind the selection can’t take is greyed out, and its hover says why (holes need a circle, a pocket a closed shape). Escape closes it.
    • Side: an outline is cut outside and holes inside. Selected together, as here, they become two profiles, the holes first, so the plate is still held while they are cut.
    • End depth: for a cut-out, the stock thickness plus a little, e.g. 12.5 mm into a spoilboard. More than the allowed overcut (0.5 mm by default, in the setup’s Stock) is refused.
    • Step down: how deep each pass goes.
    • Tabs: they keep the part from coming loose on the last pass. The outline’s profile has them; change them under Tabs.

  7. Generate: F5, or leave View > Update toolpath automatically on.

    • The toolpath shows on the canvas: arrows give the cutting direction, rapids are dashed, and each operation’s number sits at its first plunge.
    • Problems show in red on the field they’re about.
    • Warnings appear in the report.

  8. Look at it: 3D shows the toolpath at its real depths. Simulate (F6) shows the stock as cut, and the slider under the canvas plays the program back; F6 again goes back to editing.

  9. Export: File > Export G-code (Ctrl+E).

    • You can only export once the checker has passed the program.
    • The first time, it asks where to write. After that, Ctrl+E writes the same file again without asking, as Ctrl+S saves, so a changed depth is one key away from the machine. The project remembers its file for next time. File > Export G-code as… (Ctrl+Shift+E) always asks.
    • Next to the G-code comes a setup sheet (plate-sheet.html) for the operator: stock, zero, tools in order, operations and times.

  10. Save the project: Ctrl+S, as plate.chipload.

On the command line

chipload new -o plate.chipload --thickness 12 --size 200x150    # an empty project
chipload shape add plate.chipload rectangle --center 0,0 --length 160 --width 100 \
  --corner-radius 8                                              # shape 1
for at in -65,-35 65,-35 65,35 -65,35; do
  chipload shape add plate.chipload circle --center $at --radius 4   # shapes 2 to 5
done
chipload op add plate.chipload profile --shapes 1 2 3 4 5 --through
chipload plan plate.chipload                       # warnings, cycle time, chip load
chipload run plate.chipload -o plate.nc            # checked G-code and plate-sheet.html
  • new takes the rest from your library’s defaults, as the dialog does; --fit 10 in place of --size fits the board round the drawing.
  • op add makes the same two profiles the app does, the holes first, with the defaults the app gives (--through: through the board, into the spoilboard by the allowed overcut). Every value can be changed afterwards with set (see Scripting).
  • The app makes the same project. Open plate.chipload in it to see and change it.

Starting from a drawing file

A part drawn in a CAD program comes in as a DXF or an SVG, and the rest is the same:

  • In the app: File > Import DXF, SVG or STL… (Ctrl+I) in place of New project: the same dialog opens, and the drawing becomes part of the project. If a DXF doesn’t say whether it’s in mm or inches, Chipload asks; it never guesses. The report under the canvas lists what was imported, and anything skipped or repaired: duplicate lines, gaps it closed, splines turned into arcs.
  • Selecting: a single click on the canvas takes the whole drawing file; double-click takes one outline. A drawing file or one of its layers can be clicked in the Browser.
  • On the command line: chipload import plate.dxf -o plate.chipload (add --units mm if the DXF doesn’t say), then chipload inspect plate.chipload for the contours with their numbers and layers, and op add plate.chipload profile --layer CUT for every shape on a layer.
  • The project keeps the drawing itself, so the file isn’t needed again unless it changes; then File > Update drawing from DXF or SVG… takes the new version and keeps your operations (Updating). Imported and drawn shapes mix freely in one project.

The checker

Before Chipload writes a program, an independent checker reads the G-code back and refuses it if it finds, among other things:

  • a rapid move (G0) down into the stock, or sideways while the tool is below the safe height;
  • a cut deeper than the stock bottom plus the allowed overcut;
  • a move beyond the machine’s travel, when the travel is set;
  • an arc whose ends don’t lie on its circle;
  • a cutting move with no feed, or with the spindle off;
  • other units than the machine’s, or relative coordinates;
  • a tool change with the spindle running or below the safe height.

The checker is separate code that shares nothing with the part of Chipload that makes the toolpath, so a bug in one is likely caught by the other. chipload check part.nc --job part.chipload runs it on any G-code, also from other programs.

Before you cut

  • Work zero: the setup sheet says where X0 Y0 and Z0 are. Set them on the machine exactly there.
  • Clamps: look at the 3D view or the simulation for clamps in the toolpath. Chipload doesn’t know where your clamps are.
  • Air cut: run the program once above the stock.
  • What holds the part: in the last pass, tabs or an onion skin should keep it in place.

The window

The header

At the top, a slim top bar:

  • Left: Open, Save, Undo and Redo, then the menus: File, Edit, View, Draw, Modify, Operation, Wizards, Library and Help.
  • Middle: the project’s name on a tab. A dot on it means unsaved changes. Closing, opening or starting a project with unsaved changes asks first, in the middle of the window: Save (then go on), Don’t save or Cancel.
  • Changed by another program: when the project’s file is saved by another program (an AI assistant, a script), the app loads the new version within a couple of seconds and says so in the report. Ctrl+Z goes back to what you had. If you have unsaved changes, it asks first: Load it (your changes stay one Ctrl+Z away) or Keep mine (your next Save overwrites the file).
  • Right: the colour theme (a click goes System → Light → Dark) and the version.

Under it, the ribbon groups the buttons:

GroupButtonsIts menu also has
FILENew, Open, Import (a DXF, SVG or STL as a new project), Saveopen recent, add a picture, a DXF/SVG or a 3D model, save as, update drawing, export, the …from path items, quit
DRAWthe shape tools as small buttons (Circle, Rectangle, Polyline, Arc, Slot, Polygon, Text: hover for the name and key), Image, DXF/SVG (add a DXF or SVG), 3D model (add an STL for a relief)Frame… (a frame for a sign)
MODIFYsmall buttons: Offset, Fillet, Chamfer, Trim, Extend, Break, Union, Subtract, Intersect, Array, Polar array (Editing operations), and the Compass (Q)the same
OPERATIONProfile, Pocket, V-carve, Follow, Holes (for the selected shapes), and as small buttons Surface (the whole stock), Relief (of the selected 3D model, or the first), Style (one of your styles, for the selected shapes) and Chamfer (under Style), Flute and Pattern (beside them); Prism and Moulding are in the Operation menu and the S menuNew Chamfer, New Flute, New Pattern, New Prism, New Moulding, New Relief, Apply style ▸, Generate
WIZARDSSign: a whole sign in one step, its drawing and its operations; Two-sided: a back side with its dowel pins; Inlay…: a V-carve inlay of the selected shapes; Relief: a whole relief in one stepNew sign…, Two-sided…, New inlay…, Relief…

The View menu has fit, top and 3D view, Z scale, reverse left-right orbit, what is shown, the legend, the panels (open, hide, reset) and update automatically; Measure is on the canvas’s navigation bar.

Generate (F5) and Export sit at the right end; Generate is greyed out until the project has an operation. When the buttons with their names don’t fit, in a longer language or a narrow window, the large buttons show their icons alone; hover over one for its name. Narrower still, the groups scroll sideways.

Each group has the menu of its name in the top bar. Two menus have no group: Edit (undo, redo; copy, paste, mirror, join, delete and group the selected shapes, copy as selector; Preferences…, which opens Preferences in the Browser: units, language, colour theme, the setup sheet) and Help (the user guide, keyboard shortcuts, about). On a Mac the menus show Cmd for the keys written Ctrl in this guide.

The status bar at the bottom gives hints on the left. On the right it shows the cursor’s position, how many times the part is cut (its instances), the cycle time once generated, the post-processor and the units.

The Browser

The Browser on the left holds your library and the whole project as a tree:

  • Library, at the top and folded at the start: what is kept on this computer for every project. It is there also with no project open.
    • Tools and Machines: your tool library and machine profiles.
    • Materials and Styles: your materials and styles, operations saved by name.
    • Post-processors: the built-in ones and yours; click one for what it writes, and Use in this project.
  • Preferences, below the Library: the setup sheet, updating the toolpath by itself, the units, the language, the colour theme and checking for new versions.
  • The project, named after its file.
    • Machine: the post-processor, G-code units, safe Z, travel and tool changes, shown on the right when clicked. A red dot means one of them has a problem.
    • Tools: the project’s tools, each with the library tool it came from at the right (“← 6 mm 2F”, “(changed)” when the values differ). See Tool library.
    • Drawing: what the shapes come from. Each DXF has its layers under it; then come each picture, and the shapes drawn in Chipload by kind (circles, rectangles, polylines). See Several drawings.
    • Stock, Work zero and the parts, each part with its operations, in cutting order, then its instances once it has more than one. With two boards or more they move under Setups, each setup with its own stock, work zero and parts. A line marks each tool change. A floor pass is cut before its V-carve and a rest pass after its pockets, so the line above them names their tools in the order they are cut: tool change → T2 → T1. See Setups and Parts.

Rows:

  • Click a row to select it.
    • A DXF, picture or layer selects its shapes for a new operation, and Ctrl+click adds to the selection.
    • An operation, a part, a setup, its stock or work zero, a tool, Tools, Machine, a DXF (or one of its layers) or a picture shows its settings on the right, in Properties.
  • Double-click a picture for its trace settings, or a part to make it the active part.
  • Right-click a row for the rest:
    • A DXF: Place… (its properties), Update from a DXF… and Remove.
    • A picture: Trace settings… and Remove.
    • An operation: Move up and Move down (also Alt+↑ and Alt+↓), Duplicate, Save as style… (a style), for a pocket Add rest pass ▸ (a rest pass shows under it), for a relief Add roughing pass ▸ (roughing) and Add smoothing pass (smoothing, greyed out until it has a roughing pass), and Delete. On the command line: chipload op move P --op 4 --to 2, op duplicate P --op 2, op remove P --op 3.
    • A style: Apply to the selection and Delete.
    • A rest pass, floor pass, roughing or smoothing pass: Remove (or Delete with it selected); a roughing pass also Add smoothing pass.
    • A project tool: Delete, greyed while an operation or a rest or floor pass cuts with it (its hover says which).
    • The Drawing folder: Add DXF or SVG…, Import image… and Add 3D model….
    • A 3D model: Select, Hide or Show, New relief, Properties and Remove.
    • A part: Activate, Export G-code of this part…, Add instance, Add mirrored instance, New operation (for the selected shapes), Move up, Move down, Move to setup ▸ and Delete part (with its operations).
    • An instance: Mirror, Turn 90° and Remove.
    • The project, or the empty space below the Browser’s rows: New part (on the active setup), Add a setup, Face the board…, Nest… and Nest again (the active setup’s), and Export all setups… with two or more.
    • A setup: Export G-code of this setup…, Face the board…, Nest…, Nest again (once nested), New part, Add a setup, Add the back side and, with two or more, Delete. The Setups folder: Add a setup and Export all setups….
    • A library tool: Add to project, Make default, Delete. A library machine: Use in this project, Make default, Delete. Library > Tools or Machines: New, Import…, Export….
  • Del deletes what Properties shows: an operation or a rest pass picked in the Browser, a text opened there, a project tool nothing cuts with, a library tool, machine or style (as its Delete: the library has no undo, its file keeps a .bak), or else what is selected on the canvas (drawn shapes, or a text, a picture or a DXF as a whole). Parts and setups take their operations with them, so they are deleted only from their right-click menu. Ctrl+Z brings anything back.
  • The triangle opens and closes a folder, and so does a click on a plain folder like Tools.

The eye hides a DXF, layer, picture or kind of drawn shape on the canvas: it isn’t drawn and can’t be clicked. Hidden shapes are still cut.

The tick in front of an operation, a part or an instance turns it on or off. One that is off isn’t in the program, and its row is dimmed; a part that is off takes its operations and instances with it.

The active part is in bold, marked active. New operations from the ribbon go into it, whatever is open. Its name is also in the status bar.

On an operation’s row:

  • Right end: its tool, and how many hidden contours it cuts (“3 hidden · T1”).
  • A red dot marks a problem.
  • Hover the row for its kind, its geometry, its depths, its time once generated and its first problem.
  • Drag the row onto another to move it there, or onto a part to move it to the end of that part.

Drag a library tool onto the project’s Tools to copy it into the project.

The canvas

The canvas shows the stock, the drawing and the toolpath on a soft gradient. It follows the colour theme: dark, or light grey.

  • Two kinds of space, a tab each, along the canvas’s top left. Drawing has a pencil and the app’s green when shown; a setup’s tab has a board (a back the turn-over mark) and its own teal, underlined while another space is shown. When the canvas is too narrow for every name, the tabs not shown keep only their icon (hover for the name):

    • Drawing: every part’s shapes where they are drawn, to draw and change. The part you work on is in full colour, the others dimmed (still clicked and edited; clicking one makes its part the active one). The toolpath is that part’s, where it is drawn. When the part is cut where it is drawn, its board shows faintly behind it, to draw against; no instances are shown here.
    • A tab per setup (“Setup 1”, a back “Setup 2 (back)”): its board with its name above it, and every instance on it drawn where it is cut, with its badge (Side #2, and a mirror mark when mirrored) and the setup’s toolpath. No drawn shapes: here you place, turn, copy and nest the instances. Double-click an instance to open its part in Drawing. Drag a part’s row from the Browser onto the board to place it there again. Right-click empty canvas for the setup’s own menu, as on its Browser row: export it, face or nest its board, a new part or setup, its back side. The other setups lie beside the board as before.
    • + adds a setup.
    • What you select picks the tab: a part, an operation, a shape, a text or a picture shows Drawing; a setup, its stock, its work zero or an instance shows that setup’s tab. The project and the tools leave the tab as it is. A drawing tool (or its key) started from a setup’s tab goes to Drawing first.
    • Each space keeps its own view: zoom into a corner of the drawing, look at the boards, and coming back finds the corner as you left it. The setups’ tabs share one view: their boards lie in one row that stays put, whichever is active.
    • A simple project, one board with each part on it once where drawn, looks the same in both: projects open on Drawing, and you can draw, add operations, generate and export without leaving it.

  • The grid lies on the stock top, in top view and in 3D.

    • Its fine lines are where clicks snap while drawing. A stronger line comes every ten.
    • It follows the zoom, and the status bar gives its step (“grid 5 mm”).
    • G or View > Show grid hides it, and Chipload remembers that.

  • Rulers run along the canvas’s top (X) and left (Y) edges in top view, in drawing coordinates (0 at the drawing origin, as the shapes’ Position fields), in mm or inches as the app shows lengths; the unit is in their corner.

    • Their ticks fall on the grid’s lines, and the stronger lines are numbered.
    • The cursor’s place is marked on both.
    • View > Show rulers hides them, and Chipload remembers that.

  • The axes: a red line along X and a green one along Y run through the program’s zero. Short thick strokes point the positive way, and in 3D a blue one points up Z. In top view a round handle sits at their foot: click it to select the work zero, then drag it.

  • Edit and Simulate: the canvas works in one of two modes.

    • Edit (projects open in it) is the drawing board: the drawing and the toolpath’s lines; selecting, box selection, drawing, handles, tabs and start points.
    • Simulate (the switch’s Simulate half, View > Simulate or F6) shows the simulated stock and the playback slider. The canvas is then a viewer: a drag pans (in 3D it orbits) and never selects; a click on an operation’s number opens it. Properties stay editable: change a depth and watch the cut change. A blue frame round the canvas says you are in Simulate.
    • The switch, first on the navigation bar: Edit | Simulate, the mode you are in filled.
    • Starting a drawing or editing tool, or selecting something in the Browser, goes back to Edit. F6 or Edit does too.
    • Each mode keeps its own view: top or 3D, the toolpath, the drawing and the pictures as you left them there. The first time, Simulate starts in 3D with only the simulation shown (the toolpath, drawing and pictures hidden).

  • The navigation bar floats at the bottom, each button an icon over its name:

    • the mode: Edit | Simulate (F6 switches);
    • how you look: Measure (M), Fit (F), Top (T) and 3D. When the canvas changes size (the window, or a panel opening or folding), a fitted view is fitted again, and a view you zoomed or moved keeps showing the same area;
    • what is shown: Toolpath, Drawing (in Simulate; Edit always shows the drawing), Images and Grid (G). What the toolpath is of follows the tab: Drawing shows that part alone, where it is drawn; a setup’s tab every instance on its board.
    • When the canvas is too narrow for the names, the buttons show only their icons; hover over one to see its name.

  • Drawing off (in Simulate) hides the drawing’s contours, to see the simulated stock or the toolpath alone. Back in Edit the drawing shows again.

  • The corner:

    • In 3D, the top right corner has a cube that turns with the view. Click a face (TOP, FRONT, RIGHT, BACK, LEFT) to look straight at it.
    • In top view, the corner has an X–Y sign instead.

  • The toolpath’s numbers: each operation’s first plunge has a circle and the operation’s number. Click it to open that operation.

  • The legend (bottom left) explains the colours.

  • The mouse: the wheel zooms. In Edit, in top view, on Drawing, a left drag on empty canvas selects with a box: from left to right it takes what lies wholly inside, from right to left also what it touches; Shift adds to the selection, Ctrl adds or takes out. A left drag on a drawn shape moves it. To pan, drag with the right or middle button, or hold Space and drag (the way on a touchpad; the status bar’s hint starts with it). In 3D, a left drag orbits: the model turns with the mouse. To have a left-right drag move the view round the model instead, tick View > Reverse left-right orbit in 3D. In Simulate a left drag pans (in 3D it orbits). On a touchpad, a two-finger scroll or a pinch zooms.

Properties

The Properties panel on the right shows what is open, under a title:

  • Machine: the machine and its post.
  • A setup: its name, whether it is cut (with several) and its parts.
  • Stock: the setup’s board, its thickness, size and margin, and its material.
  • Work zero: the stock’s corners and centre to click, the drawing origin, its position and Z zero.
  • Preferences: the settings folder, whether a setup sheet is written with the G-code, updating the toolpath automatically, the units (mm or inch), the colour theme, the language and whether to check for new versions.
  • A post-processor: its name, files, extension, decimals, arcs and tool changes.
  • Tools: every tool, with Add from library… and New tool; a tool alone, with how it stands to the library tool it came from, Save to library and Remove tool.
  • A library tool or machine: its values, with Save and Revert while it has changes.
  • A DXF: its file, units and size, its position and scale, Update from a DXF… and Remove.
  • A picture: its width, lower-left corner and layer. A change applies when you finish the edit, and traces the picture again. Trace settings… opens its window.
  • A part: its name, on or off, and its instances.
  • An instance: on or off, its mirror, its rotation and how far it is moved.
  • An operation: its settings, under its kind and number (“Pocket · Operation 1”): its name, shapes, tool and side, then its sections (Cut, Feeds, Holding, …) folded, each with what it holds in a few words beside its title. Click a title to open it; it stays open. A section with a problem opens by itself.

Properties shows one thing at a time: the item you clicked in the Browser (Machine, an operation, a tool, a part, a DXF or picture), or what you picked on the canvas, never both. A new project, empty or imported, starts on its setup: its stock’s thickness first, ready to type, then its material, then the rest. Picking on the canvas (a click, a box, a layer from the right-click menu or the Browser, a shape just drawn) shows the Selection:

  • what is selected, by contour number;
  • a drawn shape’s exact values, to edit, and the tools for the selection (align, arrays, construction);
  • Copy as selector, which copies the selection as JSON for a project file (also under Edit, and Ctrl+Shift+C).

The selection stays on the canvas when you open something else. An operation’s Use selection button counts it (“Use selection (3)”) and takes it as the operation’s geometry. With nothing picked, Properties says how to pick something.

The Report

The Report says what the last action did: its result first, green, amber or red, then any warnings, each opening its operation when clicked. After a generate, the time table follows: the total (hover it for what the estimate leaves out) and a line for each part, operation and file. Everything else, such as the cut outline, notes about operations, where the work zero is and the drawing’s facts, is under Details, closed at first and kept open once you open it. A drawing whose import had issues shows its facts on their own, open.

Arranging the panels

The Browser, Properties, Shapes (the shape library) and the Report are panels round the canvas, each with a tab. At the start, the Browser is on the left, Properties and Shapes are two tabs on the right, and the Report is folded along the bottom. Arrange them as suits you:

  • Tabs together: drag a panel’s tab onto another panel’s tabs. Its tab joins theirs, and a click on a tab brings it to the front.
  • Side by side or stacked: drag a tab onto a panel’s edge: left, right, above or below. It splits that panel, for example Shapes under Properties to see both at once. Dropped on the canvas’s edge, it becomes a panel along that side of the window. Dropped on the canvas’s middle, it goes beside the canvas: the canvas is always a canvas of its own.
  • Sizes: drag the line between two panels. The Properties panel works down to about 250 pixels: its labels and fields get narrower, a button or a note beside a field moves under it, and a long section summary ends in “…”. Narrower than that, it scrolls sideways.
  • Folding: the triangle at the left of a panel’s tabs folds it to its tab bar, and opens it again. The Report starts folded; a warning or an error opens it, and folded, its tab shows how many warnings it holds.
  • Closing: the × on a tab closes the panel. View > Panels lists them all with a tick for those open: tick one to open it again where it goes at the start.
  • The largest canvas: P hides every panel, and brings them back as they were. Also View > Hide the panels.
  • Back to the start: View > Panels > Reset layout.

The arrangement is kept for the next start, the same for every project, in layout.json in your settings folder. Should that file not be readable (one from a newer Chipload, say), the panels start as they do at first, the status bar says so, and the file is left as it is until you arrange the panels again.

Units and coordinates

  • Millimetres inside. Chipload works in mm throughout: the project and the toolpath. A drawing in inches is converted once, on import.
  • G-code in mm or inches. G-code units (Machine) says what the program is written in: mm (G21, the default) or inch (G20), as your controller is set. In inches every coordinate, arc centre and feed (in/min) is converted as the program is written, with 4 decimals, and the header says so. The project itself stays in mm. The checker reads the program in its own units and refuses one in other units than the machine’s, or one that changes units part way.
  • A DXF’s units: Chipload reads them from the DXF’s own setting ($INSUNITS). A DXF that doesn’t say is never guessed at: the app asks for mm or inch, and the command line needs --units mm or --units inch. The choice is kept in the project, so updating from a new version of the DXF reads it the same way.
  • Coordinates: X to the right, Y up, as in the drawing. Angles count counter-clockwise. The app shows them in degrees, and project files store them in radians (90° is 1.5707963267948966).
  • Absolute moves: the G-code uses absolute coordinates (G90), in its units, from the work zero.

The canvas shows the cursor’s position in drawing coordinates and in work coordinates. View > Measure (M) gives the distance, ΔX, ΔY and the angle between two clicks.

mm or inches on screen

Units (Edit > Preferences…) says what the app shows and takes lengths in: mm, or inch. It is a setting of this computer, not of the project: a project is always stored in mm, so it opens the same for someone who works in the other units.

With inch:

  • every length field shows inches with 4 decimals (0.2500 in) and takes inches. You can type a fraction, 1/4 or 1 1/2, or another unit: 6mm is turned into inches;
  • feeds are in in/min and the chip load in in/tooth;
  • the grid steps are inch fractions (1/32“, 1/16“, 1/8“, 1/4“, …) with a stronger line every inch, and clicks snap to them;
  • the cursor position, Measure, the playback line and the setup sheet are in inches;
  • a new project starts from inch values: stock 24“ × 12“ × 3/4“, safe Z 0.2“, 0.02“ below the stock allowed, and a 1/4“ endmill.

In mm, fields also take a length in inches: type 1/4in or 0.25".

Arithmetic: any length or number field, a value typed while drawing, and any length on the command line take + - * / and brackets: 18+0.2, 600/4, (100-6)/2, 2*1/4in. * and / go first. A unit can follow any number (1in+3mm); a number without one is in the field’s units. A length can’t be multiplied by a length, nor divide anything. The field keeps the result, not the sum.

On the command line a plain number is mm, as always, so scripts don’t change. A length can carry its unit: --height 0.5in, --diameter 1/4in, --at 1in,2in, --width 1 1/2in (quoted), --travel 30in,30in,4in; feeds --max-feed 200in/min. chipload run --sheet-units inch and chipload sheet --units inch write the setup sheet in inches. --json, get, set, the project file and the MCP tools are always in mm.

Messages follow the display units too: the report’s notes, the checker’s warnings, the problems on fields and in the Problems strip, and the setup sheet’s Watch out list (“cuts 0.0118 in below the stock”). On the command line they stay in mm, as its other output. The G-code’s units are a separate setting of the machine (above): you can show inches and send mm G-code, or the other way round.

Stock and work zero

The board is its setup’s. Under each setup in the Browser are two items:

  • Stock, with its thickness and material beside it (“18 mm · Plywood”): click it for two sections, Stock (the board) and Material (the feeds new operations take). A click on the board’s edge in the top view opens it too. A new project opens here, with the cursor in the thickness.
  • Work zero, with where it is beside it (“stock bottom left”): click it to set the zero.

The machine, shared by every setup, is under Machine.

Stock

Setting
Thicknessfirst, as every job needs it. Depths count down from the stock top, so set this to the real board.
Size: Outline + marginnew projects: the stock is the drawing’s outline (its size in brackets) plus the margin on every side, centred on it, and follows the drawing as it changes. It grows round the parts’ instances too.
Size: Fixed size, centreda size you type, for a board you want to use, centred on the drawing’s outline; it starts at the size the stock had. The drawing’s size is shown under it, to check the board against.

A project from before 28 October 2026 may have its stock’s corner or centre at the drawing’s origin. It keeps that place, and a list under the size shows it, until you choose centred on the drawing there. On the command line, stock.origin still takes bottom-left and center.

The Material section chooses your own feeds per tool for what you cut (Materials): every operation, and those added later, takes it and its values. It never changes the stock.

For many jobs the stock is mostly a picture for the simulation: you cut from whatever board is at hand. What you need to know then is how big a board must be. After Generate, the report gives the cut’s outline, the area the tool cuts with its radius included, for example Cut outline: 144.068 × 111.622 mm: the smallest board that holds the job. The setup sheet has it too.

If the drawing reaches past a fixed stock (after an update, or a move), the report says so. A stock centred on the drawing can’t be used to align the drawing (it would follow it): choose a position at the drawing’s origin first.

Work zero

Work zero is the point that becomes X0 Y0 Z0 in the G-code. It has its own row in the Browser, under its setup, which says where it is (“stock bottom left”), and its own place in the view: the foot of the red and green axes.

Only the G-code moves; the drawing, the stock and the toolpath stay where they are. So moving the zero never moves a cut, only the numbers in the program. The setup sheet and the program’s first lines say where it is.

In the view: click the round handle at the foot of the axes to select the zero, then drag it. It snaps to the stock’s corners and centre and to the drawing origin, then as the pointer snaps when drawing (points of the drawing, the grid); hold Alt to place it freely. A label at the pointer says where it goes. Esc while dragging leaves it where it was, and one drag is one step for Undo. Until the zero is selected, a drag on its handle does what it does anywhere else, so a drag meant for a shape under it never moves it. Once the zero is away from the drawing origin, the origin stays in view as grey ghost axes, so you can still see where the drawing’s own X0 Y0 is.

Its properties:

  • On the stock: a small drawing of the board with a dot at each corner and the centre. Click one: the zero stays at that corner when the stock changes size.
  • Drawing origin: where the drawing has its own 0,0.
  • Position: where the zero is, in drawing coordinates. Type a value to make it a point of its own.
  • Z zero: the stock top (depths are negative Z) or its bottom, the spoilboard (the top is at Z = thickness).

New projects start with the zero at the stock’s bottom-left corner and Z0 on its top: the corner you touch off on. A machine profile can give new projects another zero. Projects from before 29 October 2026 keep theirs, often the drawing origin.

On the command line:

chipload zero part.chipload                       # where it is
chipload zero part.chipload --stock bottom-left   # also bottom-right, top-left, top-right, centre
chipload zero part.chipload --drawing-origin
chipload zero part.chipload --at 25,-10           # a point; any length unit
chipload zero part.chipload --z bottom            # Z0 on the spoilboard

Setups

A setup is one board clamped once on the machine: its stock, its work zero, and the parts cut from it. Most projects have one and never need to think about it. A project needs more when its parts come from more than one board, or from one board clamped more than once:

  • a box with sides from 12 mm plywood and a bottom from 6 mm;
  • a big sheet whose parts lie far apart, each group with its own zero nearby.

Each setup is a program of its own, run on its own, with its own setup sheet: the operator clamps the board, sets the zero and runs that program, then the next. Chipload never puts two boards in one program, because re-measuring in the middle of a program is where crashes come from.

What a setup holds

  • Its board: size, thickness, fit, Z zero, max overcut and material, as Stock and work zero describes. A board that fits the drawing fits its own parts: the shapes its operations cut.
  • Its work zero.
  • Its parts, each with its operations and instances (Parts).

The drawing, the machine, the tools and the materials are the project’s, shared by every setup: you draw all parts in one drawing, a layer per part, and each setup’s board is placed around its own.

Running

With one setup the files are named as always (part.nc, part-1-T1.nc, …). With more, each file names its setup first: part-S1.nc, part-S2-1-T1.nc, and each setup’s sheet beside them (part-S2-sheet.html). run writes every setup that is on; --setup N only one. plan, sheet and simulate work on the active setup, or the one --setup N names.

In the window

The Browser shows Parts, each part with its operations, and Setups, each with its Stock, its Work zero and the instances cut from it (“Side #2”), with one board (Setup 1) as with several. Drag a part onto a setup to cut it there (with Ctrl, to cut it there too), an instance onto another to cut it there.

Right-click the project, or the empty space below the Browser’s rows, for the project’s menu: New part (on the active setup), Add a setup (another board: it starts like the first, empty, and becomes the active one), Face the board, Nest… and Nest again for the active setup, and Export all setups… with two or more. Click a setup for its name, whether it is cut and its parts; right-click it for what is done to that board: New part in it, Nest…, Add the back side.

Each setup has a tab over the canvas, beside Drawing (the canvas): its board with its instances where they are cut, no drawn shapes. With two or more, the setup you click is the active setup: it is shown in full colour, generated, simulated and exported, its board where it is cut. The boards lie in one row, in the setups’ order from left to right (50 mm apart; a back right beside its front, 10 mm apart with a turn-over mark between them); every other setup is a sheet in it, its board faint with the outlines of what is cut from it and its name above. The row stays put: click a sheet, a setup’s tab or its Browser row, and that setup becomes the active one where it lies on screen; only its colours change. Sheets are only drawn beside the board: the project, the G-code and the setup sheet keep each board where it is. Right-click a part for Move to setup ▸. The machine, shared by every setup, is under Machine.

The same menu opens with a right-click on empty canvas in the setup’s tab.

Right-click a setup for Export G-code of this setup…: it becomes the active setup, is planned if it isn’t shown already, and its program and sheet are written. Export G-code writes the active setup’s program, named -S1, -S2 as run names them, with its own sheet. File › Export all setups… (also in the Setups menu) writes every setup’s programs and sheets at once, as chipload run does: each setup is planned and checked first, and nothing is written unless all of them pass. A setup whose parts still have operations can’t be removed: move them or delete them first.

On the command line

chipload setup add box.chipload --name "6 mm bottom" --thickness 6   # empty: place parts on it
chipload setup part box.chipload 2 --to 2        # part 2 is cut from setup 2's board
chipload setup list box.chipload
chipload setup set box.chipload 2 --name "Bottom" --off
chipload setup activate box.chipload 1
chipload setup remove box.chipload 2 --with-parts
chipload set box.chipload setups.1.stock.thickness=6     # a board's values, by path
chipload run box.chipload -o box.nc --setup 2

In paths, a setup’s board is setups.N.stock… and its zero setups.N.work_zero, counting from 0. The older stock.… and machine.work_zero still work and mean the active setup’s.

Pieces

A setup can also be cut as a puzzle of boards, for a shape larger than your boards: see Pieces.

The back side (experimental)

A setup can be the back of another: the same board turned over, for parts cut on both faces. It shares its front’s board and work zero: change the thickness of the front, and the back follows. A board that fits fits the shapes of both sides.

chipload setup add sign.chipload --back-of 1 --turn left-right   # "Setup 1 back", with a part
chipload setup add sign.chipload --back-of 1 --pins --zero       # with its dowel holes, 8 mm pins
chipload setup set sign.chipload 2 --turn top-bottom --register corner
chipload setup set sign.chipload 2 --own-board    # a board of its own again, as it is now

--turn is how the board is turned over: left-right about its vertical centre line, or top-bottom about its horizontal one. --register is how it is put back in place: pins (dowel pins on the turn axis, the default) or corner (pushed into the same corner of a fence). A board has one back, and a back can’t be turned over again. Removing the front gives its back a board of its own.

You draw the back as seen from the front, looking through the board: the back’s shapes lie where they are on the board, and the view, the toolpath and the markers stay the front’s. The back’s program is mirrored when it is made, about the board’s centre line, so it cuts those shapes on the turned board. Climb stays climb and inside stays inside on the back face.

The back’s board is as thick as the front leaves it: a surfacing pass on the front makes the back that much thinner, and its cuts through the stock follow.

Its work zero is the front’s, on the board as it lies after turning: a stock corner is the corner of the turned board in that place, so push the board against the same stops.

Dowel holes

Pins put the turned board back exactly where it was. setup dowels draws two circles on the layer dowels, on the line the board turns about, one at each end just beyond where both sides cut (an outside profile reaches a tool’s width past its outline), and adds a holes operation Dowel holes first in the front (after a surface pass). It cuts them through the board and on into the spoilboard for the pins:

chipload setup dowels sign.chipload --setup 2 --zero        # 8 mm pins, 8 mm deep
chipload setup dowels sign.chipload --setup 2 --diameter 6 --depth 10
chipload setup dowels sign.chipload --setup 2 --remove      # the operation and its circles
chipload setup dowels sign.chipload --setup 2 --place       # place them again, where they go now

The holes stay where they are made: once the front is cut they are drilled, so nothing moves them by itself. When the board, its margin or the cuts change before then, Place again in the back’s form (--place) moves the two circles to where they would go now, clear of both sides’ cuts. The operation stays as it is, and a work zero at the first pin moves with it. When Generate is refused because the back cuts into a pin, the report offers the same: Place the dowel holes again.

A back put back by pins whose front has no dowel holes (none, or all switched off) is warned about, but never stopped: ⚠ beside it in the Browser, None yet in its form and in its front’s, a line in the report after generating, and no dowel holes (warning) in setup list.

--zero puts the front’s work zero at the first pin’s centre (the bottom one, or the left one when the board turns top to bottom): a pin lands on itself when the board is turned, so front and back share it exactly. Pins of 8 mm are the default because a 6 mm end mill bores them; with a drill of the pins’ size in the project, it drills them.

The holes may go deeper than Max depth below stock, but only they, and only inside their circles: the checker knows them from the project and refuses any other cut that deep. A dowel hole moved off the turn axis is refused, as it would miss its pin on the turned board.

A board fitted round the drawing grows round the holes, its margin beyond each pin, along the turn axis only: saw it to the size the setup sheet gives. On a board of its own size the holes are centred between the cuts and the board’s edge; when there is less room than a hole and a millimetre each side, they are made anyway, with a warning, and where to put them is yours to decide: move them along the axis by hand, or make the board larger. The pins stand in the board while its back is cut, so the checker refuses a back side’s program that comes within a pin, and a front cut made after the holes that cuts into one is noted, since the pin would no longer sit firmly. Move the holes along the axis, or place them again.

Surfacing the back goes over the pins, edge to edge. That is allowed: a surface pass on the back may pass over them as deep as it cuts, and no other cut may. Its operation gets a warning, and the back’s setup sheet says to tap the pins down at least that far below the back face before you start (Tap the pins down at least 0.5 mm below the back face).

The setup sheets

The front’s sheet says that a back follows, so the board stays where it is until the front is done. The back’s sheet starts with Turn the board over: which way to turn it, the pins to put in (or the fence corner to push it into, with the size it must have), and its picture shows the board as it lies on the machine, back face up.

A text carved on the back gets a note: it reads right from the front, where you drew it, so on the back face it reads mirrored. To have it read right there, explode it (text explode) and mirror its shapes (shape mirror).

In the window

Two-sided in the ribbon’s WIZARDS group (Wizards > Two-sided…) makes the back side of the active setup in one dialog. So do Add the back side… on a setup’s right-click, and Add the back side and Add dowel holes in a front’s form. It asks:

  • Front: the setup cut first;
  • Turn over: Left to right or Top to bottom;
  • Put back by: Dowel pins or A fence corner;
  • with pins, Pin diameter (8 mm), Into the spoilboard (8 mm), Cut with (a drill of the pins’ size, else the largest end mill that bores them, or any tool of the project or your library, a library tool copied in when you click Make) and Work zero: At the first pin (on).

A picture draws the board, the area both sides cut, the turn axis and the pins. Under it, for a board fitted round the drawing, the dialog says how much longer the board gets to hold the pins; for a board of its own size with less room than they need, it warns, and Make still makes them. Make the back side adds the back setup with its part and the dowel holes, as one undo step, and opens the back’s form. Opened on a setup that has a back, it changes that back instead: its turn, how it is put back, and its dowel holes, made again.

The back is a new setup right after its front, “… back”, with a part of its own. In the Browser it is listed under its front, last, with the turn-over icon: the same board, turned over. Its Stock and Work zero rows have a lock and say “from” the front: they are the front’s, the zero mirrored across the turn line, set on the front and followed here. On the canvas the back’s zero has a lock beside it and can’t be dragged; with the front active and its zero selected or dragged, the back’s zero shows faintly where it lies on the board, named, so you see both move together. Its form has:

  • Board: Its own board, or Back of … any setup that has no back yet;
  • Turn over: Left to right or Top to bottom;
  • Put back by: Dowel pins or A fence corner;
  • Dowel holes: with pins, Add dowel holes (it opens Two-sided…), or the operation that cuts them as a link, with Place again and Remove beside it (the operation and its two circles, one undo step).

The front’s form has a Back side row: None with Add the back side, or the back as a link, with its dowel holes.

While a two-sided board is shown, the canvas draws its turn axis: a long dashed line across the board, marked ⟲ Turn axis, on the line the board turns about, where the dowel holes go. View > Show turn axis hides it.

A back’s Stock and Work zero are its front’s: their forms say so, with a button to open the front’s, and how thick the board is after the front’s surfacing. On the command line, chipload set refuses a back’s stock or work_zero and names the front’s path to set instead. You pick and edit the back’s shapes in the same view as the front’s, where you drew them; only its G-code and its setup sheet are turned over.

Simulating a back setup shows the whole part: its front cut first, then the back from below (Simulation).

In Simulate, Turn over on the navigation bar makes the other side the active setup: the back, shown back face up as it lies, or the front again.

This is an experiment (docs/specs/two-sided.md): try it, and say what should change.

Pieces

A shape larger than your boards, such as a round table top, a big sign or a map, can be cut as a puzzle. Seams split it into pieces that each fit a board, and puzzle knobs or fingers along the seams lock the pieces together. Each piece is cut as a program of its own, from a board of its own, and the pockets, V-carving and text carry on across the seams. Once the pieces are cut, you glue them together.

Turning it on

Draw the whole thing at full size, with its operations, as for one board. The pieces are the outline that the setup’s outside profiles cut out, so the setup needs at least one.

Then open the setup (its row in the Browser) and tick Cut in pieces in its Pieces section:

  • Boards: the size of the boards you have. Each piece, with its joints and a margin, fits on one. It starts at the machine’s travel when the machine profile has one.
  • Grid: Fewest that fit lets Chipload pick the fewest columns and rows. Untick it to give the columns and rows yourself; each seam can then be moved from its even place, to keep it out of a letter or a narrow spot.
  • Joint: Puzzle knobs (round heads on narrower necks, alternating in and out along a seam) or Fingers (square teeth with rounded corners).
  • Joint size: a knob’s head across (30 mm unless changed) or a finger’s length (15 mm).
  • Spacing: from knob to knob (120 mm), or a finger and a gap (60 mm).
  • Clearance: the room left between two pieces at a seam, for glue (0.2 mm). Each piece gives half of it.

The view shows each piece’s outline dashed, with its name inside it: A1, A2 and so on, the row’s letter counted from the bottom and the column’s number from the left. Generating shows the pieces’ programs put together on the whole board, and the simulation shows the puzzle as it will be once it’s glued.

How the pieces are cut

  • Each piece’s outline is cut by the outside profiles, joints and all. The seam is one curve shared by the two pieces, so they fit each other.
  • Everything else (pockets, V-carving, text, flutes, holes) is generated once for the whole board. Each piece then cuts its share of it, a little past the seam, into what is waste on its own board. A V-carved word across a seam keeps its full depth on both sides and lines up when the pieces are glued.
  • Where a cut is split at a seam, the cutter comes down there at the plunge feed. In a pocket or a profile that is at most one step down. A V-carve or another cut that moves up and down can be deeper there, and the report says so.
  • Joints stay clear of the outline’s edge, and of the corners where two seams cross.
  • A joint must suit the cut-out tool. Its tightest curve must be at least the tool’s radius plus the clearance: for knobs, a fifth of the joint size. If it isn’t, Chipload names the smallest joint size that works.

Boards, zeros and files

Each piece is cut from a board fitted round it, with the setup’s margin (10 mm when the setup’s board has a fixed size). The setup’s work zero is used on each piece’s board: a corner of the board becomes the same corner of each piece’s board. A zero that falls off a piece’s board, such as the drawing’s origin, becomes that board’s bottom-left corner, and the report says so.

Exporting writes a program for each piece, named after it: table-A1.nc, table-A2.nc, or table-A1-1-T1.nc and so on with a file per tool. The setup sheet starts with a map of the pieces as they go together. Then, for each piece, it gives the board, the zero, the programs and a reminder to write the piece’s name on its back before you unclamp it. It ends with how to glue them.

On the command line

chipload setup pieces table.chipload --board 600x400            # knobs, the defaults
chipload setup pieces table.chipload --joint fingers --grid 3x2 # fingers, 3 × 2 pieces
chipload setup pieces table.chipload --shift-x 2=15             # move the 2nd seam 15 mm
chipload run table.chipload -o table.nc                         # a program per piece
chipload run table.chipload -o table.nc --piece A2              # only piece A2
chipload simulate table.chipload -o table.png                   # the pieces put together
chipload simulate table.chipload -o a2.png --piece A2           # one piece on its board
chipload dxf export table.chipload -o pieces.dxf --pieces       # the pieces, a layer each
chipload setup pieces table.chipload --off                      # one board again

Without --board, the boards are the size of the machine’s travel. --size, --spacing and --clearance set the joints. chipload plan still plans a setup in pieces as one board.

Not yet

  • Laying several pieces out on one board: each piece gets a board of its own.
  • Seams along a line you draw, and joints other than knobs and fingers.
  • Pieces together with a back side (two-sided work) or with instances.

Depths and oversize

Start and end depth

Every operation cuts from its start depth to its end depth, in mm down from the stock top, whatever the Z zero is.

  • Rule: 0 ≤ start depth < end depth.
  • Passes: the cut goes down in equal passes no deeper than the step down, and the last pass is exactly at the end depth. Start 2, end 6, step down 2 gives passes at 4 and 6 mm.
  • Cutting through: an end depth below the stock bottom goes into the spoilboard. That’s allowed up to Max depth below stock (the setup’s Stock), with a warning; deeper is refused, so a typo can’t reach the machine bed. For 12 mm board, an end depth of 12.3 cuts 0.3 mm into the spoilboard. An operation with Through ticked goes there on purpose: the Report gives it as a plain note (“cuts through, 0.300 mm into the spoilboard”).
  • Through: tick Through next to the end depth of a profile, pocket, follow path or holes, and the end depth is the stock thickness plus Below stock, which starts at Max depth below stock. It follows the thickness: make the board 18 mm and the cut ends at 18.3. Untick it to type an end depth again. In the project file it is the operation’s through (mm below the stock, null when not ticked); chipload set part.chipload operations.1.through=0.3 ticks it from the command line, and setting the end_depth unticks it.
  • Going down: from safe Z the tool rapids to 1 mm above the stock top, then goes down at the plunge feed. The air between the stock top and the start depth is crossed at the plunge feed too: Chipload doesn’t know whether another operation already cleared it.

What a new operation starts with

A new operation is set up from what you selected, so the usual job needs few edits:

NewStarts
Profile of outlinesoutside, through, with tabs (4 per contour, 8 mm wide, 3 mm high, at most half the stock’s thickness)
Profile of holesinside, through, no tabs
Profile of an outline with its holestwo profiles: Profile 1 (holes) inside first, then Profile 2 (outline) outside with tabs, both through, so the holes are cut while the part is still held
Pocket, V-carve, follow paththe end depth (or Through) of the last one of its kind in the project, else 1 mm
Holesthrough
  • Two profiles select their shapes one by one, also when you picked a layer or a whole DXF: shapes added to it later (by updating the DXF) aren’t cut by them, and the Report says so.
  • Feeds, step downs and stepover come from the tool’s cutting data for the setup’s material. A tool without cutting data takes them from the nearest operation above with the same tool, else from its diameter.
  • Going down: a pocket helixes and a follow path ramps in unless the tool is marked centre-cutting (see Tools).
  • When the selection doesn’t tell (nothing selected yet, or a traced picture’s or a text’s outlines and holes together), a profile is 1 mm deep with its side to choose.
  • On the command line: chipload op add part.chipload profile --dxf 1 (also --layer, --shapes, --group; kinds profile, pocket, vcarve, path, holes) makes the same operations and says what it added; --depth, --through and --tool change the defaults.

Oversize

Oversize is how much more than drawn is cut, in mm: the cut moves into the material that is kept by that much. Positive removes more; negative leaves material. In the project file the field is clearance. Other CAM programs call the opposite “stock to leave” or “allowance”: there, a positive value leaves material.

OperationPositive oversizeNegative oversize
Pocketthe pocket larger all round, islands smallerthe pocket smaller, islands larger
Profile, outsidethe part smallerthe part larger: stock left for a later cut
Profile, inside (a hole)the hole largerthe hole smaller
Profile, on the linenot allowednot allowed
  • How: the tool’s centre runs at its radius minus the oversize from the drawn line.
  • Limit: an oversize of the tool radius or more is refused. Use a profile on the line, or a smaller tool.
  • Corners: inside corners of the kept material stay as round as the tool. Outside corners get rounded with the oversize as radius, because the tool reaches past them.
  • Use:
    • a little positive oversize gives a fit, such as a lid that drops into its box;
    • a negative oversize leaves stock for a finishing pass (see also the profile’s finishing pass).

Side and direction

Side (profiles)

A profile cuts outside the line (the part is what’s inside), inside it (a hole: the waste is inside), or on the line (the tool’s centre follows it, like a slot).

When you add a profile, Chipload picks outside for outlines and inside for holes. For a selection with both, or with nothing to go by, it asks: the side is marked until you choose one. It never guesses which side is waste.

Direction

Climb or conventional, per operation:

  • Climb: the cutter’s edge enters the material at full chip thickness. This gives a better finish and less rubbing on rigid machines, and is the usual choice on CNC routers.
  • Conventional: the edge enters at zero chip thickness. It is gentler on machines with backlash.

What it means for the path. With the spindle turning clockwise (M3, Machine > Spindle):

  • climb goes clockwise round an outside profile and an island;
  • climb goes counter-clockwise round an inside profile and a pocket’s walls.

Conventional, or a counter-clockwise spindle, turns each way round. The arrows on the canvas show the direction.

Tools and tool changes

The project’s tools

Tools in the Browser lists the tools the project cuts with; click it to see them all in Properties, or click one tool. Each has an id (T1, T2, …), which is the tool number in the G-code, and:

Setting
Typeendmill, drill, V-bit or ball nose
Diametermm
Spindle speedrpm. Required: a program can’t start the spindle without it.
Centre cuttingwhether the endmill can plunge straight down. Profiles spiral down with any endmill. New pockets go down round a helix and new follow paths ramp unless it is marked centre-cutting; when it can’t plunge, sloped tabs may be no steeper than the ramp angle.
Corner radiusend mills: the radius of rounded corners, a bull nose. Not set: square corners. Less than half the diameter; a tool rounded that far is a ball nose
Angle, TipV-bits: the included angle and the flat tip’s diameter. Tapered ball noses: the taper’s included angle and the ball’s diameter at the tip; a plain ball nose has neither

Each operation names its tool: pick it in the operation’s form, or on the command line chipload op tool part.chipload --op 2 --tool 3 (the tool must be in the project). Changing the tool keeps the feeds, unless the operation has a material with values for the new tool; otherwise check them. Under the tool, each operation’s Material list (material in the project file) chooses where its feeds come from. Tools usually come from your tool library: the project keeps its own copy, so it cuts the same later.

Tool changes

Operations run in the list’s order. A run of operations next to each other with the same tool is a tool group, and between groups the tool changes. The Browser draws a line at each change.

Tool changes (Machine) says how a job with several tools is written:

  • One file per tool: part-1-T1.nc, part-2-T3.nc, … Change the tool by hand between files, set Z zero with the new tool, and keep X and Y zero. Every post can do this.
  • One file with tool changes (T2 M6): for a sender or controller that handles the change. Only posts that support it offer it (GRBL, whose sender must do the change).

The command line can override it for one run: chipload run part.chipload -o part.nc --tool-change program. The setup sheet lists the tools in the order they’re used.

A ball nose carves 3D reliefs and can follow a path; the other operations need a flat bottom and refuse it. chipload tool add "Ball 3" --type ballnose --diameter 3 adds a plain one, and --angle 7.2 --tip 1 a tapered one.

A bull nose is an end mill with round corners: a flat bottom, then a quarter round of its corner radius up to the side. Its corners are stronger than square ones, and it cuts wherever an end mill does:

  • Reliefs are cut with its true shape: it roughs with smaller steps on slopes than a square end mill, smooths them, and finishes wide reliefs fast, as its flat leaves no ridges on flat ground and its corners leave a ball nose’s soft ones on slopes.
  • Profiles, pockets and the other operations cut an end mill’s path; the corners leave a fillet where the floor meets a wall, which the simulation shows. Two warnings watch for what the corners change: a cut through the board that goes less than the corner radius below it leaves a fillet along the bottom edge that can hold the part, and a pocket’s or surface’s stepover wider than the flat bottom leaves ridges on the floor.

chipload tool add "Bull 6" --diameter 6 --corner-radius 1 adds one; in the window, tick Corner radius under the end mill’s diameter. Lists show it as Ø6 mm R1 mm.

The checker

Every program goes through an independent checker before Chipload writes it, in the app and on the command line. A program that fails isn’t exported.

What it is. The checker is separate code that shares nothing with the code that makes the toolpath. It reads the finished G-code line by line, as a controller would, and judges it against the project’s stock, safe Z and machine. Because it is independent, a bug in the toolpath code is likely to be caught by the checker, and the other way round.

What it refuses:

  • a rapid (G0) down into the stock, or sideways while the tool is below safe Z;
  • a cut deeper than the stock bottom plus Max depth below stock;
  • moves beyond the machine’s travel, when the travel is set;
  • an arc whose ends don’t lie on its circle, or an arc that is a full circle in one move;
  • a cutting move with no feed, a zero feed, or the spindle off;
  • relative (G91) coordinates, and motion before G21 or G20 and G90;
  • a program in other units than the machine’s G-code units, or one that changes units part way (an inch program is read in inches and checked in mm);
  • a tool change with the spindle running or below safe Z;
  • any G or M code it doesn’t know (see Post-processors).

What it warns about:

  • cutting moves that end outside the stock (grown by the tool radius);
  • travel limits that weren’t checked because the machine’s travel isn’t set.

What it says as a plain line: how many moves go into the spoilboard and how deep, when they stay within the allowed depth. A through cut does this on purpose, so the Report shows it as a note, not a warning. The operation’s own line says whether it was meant: a note for an operation with Through ticked, a warning for one that isn’t.

On any G-code: chipload check part.nc --job part.chipload checks a file, also one made by another program, against a project’s stock and machine. With several tools, add --tool N to check one file of a set.

What it can’t know: your clamps and fixtures, the tool’s real length, whether the stock is really there, and whether the zero is set right. The safety rules still apply.

Drawing shapes

Chipload draws the parts it cuts, with or without a DXF:

  • Circle (key C): the centre, then a point on it. Two more ways, from the small arrow on its button (below):
    • Two points: the two ends of a diameter (type a length after the first for the diameter).
    • Three points: three points on it, such as three corners it must pass through. Three points on one line make no circle, and the status bar says so.
  • Rectangle (R): two opposite corners. It keeps its centre, length and width, a rotation, and a corner radius (0: sharp corners). Two more ways to draw it, from the small arrow on its button (below):
    • Centre and corner: the centre, then a corner.
    • Aligned (three points): the two ends of one edge, at any angle, then its width (a click on either side of the edge, or type it). The rectangle is turned to the edge.
  • Polyline (L): points joined by straight or arc segments, open or closed. Click the first point again to close it; Enter or a double-click finishes it open.
    • Arcs while drawing: press A (or Arc on the status bar) and the next sides are arcs, each going on smoothly from the side before it; the click sets where the arc ends. L (or Line) goes back to lines. Shift holds the arc’s sweep to 15° steps (a quarter or half circle), and the readout shows its radius and sweep. The first side needs a direction to go on from, so it is a line, unless you start on the end of an open drawn shape: then the arc goes on from that shape. A point straight behind can’t be reached by an arc. Each new polyline starts with lines. On the command line, t in --bulges is such an arc: chipload shape add P polyline --open --points "0,0 40,0 60,20 60,40" --bulges "0 t t".
    • Rounded corners: each corner can have a Radius, an arc tangent to both sides, beside an arc side too (a line running into an arc, or two arcs). The point stays at the sharp corner, so the numbers you drew stay as they were; an arc side is cut back along its own circle to meet the round. Where the sides already meet smoothly there is no corner. Set it in the form’s points table (the Radius column), or give Radius on the status bar while drawing: every corner gets it, and a corner whose sides are too short gets the most that fits. An open polyline’s ends have no corner. On the command line: chipload shape add P polyline --points "0,0 100,0 100,60 0,60" --radii "0 5 5 0", shape set P ID --radii ….
  • Arc (A): where it starts, where it ends, then a point it passes through (or type its radius: it bows to the cursor’s side). It becomes an open polyline of one or two arc segments. Another way, from the arrow on its button:
    • Centre, start, end: the centre, where it starts (the radius; type a length for it), then where it ends: only the direction from the centre counts. The arc goes round the way you move the cursor from the start, so move the other way round for the other side. Type <90 for the end at 90° on the circle.
  • Slot (U): the centres of its two ends, then its width (a click, or type it). It is a rectangle whose corner radius is half its width; it shows as Slot, and stays one when you change its width. Another way, from the arrow on its button:
    • Arc slot: the centre of its arc, where its centre line starts (the radius; type a length for it), where it ends (the way you move round, as the arc by its centre does; <120 types the end’s angle), then its width (a click, or type it). It becomes a closed polyline of arcs with round ends, for clamp and adjustment slots; afterwards it is edited as a polyline. chipload shape add P arc-slot --center 0,0 --radius 40 --start-deg 30 --end-deg 150 --width 8.
  • Polygon (N): the centre, then a corner. Its number of sides is on the status bar while you draw (6 to start with, then the last you chose). Its form has the radius to the corners and the size across flats, either of which sets the other (a hexagon nut’s spanner size), and the angle of its first corner. chipload shape add P polygon --center 50,30 --sides 6 --across-flats 13. Two more ways, from the arrow on its button:
    • Centre and side: the centre, then the middle of a side: the size across flats is twice that distance (type it as a length: 6.5 for a 13 mm nut). Snapping perpendicular from the centre onto a line makes a polygon that touches it.
    • On an edge: the two ends of one side. The polygon lies to the side’s left, so click from left to right for a polygon above the side, right to left for one below.
  • Spline (B): click the points a smooth curve passes through, as for a polyline: Enter or a double-click finishes it open, clicking the first point closes it, Backspace drops the last point. The preview shows the curve through the cursor. It bends evenly through each point, never kinks or loops between two, and keeps its points: drag one, or change them in its form (a table of X, Y, as a polyline’s, and Closed). Snapping to a spline takes its points (as ends), the nearest point on it, and tangents and perpendiculars. It is cut as lines and arcs within 0.01 mm of the curve, since controllers have no spline move. On the command line: chipload shape add P spline --points "0,0 20,15 45,5 60,20" [--closed], and shape set P ID --points … --closed. Moving, turning, mirroring and scaling move its points, and the curve follows exactly.
