Pick the tool in the ribbon’s DRAW group (the small buttons; hover over one for its name and key) or by its key, then click on the canvas. With no tool active, Enter starts the last one again.

A button with a small triangle in its corner draws its shape more than one way. Click the triangle, or right-click the button, for the list; the way you choose stays on the button until you close Chipload. The Draw menu lists them under the shape. Pressing the key again before the first click moves to the next way (R, R, R: two corners, centre, aligned; C, A, U and N go round theirs the same way) and the status bar names it. Clicks snap to the drawing (below), else to the grid, whose step the status bar gives (“grid 1 mm”).

Snapping

Within 8 pixels of the cursor, a click or a drag lands exactly on a point of the drawing, marked with a small diamond and named on the status bar (“intersection of #3 and #4”):

  • End of a line or arc, intersection where two cross or touch, centre of an arc or circle, quadrant (an arc’s points at 0°, 90°, 180° and 270°), middle of a line or arc.
  • While drawing a polyline or measuring, from the last point: perpendicular to a line or arc, and tangent to an arc.
  • On a line or arc: the nearest point of it, when none of the others is near.

The nearest of them wins; at the same spot, they count in the order above. Hold Alt to turn snapping off.

Exact values

While a tool waits for a point, type a number: an entry opens beside the cursor. Enter uses it, Esc closes it.

TypeFor
120,40the point X 120, Y 40
@30,-1030 to the right of the last point and 10 down
3030 from the last point, towards the cursor
30<4530 from the last point at 45° (counter-clockwise from the X axis)
<45at 45° from the last point, as far as the cursor is (an arc by its centre: its end at 45°)

Lengths are in the units the app shows, unless you name them: 1/2in, 12mm. Every value can be a sum: 600/4,18+0.2, (100-6)/2, <360/7 (arithmetic). Where the decimal point is a comma, separate X and Y with a semicolon: 12,5;30. For a circle, a plain length after the centre is its radius; for an aligned rectangle, after the edge, its width. The preview shows where the value lands before you press Enter; a value that can’t be used turns red and the status bar says why.

Beside the cursor, the size being drawn: the line’s length and angle, a circle’s radius, a rectangle’s length × width. Hold Shift to keep the line from the last point at a multiple of 15° (0° and 90° for straight lines), its length on the grid.

Editing operations

The ribbon’s MODIFY group (and the Modify menu) holds tools that make new shapes from drawn ones. Shapes from a DXF, a picture or a text can’t be changed by them (explode a DXF first, Explode).

  • Offset (O): select the shapes (or click one with the tool), then move the cursor to the side you want, outside or inside a closed shape, left or right of an open one: the preview follows at the cursor’s distance on the grid (Alt: off the grid). Click, or type the distance and press Enter. The status bar has the corners, round (the exact offset, arcs round the corners) or sharp (the sides extended until they meet, as a frame), and a Count for several rings, each the distance further. The shapes stay; the new ones go on the same layer and are selected. A circle, a rectangle or slot, and a polygon inward or with sharp corners keep their kind; anything else becomes a polyline with true arcs. On the command line: chipload shape offset P 1 2 --distance 3 [--side inside] [--corners sharp] [--count 2].
  • Fillet and Chamfer: click a corner of any drawn shape (it is marked when the cursor is near it; no need to select the shape first, and a selected shape’s corners come first) to round it with an arc of the Radius on the status bar, or cut it straight across, the Distance back along each side. Shift+click does every corner. Fillet rounds any corner, beside an arc too; Chamfer only cuts corners between two straight sides. A radius too large for the sides is refused with the most that fits (two corners on one side share it); a Radius of 0 makes a rounded corner sharp again. A polyline’s corner gets that Radius (above), so it stays editable; a rectangle rounded all round keeps its kind (its corner radius); any other shape becomes a polyline and keeps its number, so operations still cut it. Chamfer leaves rounded corners as they are. chipload shape fillet P 3 --radius 2 [--corner 0], chipload shape chamfer P 3 --distance 1.
  • Fillet between two shapes: two open drawn polylines (lines and arcs too) that meet, cross or fall short are joined by a round: each is trimmed or extended to it, on the part you pick, and they join into one polyline as after a Trim when they’re on one layer (the round goes on the first one’s layer). Between two lines the round stays the corner’s Radius. A radius of 0 trims or extends them to meet in a sharp corner; Chamfer between two lines cuts across, the Distance back from where they meet. On the command line, a point near each part to keep: chipload shape fillet P 3 --with 4 --radius 5 --keep 10,0 --keep 0,10 (or shape chamfer … --distance 2). In the window, with Fillet (or Chamfer): click the first shape on the part to keep (away from its corners), then move to the second: the round and both shapes trimmed or extended are shown before you click it, on its part to keep; if no round fits, the status bar says why. Escape, or a click on empty space, forgets the first pick. One undo step.
  • Trim (X), Extend and Break work on the drawn shape under the cursor; no need to select it first. Trim shows the piece it will remove before you click, Extend the shape grown to the next one ahead (click near the end to extend), Break a mark where it will split. Each click is one step, and the changed shape is selected; a click on empty space deselects it, another ends the tool, as Esc does. On the command line:
    • chipload shape trim P ID --at X,Y removes the piece of a drawn shape at that point, between the nearest places where shown shapes cross it: an open shape cut in its middle becomes two, a closed one opens, a circle becomes an arc. What is left joins the drawn open shapes on its layer whose ends meet its own into one shape (closed when it comes round): a slot trimmed in half by a line, and the line’s ends trimmed, is one closed shape. Construction geometry never joins, and nothing joins where three ends meet.
    • chipload shape extend P ID --end start|end lengthens a straight end along itself to the next shape ahead.
    • chipload shape break P ID --at X,Y splits an open shape there, or opens a closed one.
    • The shape keeps its id for the first piece, so operations follow it.
  • Union, Subtract and Intersect combine closed drawn shapes, with true arcs where they have arcs: a union is everything any of them covers, a subtraction the first shape with the others cut out of it, an intersection what they all cover. The result takes the first shape’s number, so operations that cut it follow; the other shapes go, and operations that cut those are flagged until you choose their geometry again. A result that is one of the shapes unchanged (a hole cut where a circle already lies, a shape a union swallows whole) stays as drawn, a circle still a circle; anything else becomes a polyline. A ring of shapes united gives the outline and the hole inside as two shapes. Open shapes are refused, and so is a result with nothing left. In the window: select the shapes, then Union or Intersect acts at once (the shape drawn first keeps its number). For Subtract, select the shapes to cut out, choose Subtract, then click the shape to cut them from: the preview shows the result under the cursor. On the command line: chipload shape union P 1 2 3, chipload shape subtract P 1 4 5 (4 and 5 cut out of 1), chipload shape intersect P 1 2.
  • Mirror (I): select drawn shapes, then click two points of the mirror line (they snap; Shift keeps the line to 15° steps), or click a straight line, such as a construction centre line, to mirror about it. The preview shows the result before the last click. With Keep the original (on the status bar, on to start with) the mirrored shapes are copies; a half outline whose two ends lie on the line joins its mirror image into one closed shape that keeps its number, so drawing half a part against a centre line and mirroring it gives the whole outline, ready to cut. Unticked, the shapes flip in place and keep their numbers, and operations’ start points and tabs on them follow. On the command line: chipload shape mirror P 3 4 --line "0,0 0,100" [--copy], or --about-shape 7 for a drawn line.
  • Scale by a length: when you know what a length on the drawing should be (two holes 100 mm apart, a side 450 mm long), select what to scale, choose Scale in MODIFY and click the two ends of that length. They snap, and the first one stays where it is. The entry beside the cursor shows the length now; type the length it should be (units and arithmetic work, as in every field) and press Enter, or click a third point: its distance from the first becomes the new length, so a length can be taken from another shape. The two points don’t have to lie on the selection. Esc goes back a step. It scales whatever the box’s corners scale: drawn shapes, a text, a picture, a group or a whole DXF, which is handy for a drawing or a traced picture that came in at the wrong size. On the command line: chipload shape scale P 3 4 --reference "10,10 110,12" --length 120, and for a DXF chipload place P --reference "0,0 254,0" --length 100.
  • Stretch: a different factor along X and along Y, to make shapes wider without making them taller. Straight sides stretch exactly. A rectangle square to the axes stays a rectangle; a spline goes through its stretched points. A stretched circle or arc is part of an ellipse, which can’t be cut as one arc, so a circle, a polygon, a turned rectangle or a polyline with arcs becomes a polyline of lines and arcs that follows the stretched curve within 0.01 mm; it keeps its number, so the operations that cut it follow. Corner radii stay the radii you typed. Equal factors scale evenly, and a circle stays a circle. Only drawn shapes stretch: explode a DXF (chipload dxf explode) or turn a text’s letters into shapes first. In the window: drag the middle of a side of the box round the selected shapes, or untick Keep proportions and type a width or height (both below). The status bar names the shapes that became polylines. On the command line: chipload shape stretch P 3 4 --x 1.25 (about the middle of their box), --width 500 --about 0,0, --y 0.8.
  • Arrays add copies of drawn shapes in a grid or round a centre; the originals stay and the copies go on their layers with new numbers. A grid counts the originals in its columns and rows and spaces them centre to centre (negative goes left or down). A polar array counts the originals too: over 360° the copies spread evenly round, over a smaller angle the first and last sit at its ends; each copy turns with its place unless you say not to. This adds shapes to the drawing; a part’s instances are different: they repeat the whole part with its operations. In the window: select the shapes, choose Array or Polar array, and set the copies under Selection; for a polar array click the centre on the canvas (it snaps as drawing does). The preview shows the copies; Create adds them and ends the tool. Cancel, Esc or the tool’s button again ends it without copies. On the command line: chipload shape array P 2 3 --columns 4 --rows 2 --dx 30 --dy 20, chipload shape array-polar P 5 --count 6 --center 100,80 [--angle-deg 180] [--no-rotate].
  • Align and Distribute sit under Selection when drawn shapes are selected. Align lines up each shape’s box (its left or right side, top or bottom, or middle) with that of the shape you picked first, which stays put, however many times you align the same selection. Shapes picked all at once with a box have no first: they line up with the box round them all. With To the stock they line up with the stock’s box (then one shape is enough). Hover over Align to see which applies. Distribute, with three shapes or more, evens out the gaps between their boxes across or up and down; the outermost two stay put. Shapes move whole and keep their numbers. Pieces that join into one outline (a slot drawn as four lines and arcs) line up as one, and so does a group with its texts. On the command line, the first shape named stays put: chipload shape align P 2 3 --to left|centre|right|top| middle|bottom [--stock], chipload shape distribute P 2 3 4 --horizontal (or --vertical).
  • New shapes on a layer that an operation selects by its name are cut by that operation too, as a shape drawn there is; the status bar names those operations.

Construction geometry

Lines and circles that help you draw (a centre line to mirror about, a circle to place holes on, a guide at 45°) and are never cut.

  • Draw one: with a drawing tool active, press K (or tick Construction on the status bar): the shapes you draw next are construction geometry, until you press K again.
  • Make a shape one, or an ordinary shape again: select drawn shapes and press K, tick Construction under Selection, or right-click.
  • It shows finely dotted and faint, and the cursor snaps to it like any shape: its ends, middles, centres and where it crosses other shapes.
  • It is never cut: it never joins other shapes into a contour, an operation never picks it up from a layer, and an operation that names it by its number is refused until it cuts something else. Trim and extend can use it as the edge to cut back to; offsets, copies, pasted copies and arrays of it are construction geometry too.
  • Exported only when you ask (chipload dxf export … --construction, or the checkbox), on a layer of its own named after its layer with _construction.
  • On the command line: chipload shape add P polyline --points "0,0 100,0" --open --construction, chipload shape set P 4 --construction false.

Editing

  • Moving with the mouse: drag a drawn shape to move it; if it is selected, the whole selection moves with it. The point you grab snaps as drawing does, so a corner can be dropped exactly on another shape’s corner or a crossing; Shift keeps the move to 15° steps, Alt turns snapping off. Select several with a box first (drag on empty canvas, the canvas). Shapes from a DXF, a picture or a text move as a whole with their own handles.
  • Handles: drag a selected shape’s handles to change it. A drag snaps as drawing does; hold Alt to turn snapping off. Hold Shift to keep a point (or a centre, or a corner) to 15° steps from where it was, so straight across, up or at 45°; on a turn handle or a polygon’s corner, Shift turns the shape in 15° steps. Shapes whose ends meet make one outline (an open polyline closed by another): a click selects the whole outline for operations, and the handles and form are the clicked shape’s.
  • Several points at once: on a selected polyline or spline, Ctrl+click (⌘ on a Mac) its points to pick them; they turn solid. Drag any of them and all move together, as one undo step (Shift keeps the move to 15° steps). Ctrl+click a picked point to leave it out; Escape, or a click elsewhere, lets go of them. Rounded corners keep their radius; a move that leaves no room for one is held back, and the form says why.
  • Polyline segments: on a selected polyline, drag one of its lines (anywhere, or its round middle handle) to move that segment; the segments either side stretch to stay joined. Ctrl+drag inserts a new point there instead, and Shift+drag bends the segment into an arc. The dashed box round the polyline moves it whole.
  • Exact values: under Selection in the Properties panel, a selected shape has its exact numbers and its layer.
  • Lengths on the canvas: a selected drawn shape shows its lengths beside it: each straight side of a polyline, a rectangle’s length and width, a circle’s or polygon’s radius (a side too short on screen to hold its label shows none until you zoom in). Click one, type the new length and press Enter: a polyline side keeps its start and its end moves along it, a rectangle keeps its centre.
  • Rounded corners: a rectangle’s Corner radius rounds all four corners with true arcs (G2/G3 in the program). It can be at most half the shorter side: half of it rounds the short ends fully, into a slot. A larger radius is refused rather than shrunk, and a corner handle won’t drag the rectangle smaller than its corners allow; lower the radius first. The reset button makes the corners sharp again.
    • Each corner its own: tick Each corner under Corners in a rectangle’s or a polygon’s form for a radius per corner, numbered counter-clockwise from the first (a rectangle’s from its lower left as drawn at 0°). The shape stays a rectangle or a polygon, its size, angle and sides still editable. Unticked, one radius rounds every corner (for a polygon, Corner radius). Fillet on one corner of either sets that corner’s radius. On the command line: --radii "0 10 10 0" (--corner-radius sets every corner alike).
  • Move, turn and size: select a polyline, or several drawn shapes (Ctrl+click), and a dashed box shows round them: drag the square in the middle to move them, the round handle beyond the right side to turn them (in steps of 15°; hold Alt for 0.1°), a corner to scale them evenly, the opposite corner staying put, or the middle of a side to stretch them one way, the opposite side staying put. One circle or rectangle keeps its own handles instead. The shapes keep their ids, so the operations that cut them follow, and so do the operations’ start points, corner choices and tabs placed by hand on them (also with shape move, turn and scale, and when you change one shape through its own handles or form: a corner’s choice stays with that corner of a rectangle you move or resize).
  • Position, Size and Turn: a selected polyline’s or spline’s form, and the Selection section when several drawn shapes are selected, have Position (the lower-left corner of the box round them: type a value to move them there), Size (the box’s width and height) and Turn: ⟲ 90, ⟳ 90, or type an angle and press Enter. With Keep proportions ticked (as it starts), a new width or height scales them evenly about the middle of their box; unticked, it stretches them one way. A polyline keeps no angle of its own; turning moves its points.
  • In the Browser: under Drawing, the Circles, Rectangles, Polylines, Polygons and Splines groups list their shapes in a folder per layer. Click a row to select the shape (Ctrl+click adds or removes); right-click to rename, copy or delete it. A group with more than 20 shapes starts closed.
  • Names: give a drawn shape a Name in its form (or Rename… in the Browser); the Browser shows it, with its number beside it. Empty: it’s shown by its number. Copies start without a name.
  • Copy and paste: Ctrl+C and Ctrl+V. Draw > Paste puts the copy 10 mm right and up; Ctrl+V over the canvas pastes at the cursor.
  • Mirror: Draw > Mirror left–right and Mirror up–down flip shapes about the middle of their box; Mirror in MODIFY mirrors about any line (above).
  • Delete: Del (or right-click > Delete). Operations that cut the shape are flagged. Del also deletes a text, a picture or a DXF selected as a whole (by a click on a letter, or on its Browser row), as its Remove does; a box round a sign takes its words with the frame. A shape picked out of an imported DXF or traced picture stays: it changes only with its file.
  • Undo: Ctrl+Z.

Imported shapes can’t be edited: they change only with the DXF, unless you explode it. Lines whose ends almost meet can be joined into one shape with Draw > Join ends… (same page).

The Compass

The Compass moves, turns and copies the selection from one handle: two arrows (its X and Y axes), a ring and a centre. Select drawn shapes, a group, or a whole picture, text, 3D model or drawing, then press Q (also MODIFY > Compass in the ribbon, or Compass in the canvas’s right-click menu). It shows on the middle of the selection, and the selection’s own handles hide while it shows. Q again or Esc hides it.

  • An arrow: drag to move along that axis. The distance goes in steps of the grid, or to where a point of another shape lies along the axis.
  • The ring: drag to turn about the centre, in steps of 15° (Alt: 0.1°).
  • The centre: drag to move freely; the centre snaps to points, so a point of the selection lands exactly on a point of another shape.
  • Exact values: double-click an arrow, the ring or the centre and type the distance, the angle, or ΔX and ΔY (two numbers), then Enter.
  • Put the Compass on a point first: tap Space (press and let go). The Compass turns grey: drag its centre onto a corner, a hole’s centre or a line’s middle (the selection’s own points count too), or drag an arrow to turn its axes, which lie along a line under the cursor. Tap Space again to lock it: the next drag is measured from there. Holding Space while dragging still moves the view.
  • Right-drag an arrow, the ring or the centre, and choose when you let go: Move here (or Turn here), Copy here (the copy is then selected, so you can place the next one), Linear array… (from an arrow or the centre: count and step, the step as dragged) or Circular array… (from the ring: count and angle, round the Compass’s centre, the angle as dragged). The arrays show their copies before Create. The menu starts with the dragged distance or angle (ΔX and ΔY from the centre), selected: type an exact value over it before choosing, or press Enter to move or turn by it.
  • Right-click the centre or an arrow (without dragging) for To point or To centre point, then click: the selection moves so that the Compass’s centre comes to the clicked point, snapping as when dragging (Alt: no snapping), or to the centre of the shape you click (a circle’s or an arc’s centre, else the middle of its box). From the centre it moves in X and Y; from an arrow only along that axis, as far as the point lies along it. The outline shows where it will go while you point; Esc cancels. Unlocked, the same moves the Compass itself the same way, and the selection stays.

Every move or turn is one undo step, and the shapes keep their ids, so the operations that cut them follow. Copies and arrays are for drawn shapes; a picture, text, 3D model or whole drawing only moves and turns. The command line does the same with shape move, shape turn --about, shape copy, shape array-linear and shape array-polar.

On the command line

chipload shape add part.chipload circle --center 40,30 --radius 5 --layer HOLES   # prints its id
chipload shape add part.chipload rectangle --center 80,30 --length 40 --width 10 --angle-deg 15
chipload shape set part.chipload 9 --corner-radius 3  # round the rectangle's corners
chipload shape add part.chipload polyline --points "0,0 50,0 50,20" --bulges "0 1" --open
chipload shape set part.chipload 8 --radius 6         # change a value or the layer
chipload shape set part.chipload 8 --name "Lid"       # its name in the Browser ("" takes it away)
chipload shape copy part.chipload 8 9 --dx 25 --dy 0  # copies, with new ids
chipload shape array-linear part.chipload 8 --count 5 --step 20,10   # a line of copies, any direction
chipload shape mirror part.chipload 8 9 --axis x      # flip in place; ids kept
chipload shape move part.chipload 8 9 --dx 10 --dy -5
chipload shape turn part.chipload 8 9 --angle-deg 30  # about the middle of their box (or --about X,Y)
chipload shape scale part.chipload 8 --factor 1.5
chipload shape scale part.chipload 8 --reference "0,0 30,0" --length 45   # A–B becomes 45 long, A stays
chipload shape stretch part.chipload 8 9 --x 1.5      # wider, not taller (also --y, --width, --height, --about)
chipload shape remove part.chipload 8
chipload shape join part.chipload --gap 0.05   # join ends that almost meet

Bulges are as in DXF: tan(sweep / 4), 0 straight, 1 a counter-clockwise half circle, −1 clockwise.

Importing a DXF

File > Import DXF, SVG or STL… (Ctrl+I), or chipload import part.dxf -o part.chipload, makes a new project from a DXF. File > Open… also opens a .dxf directly. An STL file picked here starts a project with that 3D model. chipload inspect part.dxf shows what Chipload sees in a DXF without making a project.

Dropping files on the window:

  • A .chipload project opens (after asking about unsaved changes).
  • DXFs, SVGs and pictures are added to the open project: each DXF or SVG as another drawing, each picture through its image window, one after the other. With no project open, the first starts one.
  • The hint while you drag says which will happen.

What is read

  • Read: lines, arcs, circles, polylines with arcs (bulges), splines and ellipses (turned into arcs within 0.01 mm), and blocks inserted with a move, turn or scale.
  • Not supported: DWG files. Save as DXF in your CAD program.
  • Layers are kept, and you can select by them: right-click a shape on the canvas selects its whole layer. In DXF, layer names don’t care about upper or lower case.
  • Closed shapes are read as drawn: a closed polyline, a circle or a closed spline is one closed contour, even where other shapes touch it or share its edges. A pocket drawn against the part’s outline leaves both whole.
  • Joining: loose lines and arcs whose ends meet (within 0.001 mm) are joined into contours, only with others on the same layer:
    • closed, for profiles, pockets and V-carving;
    • open, for follow path; profiles and pockets skip them with a note.
  • Outer and hole: each closed contour is an outer (outline) or a hole (inside another), which sets the side of a new profile (and an outline with its holes gets two profiles).
  • The project keeps the geometry itself. The DXF isn’t needed afterwards, unless the drawing changes (Updating from a new DXF).

The import report

Chipload reads the drawing as drawn: as little repair as possible, and nothing dropped without saying so. The report under the canvas (and from inspect) lists:

IssueMeaning
duplicatea line, arc or shape drawn twice on top of itself on the same layer; one is kept. On different layers both are kept
zero_lengtha line or arc of no length; left out
nonzero_zthe drawing has Z values (3D); they are ignored, the drawing is read flat
entity_skippedan entity Chipload doesn’t read (text, dimensions, hatches, …), or one with a coordinate beyond 1 km or not a number, which only a broken file has
block_depthblocks nested too deep; the inner ones are skipped
open_contoura contour that doesn’t close: profiles and pockets skip it
branch_pointthree or more loose lines on one layer meet at a point, so the contour’s path is ambiguous
invalid_contoura closed contour that crosses or folds back on itself: it can’t be cut until fixed
units_chosenthe DXF didn’t say its units; this records your choice

Crossing contours are fine. Two shapes that cross, such as a pocket cutting through a part’s outline to make an opening, are separate shapes, each cut on its own. Chipload never merges them.

Centre lines and other open lines on a cutting layer don’t stop a profile or pocket on that layer: they’re skipped, with a note.

Units

If the DXF doesn’t say whether it’s in mm or inches, Chipload asks (command line: --units mm or --units inch). See Units and coordinates.

The new project dialog

Before the project is made, a dialog asks what it starts with. It opens for File > New project (Ctrl+N), Import and a drawing dropped on an empty window:

  • Material, from your materials. A material with a thickness of its own fills in the thickness.
  • Thickness of the board: depths are measured from its top, and cuts through it follow it.
  • Board: Fit the drawing with a margin (it follows the drawing as it changes), or a Size. Either way it is centred on the drawing.
  • Z zero: on the top of the board or on the spoilboard.
  • Machine, from your machine profiles, with its post, G-code units and tool changes shown under it.
  • Tools: tick the library tools the project uses; the search narrows a long list. Each keeps its library number; when two have the same, the second takes the next free one, and the T column shows it.

Enter creates the project, Escape cancels. Everything is remembered for the next project, which is often the same, so New project, Enter is enough.

The first time, your library’s defaults are offered: the default machine, material and tool, the material’s thickness (else the default thickness, else 10 mm), the board fitted with a 10 mm margin, and Z zero on top. Without a library you get GRBL, a safe Z of 5 mm, a travel of 800 × 800 × 100 mm (set your machine’s own under Machine, so the checker keeps every program inside it), and a centre-cutting 6 mm endmill at 18 000 rpm. New pockets go down round a helix, and new follow paths and chamfers ramp in, whatever the tool; only a V-bit plunges. Choose Plunge in an operation’s form for a straight plunge.

On the command line, chipload new and chipload import take the same choices and otherwise use your library’s defaults:

chipload import part.dxf -o part.chipload --material Plywood --thickness 18 \
    --fit 10 --z-zero top --machine "Shapeoko 4" --tools "6 mm end mill,V 60"
chipload new -o sign.chipload --size 600x300 --tools ""      # no tools yet

Importing an SVG

Logos and artwork often come as SVG, from Inkscape, Illustrator, Affinity and the like. An SVG is a drawing file just like a DXF: imported, placed, turned and scaled, updated from a newer file, removed or exploded the same way, with the same commands.

chipload inspect logo.svg                          # what Chipload sees in it
chipload import logo.svg -o logo.chipload          # a new project from it
chipload dxf add part.chipload logo.svg            # beside the project's DXFs
chipload update part.chipload --from logo.svg --dxf 2   # a new version of it

In the window: File > Import DXF, SVG or STL… (Ctrl+I) or File > Open… starts a project from it, Add DXF or SVG to project… adds it, and dropping an SVG on the window does either, as for a DXF. The file dialogs offer both kinds.

The SVG shares the DXFs’ numbers: --dxf 2, place --dxf 2 and dxf remove … 2 work on it, and dxf list shows its format. Compressed .svgz files are read too.

What is read

  • Every path, and every rectangle, circle, ellipse, line, polyline and polygon, with their transforms, styles and use copies. Each subpath is a shape: closed when it ends with Z or where it started, open otherwise.
  • The path itself, not its look: a shape follows the path’s centre line, what a laser or a router follows. Fill, stroke width and colour don’t change the geometry. A path you can’t see in the file (no fill and no stroke, or hidden) is still read, and the report says so.
  • Curves become lines and arcs within 0.01 mm, as text does, so circles and round corners are cut as arcs (G2/G3).
  • Holes: a shape inside another is a hole, by where it lies, as in a DXF; the file’s fill rule doesn’t matter.

Layers

  • Inkscape layers are the shapes’ layers: a path in the layer Cut is on layer Cut (in nested layers, the outermost one). A path outside every layer is on layer 0.
  • Without layers, shapes go on their stroke colour, written as #ff0000 (the fill colour when there is no stroke): the “red cuts, blue engraves” habit of laser work. Select a colour’s layer to cut it.

Size

  • Most files give their real size (width="210mm", inches, points): Chipload reads it exactly, and the page’s lower-left corner becomes the drawing’s origin, so the drawing sits as it does in the editor.
  • A file in plain pixels gives no real size: Chipload asks how many pixels make an inch, as it asks a DXF without units. Inkscape and browsers use 96; older Illustrator files use 72; any other number can be typed. On the command line: --px-per-inch 96. The answer is kept with the file and used again on update.

Left out, and said so

The import report (inspect, and under the canvas) names what wasn’t read:

IssueMeaning
svg_texttext isn’t read: it would need the fonts of your computer, and could cut differently on another one. Turn it into paths in the editor (Inkscape: Path > Object to Path; Illustrator: Create Outlines), or use Chipload’s own text
svg_imagepictures inside the SVG aren’t read; trace a picture on its own
svg_clipclip paths and masks are ignored: what they clip is read whole
svg_hiddenpaths that don’t show in the file were read
svg_emptysubpaths with no length were left out
svg_px_chosenthe file gave no real size; this records the pixels per inch chosen

Links to other files or to the web are never followed.

Placing the drawing

The imported drawing can be moved, turned by any angle and scaled on the stock, without changing the DXF. In the app, click the DXF in the Browser: its Position, rotation and scale are in Properties.

RowWhat it isA change
Position X Ythe lower-left corner of the drawing’s box, mmmoves the drawing so its corner is there. Bottom left puts it 10 mm in from the stock’s corner, Centre on the stock’s centre.
Rotationdegrees counter-clockwise from the DXF as drawn, any angleturns it about its centre; ⟲ 90 and ⟳ 90 turn it a quarter
Scalethe factor from the DXF as drawnscales it about its centre
Sizethe box’s width and height

Each row has a reset button: the position back to where the DXF has it, rotation 0, scale 1. A change applies when you finish the edit (Enter, Tab or releasing a drag), as one undo step.

On the canvas: click the drawing, and its box shows handles (in top view), as a picture’s does:

  • the square in the middle moves it, and so does dragging one of its lines;
  • the square beyond its side turns it about the box’s centre, in steps of 15° (Alt: 1°); the box stays turned with it;
  • a corner square scales it, the opposite corner staying where it is.

Each drag is one undo step, and start points and tabs move with it. Dropped on the Shapes tab, it is saved to the shape library instead of moved. A double-click picks one contour alone, for an operation that cuts only that.

On the command line, chipload place (for a picture, --image ID with --at, --rotate and --width):

Command line
Put the box’s lower-left corner at a point--at 10,20
Move by--by 10,-5
Put at an offset from where the DXF has it--to 10,-5
Align with the stock--align bottom-left --margin 10, --align center
Turn about its centre, on top of any turn--rotate 30 (any angle; negative: clockwise)
Scale about its centre--scale 2, --width 100, --height 50
Scale so a length on it becomes another, the first point staying--reference "0,0 254,0" --length 100
Back to as drawn--reset
  • Order: a turn and a scale come before an alignment, so --rotate 90 --align bottom-left turns the part and then puts it in the corner.
  • Saved as values: the placement is kept in the project, and updating from a new DXF puts the new drawing in the same place.
  • What follows: operations follow the drawing, and their start points move with it.
  • Arcs: scaling and turning keep arcs exact. Turns by 90°, 180° and 270° are exact to the bit; other angles are as exact as the computer’s arithmetic.
  • Where it is now: chipload place part.chipload with no option prints the placement and size.

Shapes drawn in Chipload aren’t part of the imported drawing and stay where they are. With several DXFs, each is placed on its own: --dxf ID says which.

To have the G-code’s zero somewhere else, without moving anything, set the work zero instead.

Updating from a new DXF

When the drawing changes, File > Update drawing from DXF…, or chipload update part.chipload --from part.dxf, takes in the new version and keeps the operations.

  • Same shape, same id: shapes are matched by their DXF handle, the DXF’s own id for each entity, which CAD programs keep when you edit. An operation that selected a shape keeps it.
  • Placed as before: the new drawing gets the same move, turn and scale.
  • Drawn shapes stay: shapes drawn in Chipload are kept, with their ids.
  • Several DXFs: --dxf ID says which one the new file replaces; the others stay as they are (Several drawings).
  • The report lists what’s unchanged, changed, added and removed, and what that means for each operation:
    • geometry changed; check its toolpath;
    • layer X now uses the updated drawing;
    • shapes … no longer exist; choose its geometry again: the operation can’t run until you give it geometry again. The command line exits with 1 in that case.
  • Units: taken from the last import. --units overrides them, if the new DXF is different.
  • Stock: the stock isn’t changed. If the drawing now reaches past it, the report says so.

An empty project (New project on the start screen or under File > New…, chipload new) takes its first DXF the same way: Add DXF, or File > Add DXF or SVG to project….

A DXF saved by a program that gives every entity a new handle on each save makes every shape look new. Operations that select by layer survive that; ones that select single shapes need their geometry chosen again.

Several drawings

A project can hold several DXFs (and SVGs, which work the same way), for example two parts cut from one sheet, besides its images and the shapes you draw. Each DXF has its own place, turn and scale, and is updated from its own file.

chipload dxf add part.chipload lid.dxf --name lid     # a second DXF, where its file has it
chipload dxf list part.chipload                       # id, name, file, placement, size
chipload place part.chipload --dxf 2 --by 250,0       # move, turn or scale that one
chipload update part.chipload --from lid.dxf --dxf 2  # a new version of that one
chipload dxf remove part.chipload 2                   # it and its shapes

In the app: File > Add DXF or SVG to project… (or Add DXF on the ribbon), or drop the file on the window. Any open project takes one, also a project started empty or drawn in Chipload.

In the Browser

The Drawing folder of the Browser lists what the project’s shapes come from:

  • Each DXF, with its layers inside.
  • Each image.
  • The shapes drawn in Chipload, by kind: rectangles, circles, polylines.

  • A click selects the whole group, or one layer of one DXF; Ctrl+click adds. An operation added from it cuts only that (its selector names the DXF or image). A DXF or picture also shows its properties: position and scale, or size and place.
  • Right-click has the rest:
    • For a DXF: Place… (its properties: Placing the drawing), Update from a DXF… or from a path…, and Remove.
    • For an image: Trace settings… (also a double-click) and Remove.
  • The eye hides a group or a layer on the canvas: it isn’t drawn and can’t be clicked. Hidden shapes are still cut. An operation that cuts some says so on its row (“3 hidden · T1”), and its toolpath still shows. To stop cutting them, untick the operation.
  • Saved: what is hidden is saved in the project (drawing.hidden). Changing it is a step you can undo.

File > Update drawing from DXF… works while the project has one DXF. With several, update each from its row’s right-click menu.

  • Where it goes: a new DXF lands where its file has it, often on top of the first. Place it afterwards.
  • Which one: with several DXFs, place and update need --dxf ID. With one, they work as before.
  • Name: the file’s name without its extension, unless you give --name. A second copy of the same file is named “part 2”.
  • Removing a DXF removes its shapes. The report names the operations that used them; they can’t run until you give them geometry again.

What an operation selects

An operation selects a DXF or image as a whole, a layer within one, or shapes:

"select": {"dxf": 2}                       // all of part B, whatever its layers
"select": {"dxf": 2, "layer": "HOLES"}     // part B's holes
"select": {"image": 1}                     // a traced picture
"select": {"image": 1, "exclude": [863]}   // the picture but one of its shapes
"select": {"shapes": [12, 14]}             // these shapes

A DXF or an image selected as a whole, or one of its layers, follows the DXF when it is updated and the picture when it is traced again. In the app:

  • A click on a DXF or image in the Browser, or on one of its contours on the canvas, picks the whole DXF or image. A double-click on a DXF’s contour picks that contour alone.
  • A click on a layer in the Browser, or right-click Select layer on the canvas, picks that layer of that DXF only.
  • Leaving some out: with a whole DXF or picture selected, Ctrl+click a contour on the canvas to leave it out, and again to put it back. The selection then reads “image 1, 1 left out”, and the operation’s selector gets exclude.

When a picture is traced again (a new size, place or setting), its shapes get new numbers. Left-out shapes are found again by where they sit in the picture, and the report says so. One that can’t be found (the tracing changed too much) gives a problem, and nothing is generated until you pick the shapes to leave out again. Ctrl+Z undoes the retrace.

Adding a DXF never changes what an existing operation cuts. An operation that selected a layer by its name alone, made while the project had one DXF, keeps cutting that DXF when the new one has the same layer. The report says so: Operations 1, 2 keep cutting part only.

A selector with a layer and no DXF (written by hand, or by Chipload before 0.16) means that layer in every DXF. When a layer is in more than one, the report says so: layer “HOLES” is in lid and part; it cuts all of them.

The setup sheet lists every DXF and image with where it was put, when there is more than one.

Exploding a DXF, picture or text, joining ends

A DXF’s shapes are read-only: they change only when the DXF is updated. A traced picture’s shapes and a text’s letters are read-only too: they change only when the picture is traced again or the text is edited. To rework them in Chipload, explode the DXF, picture or text: its shapes become drawn shapes, with handles, values you can type, copy, mirror and delete, like the ones you draw.

Explode a DXF

Right-click the DXF in the Browser, Explode into shapes…, or Explode… in its properties. With the whole DXF selected on the canvas, right-click there for the same.

  • The project forgets the DXF file. It leaves the Browser, and Update from DXF is no longer possible for these shapes. Ctrl+Z undoes it until the project is saved; after that, the only way back is adding the DXF again.
  • Circles stay circles; everything else becomes a polyline of straight and arc segments. An arc of more than half a circle becomes two segments.
  • Operations keep cutting the same shapes, with the same G-code. The shapes keep their ids and layers. An operation that selected a layer of that DXF now selects its shapes by number, so a layer of the same name elsewhere doesn’t join in; the report lists these.
  • Shapes drawn twice, on top of another on the same layer, go: the import report already listed them as Removed duplicate, and they are never cut. Left in, they would stay behind when you move what they repeat. The report names them.
  • Circles that the placement turned start at their right-hand point afterwards, as every drawn circle does, so the cut may start elsewhere on them. The report names them.

Tick Join ends closer than to join the exploded lines straight away (below).

Explode a picture or a text

Right-click the picture or text in the Browser, Explode into shapes…; or select it on the canvas and right-click there.

  • A picture goes from the project with its trace settings: it is no longer shown under its shapes and can’t be traced again. The project file gets smaller by the picture.
  • A text’s words can’t be edited again; its letters stay where they are, each outline and each hole (the inside of an O) a shape of its own. A text in a group is replaced there by its letters.
  • Operations keep cutting the same shapes, as for a DXF: an operation that cut the whole picture or text now selects its shapes by number. The G-code stays the same, apart from a V-carve’s depth now and then by 0.001 mm (arcs come back a hair different).
  • No join: traced outlines and letters are closed already. Join ends (below) is there if a centre-line trace needs it.
  • Ctrl+Z undoes it until the project is saved.

Join ends

A drawing made of separate lines and arcs whose ends almost meet can’t be pocketed: each piece is an open contour. Draw > Join ends… joins open drawn polylines whose ends lie closer than the gap into one, and closes a chain that comes back to its start.

  • The selected shapes, or every drawn shape when none is selected.
  • Gap: 0.01 mm unless you type another, at most 1 mm. Both ends move to the point between them; a shape moves by at most half the gap.
  • Same layer only. Pieces on different layers stay apart.
  • Only clear pairs. Where three or more ends meet within the gap, nothing joins there: which way the outline goes would be a guess. The report gives the point.
  • The joined shape keeps the lowest id of its pieces; operations that selected any of them select it.
  • The report says how many shapes became how many, the widest gap closed, and the ends still open with the distance to the nearest other end: the gap that would close them.

On the command line

chipload dxf explode part.chipload                  # the project's only DXF
chipload dxf explode part.chipload 2 --join 0.05mm  # DXF 2, then join ends closer than 0.05 mm
chipload image explode part.chipload                # the project's only picture (or give its id)
chipload text explode part.chipload 2               # text 2
chipload shape join part.chipload                   # every drawn shape, gap 0.01 mm
chipload shape join part.chipload 12 13 14 --gap 0.1

With --json, shapes lists the shapes that came of it and removed those joined into others.

Exporting the drawing

Once a part is drawn or edited in Chipload, the drawing can go back out as a DXF: to a laser cutter’s software, to a shop for a quote, or into a CAD program.

chipload dxf export part.chipload -o part.dxf
chipload dxf export part.chipload -o part.dxf --inch --layer Cut --layer Holes

In the window: File > Export drawing as DXF…, then the choices and the file name.

What is written

  • Every shape the canvas shows, where it shows it: shapes you drew, a DXF’s or an SVG’s shapes as placed, traced pictures, and texts as their letters’ outlines. Hidden layers and groups are left out unless you ask for them (--hidden, or the checkbox).
  • On their layers. A layer name with a character a DXF can’t hold (`< > / \ “ : ; ?
    • | =) is written with _` in its place, and the report says so.
  • Circles as circles, everything else as polylines whose arcs stay true arcs (bulges), so round corners and holes are cut as arcs by whatever reads the file. A spline goes out as the lines and arcs it is cut with, within 0.01 mm of its curve: the file’s classic DXF layout, which every program reads, has no spline entity.
  • Only some of it: --layer NAME (repeatable) or --shape ID … on the command line; Only the selected shapes in the window.
  • Construction geometry only when you ask (--construction, or the checkbox), each on a layer named after its own with _construction.
  • Not written: toolpaths (that’s the G-code), the stock, the parts’ instances, operations.

Units

Millimetres, and the file says so ($INSUNITS), so programs that read it (Chipload too) don’t ask. In inches (--inch) writes inches and says that instead.

The file

The classic DXF layout every CAD, laser and CAM program reads (AutoCAD R12 style), as plain text. The same project always gives exactly the same file. Before writing, Chipload reads the file back with its own importer: if any shape doesn’t come back the same, nothing is written and the error names the shape.

Images

A picture, such as a logo, a scanned sketch or lettering, can be traced into shapes and then V-carved. Chipload turns it black and white, traces the edges between black and white, and puts the outlines on a layer of their own.

A picture of strokes, such as handwriting, a signature or line art, can instead be traced into the strokes’ centre lines and cut with a follow path.

Black is what gets carved: for a V-carve, black is the inside of the shapes. A light logo on a dark background needs invert.

PNG, JPEG and BMP pictures work, up to 20 MB and 20 megapixels. The picture is stored in the project, so its settings can be changed later.

Size and place

A new picture comes in half the stock’s width, with its lower-left corner at X0 Y0. Click it in the Browser (it is selected right after importing) for its properties:

  • Position: the lower-left corner of its box, mm;
  • Rotation: degrees counter-clockwise, any angle, about its centre, with ⟲ 90 and ⟳ 90. The picture is traced as it is and its shapes are then turned, so the trace doesn’t change; on the canvas the picture is drawn turned too;
  • Width in mm; its height follows from the picture. Chipload doesn’t use the picture’s DPI, which is often missing or wrong.

On the canvas: with the picture selected, its box shows handles (in top view):

  • the square in the middle moves it, and so does dragging one of its lines;
  • the square beyond its right side turns it about its centre, in steps of 15°;
  • a corner square makes it larger or smaller, keeping its proportions; the opposite corner stays where it is. Its width is rounded to 0.1 mm.

The box follows the cursor while you drag, and the picture is traced again when you let go, as one undo step. Hold Alt for no steps (any angle, any size) and no snapping.

On the command line, give --width, --at (the picture’s corner before it is turned) and --rotate.

Black and white

SettingDefault
Thresholdautogrey values (0 black, 255 white) below it are black, 1 to 254. Auto picks the value that best separates the picture’s dark and light parts (Otsu’s method) and says which it picked.
Invertoffcarve the light parts instead
Blur1 pxsmooths ragged edges and scan or JPEG noise before the threshold, 0 to 10 px
Smallest piece0.5 mm²black or white specks smaller than this are dropped, 0 to 100 mm²
Smoothing0.05 mmhow far a traced edge may be straightened away from the picture’s edge, 0.01 to 1 mm
Traceoutlinesoutlines round the black parts, for a V-carve; or centre lines of black strokes, for a follow path
Arcsonfit arcs as well as lines (below)

The traced shapes

  • Smooth edges: the edge is traced where the blurred grey crosses the threshold, between pixel centres, so there are no pixel steps.
  • Always valid: the outlines are always closed and never cross. A white part inside a black one is a hole, as in a pocket.
  • Picture border: black that reaches the picture’s edge is closed along it, with a note: usually a background that wants invert.
  • Too many shapes: a picture that traces into more than 5000 shapes (a photo, noise) is refused. Blur it more, raise the smallest piece or change the threshold.
  • Arcs: with arcs on, the edges are fitted with arcs as well as lines, still within the smoothing: a traced logo becomes a few dozen pieces instead of hundreds of short lines. A profile, pocket or follow path of them is then cut with G2/G3 arcs: a smaller program, and smoother motion, since a controller slows down at every short line. A V-carve gains little: its moves are lines anyway. An outline whose arcs would come too near another one is kept as lines, and the report says so.
  • Not editable: the shapes can’t be edited in Chipload.

Changing a setting traces the picture again and replaces its shapes, which get new ids. So an operation selects the image as a whole ("select": {"image": 1}), never its shapes one by one: Chipload refuses an operation that selects traced shapes by id.

The drawing’s placement moves only the DXF’s shapes, never a traced picture; a picture has its own X, Y and width.

Centre lines

With Trace: centre lines, each black stroke becomes a path along its middle: open, or closed for a ring like an O. A follow path cuts each stroke in one pass at the depth you choose, as wide as the bit, whatever the pen did. Select the image and Follow (ribbon, OPERATION).

  • Junctions: where strokes meet (a T, a Y), their paths end at the same point.
  • Ends: a path stops half a stroke’s width short of a round stroke end, at the middle of its curve, so a bit as wide as the stroke reaches the end. Small side branches from a rough edge or a square corner are tidied away.
  • Stroke width: the report gives the strokes’ width (“Strokes 2.9 to 3.2 mm wide”), to pick a bit by.
  • Dots (the point of an i, a full stop) are too small for a centre line. They are left out and counted in the report.
  • For strokes only: a filled area’s centre line is a branching skeleton. That is correct, but rarely what you want; a wide range in the stroke widths shows it.
  • Closed lines are outlines too: an outline drawn by hand and scanned traces as one closed centre line, and a profile or pocket can cut round it like any outline. The report says how many lines are closed.
  • Open lines only for a follow path: a profile, pocket or V-carve of a picture whose centre lines are all open is refused, with a hint to use a follow path or trace the image as outlines. With both kinds, the open lines are skipped, with a note.

In the app

File > Import image… picks a picture and opens its window. A project is made first if none is open. Without a file dialog (see Install), File > Import image from path… takes the picture’s path instead.

  • Left: the picture as it will be carved, updated as you change the settings: light oak where the wood stays, dark brown for the black parts, which are traced and carved. The traced lines (outlines or centre lines) are drawn over it in thin blue; untick Show the traced lines to see the picture alone, to judge the threshold by its detail. Scroll over it to zoom in at the pointer, up to 32 times, drag to move about, and double-click to see all of it again.
  • Right: the conversion settings, and what was traced (with the threshold auto picked). The size, place and layer are not here: they are the picture’s properties.
  • Import: puts the shapes into the project and selects the image, so V-carve carves them (or, for centre lines, Follow cuts them). It is one undo step.

Afterwards:

  • Move or resize it: click it in the Browser and change its position, rotation, width or layer in Properties.
  • Trace it differently: double-click it, or right-click it > Trace settings…: the same window opens (its button says Apply).
  • Remove it: right-click > Remove removes the picture and its shapes; so does Del with the picture selected.
  • Edit its shapes by hand: right-click > Explode into shapes… makes them drawn shapes and takes the picture out of the project (Exploding).
  • See it: Images on the canvas’s navigation bar (or View > Show images) shows the pictures faintly on the stock top, over the simulated stock and under the drawing, in top view and 3D, for comparing the traced shapes, and the cut, with the picture.
  • Leaving shapes out: with the picture selected, Ctrl+click a shape to leave it out of the operation (What an operation selects).
  • Operations: clicking traced shapes on the canvas and adding an operation selects the whole image, not the shapes.

On the command line

chipload image add part.chipload logo.png --width 120 --at 10,10 --threshold auto
chipload image add part.chipload signature.png --width 80 --trace centre-line
chipload image set part.chipload 1 --threshold 140 --blur 2 --invert true --arcs false
chipload image set part.chipload 1 --rotate 30         # turned 30° about its centre
chipload place part.chipload --image 1 --at 10,10      # as for a DXF: --at, --rotate, --width
chipload image list part.chipload
chipload image preview part.chipload 1 -o bw.png
chipload image remove part.chipload 1
  • image add: the layer is the file’s name unless --layer says otherwise. It reports how many shapes were traced, the threshold, the specks dropped, and whether black touches the border; for centre lines also the stroke widths and the dots left out.
  • image preview: writes the black-and-white picture exactly as it is traced.
  • chipload set: also reaches the settings (drawing.images.0.threshold=140). The image is then traced again, as with image set, also for --set on a single run.

In the file

drawing.images in the project file: id, file, sha256, data (the picture, base64), layer, x, y, width, threshold (a number or "auto"), invert, blur, min_area, smoothing, trace ("outline" or "centre_line"), arcs. The traced shapes are path shapes with "image": id. Images in projects from Chipload 0.15 open as outlines without arcs, exactly as they were traced.

Text

Write a name, a label or a sign straight into the project: Chipload turns the letters into shapes that you V-carve, pocket or cut out with a profile. (Design: docs/specs/text.md.)

For a sign, Draw > Frame… then draws a frame round the text, with border lines: Signs and frames.

Writing a text

  1. Text among the ribbon’s small DRAW buttons (or Draw > Text), then click on the canvas where the text should start. That point is the lower-left corner of the text’s box; set Anchor to centre afterwards to keep it centred on a point instead.
  2. A text saying “Text” appears there, selected, with its properties open and the text field ready: type what it should say. Enter starts a new line. The letters are made again when you click elsewhere.
  3. With the text selected, V-carve (or Pocket, Profile) in the ribbon cuts it. A new V-carve takes a V-bit from the project if it has one.

Esc stops placing a text.

Properties

Click the text in the Browser (under Drawing, with an A) or one of its letters on the canvas:

  • Text: what it says, on one or more lines;
  • Font: one of the built-in fonts, or one installed on your computer (below);
  • Height: the height of a capital letter, mm; what a ruler measures on an H;
  • Anchor: what Position is: the lower-left corner of the text’s box, or its centre. A centred text stays centred when you change its words, font or size, which is handy for a name in the middle of a sign. Changing the anchor doesn’t move the text;
  • Position: the anchor’s place on the stock, mm;
  • Rotation: degrees counter-clockwise, any angle, about the box’s centre, with ⟲ 90 and ⟳ 90;
  • Align: how lines line up: left, centre or right;
  • Letter spacing: extra space between letters, mm; 0 keeps the font’s own spacing, kerning included (in a serif font, “AV” sits closer than “A V”);
  • Line spacing: from one line’s base to the next, in capital heights (1.6 by default);
  • Layer: the layer its letters go on (text by default);
  • Size: the box’s width and height, for reference.

Each change applies when you finish the edit, as one undo step: Ctrl+Z brings the earlier text back. An operation that selects the text follows the change.

Del removes the selected text with its letters (as Remove on its Browser row does). To edit the letters by hand, right-click > Explode into shapes…: they become drawn shapes, and the words can no longer be edited (Exploding).

On the canvas: with the text selected, its box shows handles (in top view):

  • the square in the middle moves it, and so does dragging one of its lines;
  • the square beyond its right side turns it about its centre, in steps of 15°;
  • a corner square makes it larger or smaller, keeping its proportions; the opposite corner stays where it is. Its height is rounded to 0.1 mm.

The box follows the cursor while you drag, and the text is written again when you let go, as one undo step. Hold Alt for no steps (any angle, any size) and no snapping.

On an arc

Tick On an arc in the text’s properties and its line follows a circle, as on a round plaque, a coaster or a badge. The circle’s centre is the text’s Position (anchor and rotation don’t apply then), and three more fields set the rest:

  • Radius: of the circle through the middle of the letters, half a capital height above their base. A top text and a bottom text with the same radius lie in one ring;
  • Side: Top stands the letters on the outside, reading clockwise, as a name along the top; Bottom keeps them upright at the bottom, reading counter-clockwise, as a date along the bottom;
  • Angle: how far the text is turned round the circle from straight up (top) or straight down (bottom). With Align centre (which a text gets when it goes on an arc) its middle sits there; with left or right, its start or end.

Each letter is moved and turned whole onto the circle, so it keeps the font’s exact shape; on a top text their tops spread a little and their feet close in, as on any lettered ring. More lines stack the way they’re read: nearer the centre on the top, further out on the bottom. A line longer than its circle, or one that would reach the centre, is refused with the reason. On the canvas the box is the square round the ring: its middle moves the circle, its turn handle moves the text round it, and a corner scales the radius and the letters together.

Fonts

Chipload has 18 fonts built in, so a project writes the same letters on every computer. The Font list groups them and shows each name in its own face; chipload text fonts lists them with their style.

  • Plain: FreeSans, FreeSerif and FreeMono, each also bold (GNU FreeFont), and Roboto Slab and Roboto Slab Bold.
  • Script: Satisfy, Yellowtail and Rochester, handwriting with joined letters.
  • Display: Luckiest Guy, Ultra, Chewy, Smokum, Cherry Cream Soda and Permanent Marker, bold and decorative, for headings and signs.
  • Single-line: Relief SingleLine, made for CNC engraving (below).

They all have the Latin letters with accents (Å, Ä, Ö, É, Ñ, Ø, …), digits and the common symbols (€, °, ±; ⌀ only in some of the FreeFont faces). A character a font lacks is left out, and the report names it.

Joined letters: where letters overlap, as in a script where one letter runs into the next, or where a letter is drawn as overlapping strokes, Chipload joins them into one outline, so a pocket or V-carve sees clean shapes. Holes stay holes where nothing covers them. Letters that don’t overlap anything keep the font’s exact outline.

Choosing: serif fonts V-carve beautifully: the V-bit cuts the thin strokes shallow and the thick ones deep. The scripts V-carve well too. For a pocket or a profile, a font with even, sturdy strokes works best (FreeSans Bold, Roboto Slab, Ultra, Luckiest Guy, Chewy); Smokum’s pointed serifs need a pocketing tool of 2 mm or more.

Licences: GNU FreeFont is under the GPL with the font exception, Relief SingleLine under the SIL Open Font License 1.1, the others under the Apache License 2.0. None puts any condition on what you make: the letters you cut and their G-code are yours.

Single-line text

The other fonts give each letter as the outline round its strokes, which suits a V-carve or a pocket. A single-line font gives each stroke once, down its middle, as open lines and arcs: one pass of a small bit cuts the letter. That is what labels, serial and part numbers, scales, small lettering (under about 8 mm, where V-carved grooves run together) and quick nameplates want.

  • Font: Relief SingleLine, in the Single-line group of the font list. It has the Latin letters with accents of all the app’s languages, digits and the common symbols, but no Cyrillic: write Russian in an outline font.
  • Cutting it: a Follow path on the text, with a V-bit (the line as wide as the bit is deep) or a small end mill, at the depth you choose, 0.3 to 1 mm for most engraving. Profile, Pocket and V-carve need closed outlines and refuse these letters, saying why.
  • Everything else as for any text: height (the capital height), alignment, lines, on an arc, moved, turned and edited. Strokes that cross are each cut; they aren’t joined.
  • On the command line: chipload text add P "PART 42-A" --font "Relief SingleLine" --height 6 --at 10,10, then chipload op add P path --layer text --depth 0.5.

Your computer’s fonts

The Font list also has On this computer: every TrueType or OpenType font installed there, by name (“Arial Bold”). Hover over a name to see a sample. The first time the list opens, Chipload looks for them (Looking for fonts…).

When the list opens you can type at once: the search field at its top keeps only the fonts whose name has what you typed, built in and on this computer. Recently used, at the top, has the last five fonts you picked, each in its own face.

A font you don’t want to install can go in a folder named fonts in your settings folder; Chipload finds it there too.

Only the font’s name goes into the project, never the font file: fonts are licensed to the computer they are installed on. The letters are in the project as shapes, so the project cuts the same on any computer (below).

In the project file

A text is stored with what it says and how, and its letters are stored as shapes too. So the font is needed only to change the text; a project cuts the same whatever the computer has installed. Chipload 0.19 and older can’t open a project that has a text.

On a computer without the text’s font, the text keeps its letters and cuts as they are. Its properties say so, and its words, height, alignment and spacing are greyed out; the Browser marks it with ⚠. You can still move it, turn it and put it on another layer. To change its words or size, choose another font: its letters are then made again in that one.

Leaving letters out

As with a picture: with the whole text selected, Ctrl+click a letter (or one part of it, such as the hole of an O) to leave it out of the operation, and again to put it back. A left-out letter is found again when the text changes size, place or font.

Command line

chipload text add sign.chipload "MAKER\nSPACE" --height 20 --at 40,30 --font "FreeSerif Bold" --align center
chipload text set sign.chipload 1 --rotate 15 --spacing 1     # change it; its letters are made again
chipload text set sign.chipload 1 --anchor center --at 85,55  # centred on X85 Y55, and stays centred
chipload text add plaque.chipload "MAKER SPACE" --height 12 --at 0,0 --arc 60   # along the top of a circle round 0,0
chipload text add plaque.chipload "EST. 2026" --height 8 --at 0,0 --arc 60 --side bottom
chipload text set plaque.chipload 1 --angle 100               # turned round the circle; --straight: off the arc
chipload place sign.chipload --text 1 --at 10,10 --height 25  # its corner, turn and height
chipload text list sign.chipload
chipload text remove sign.chipload 1
chipload text fonts                                           # built in, then the computer's

\n in the text starts a new line. An operation selects the text with "select": {"text": 1}, and any value can be changed with chipload set (drawing.texts.0.text="SIGN"); the letters are made again.

3D models

A 3D model in an STL file, or a picture read as a depth map, can be carved into the board from the top as a relief: a sign with raised, rounded letters, a rosette, a coin, a landscape. The model lies on the board like a picture, seen from above, and is carved in three axes; nothing under an overhang is reached. (Design: docs/specs/stl-relief.md.)

3DBenchy by Creative Tools (CC0), 150 mm wide and 10 mm deep, with a rim filled out by Fuller at 90 %, a roughing pass with a 6 mm end mill, a smoothing pass and a 2 mm ball nose finish.

A relief in one step

Relief… (the ribbon’s WIZARDS group, or Wizards > Relief…) makes a whole relief, one question at a time, each needing only the ones before it:

  1. Model: an STL file, or a model already in the project.
  2. Turn and cut: which side faces up, quarter turns, and how much to keep from the top (as in Turn and cut).
  3. Size and place: width and depth, centred on the board or at a point; the start depth, how far below the board’s surface the model’s top lies (0 by default; a millimetre or so on a board that isn’t planed, so its uneven top doesn’t reach the relief); the board’s thickness, which the start depth and the depth must stay within.
  4. Round it: a rim round the model’s outline (the default; the Fuller slider fills it out towards a rubber band round the model) or round a box, rounded corners if you like: a flat at the relief’s depth, then a ramp up to the surface; or the relief cut out through the board with tabs.
  5. Tools: the ball nose that finishes it; an end mill to rough it first, ticked when the relief is deeper than the ball nose is wide, and Smooth the steps after it (a smoothing pass, ticked); for a cut-out, its end mill, by default the roughing one, so one tool does both.
  6. Check and make: what will be made, in cutting order, with the tools and tool changes.

From step 3 on, a picture of the board beside the questions shows the relief where it will lie, and the rim or cut-out round it. Make adds it all as one undo step, in a part of its own: the model, the relief with its rim, roughing and smoothing passes, the cut-out. A board fitted to the drawing grows to hold the rim. Afterwards they are ordinary shapes and operations.

From the command line, the same answers in the same order:

chipload relief add sign.chipload rose.stl --keep 50% --width 120 --depth 6 \
    --around box --margin 10 --ramp 45 --tool 2 --rough-tool 1 --smooth

--around is rim (the default), box or cut-out; --hull 50% fills an outline rim out towards the convex hull; --smooth adds the smoothing pass. --model ID uses a model already in the project; --up, --rotate, --at X,Y, --start-depth, --corner, --allowance and --end-mill answer the rest. The tools are project tool numbers.

Adding a model

In the window: 3D model in the ribbon’s DRAW group, File > Add 3D model…, Add 3D model… on the Browser’s Drawing folder, or drop an STL file on the window. File > Import… and File > Open… take an STL too, as a new project. The model starts half the board wide at the drawing’s origin, as deep as its own proportion gives but no deeper than half the board (then Keep proportions is off), and its properties open.

From the command line:

chipload model add sign.chipload rose.stl --width 120 --depth 6 --at 20,20
  • The file: binary or text STL. It is kept inside the project, as pictures are, so the project stays one file; a model over 20 MB says that the project gets as large.
  • --width: its width on the board; the height follows. Without it, the model keeps its own size.
  • --depth: how deep the relief goes, from the model’s top at the stock top to its lowest point. Reliefs are usually flatter than the model: a 14 mm tall model carved 4 mm deep. Without it, the depth is in proportion to the width, and follows the width when it changes.
  • --at X,Y: where the lower-left corner of its footprint goes; --rotate turns it about its centre, any angle.
  • --up: which of the model’s axes points up: z (the default), y, x, or one of them reversed (-z …). Many models are drawn with Y up.
  • --units inch: STL files carry no unit. The size on import is read in mm, or in inches with this; since the model is placed by its width, it matters only for that first size.
  • --keep: how much of the model to keep, from its top: 0.5 or 50% keeps the top half and cuts away the rest, its back (see below). By default all of it.
  • --layer: the layer for its outline; by default the file’s name.

The report says how many triangles it has, any without an area that were left out, and how many open edges its surface has: a gap in a model lets the tool fall through to the relief’s floor.

A relief from a picture

A grey picture where brightness is height, a depth map, is a 3D model too: the usual way CNC reliefs are drawn and shared. Add 3D model…, the Relief wizard and model add take a PNG, JPEG or BMP as well as an STL. White is the top and black the bottom; the picture is sized by its width, and its depth is yours (4 mm to start with).

chipload model add sign.chipload portrait.png --width 120 --depth 4 --background 10%
chipload model set sign.chipload 1 --smoothing 0.5mm --gamma 1.5
chipload relief add sign.chipload portrait.png --width 120 --depth 4 --tool 2 --rough-tool 1 --smooth

Its properties, and step 2 of the wizard, have four settings in place of Up:

  • White is high: untick for a depth map that has it the other way round.
  • Smoothing: a blur over the heights, its radius on the board (0.2 mm to start with): it takes pixel noise and a photo’s grain away; 0 keeps the picture as it is.
  • Background: the darkest greys up to this share are not the model. The outline goes round the rest, and the rim round that; at 0 % the whole picture is the model.
  • Gamma: above 1 flatter low ground and steeper tops, below 1 the other way.

A large picture is averaged onto at most 800 points along its longer side (0.15 mm apart on a 120 mm relief), finer than a small ball nose can cut. Everything else is as for an STL: place, turn, keep from the top, the Relief operation with its rim, roughing and smoothing.

Inflated shapes

A black-and-white picture (a logo, lettering, a silhouette) can be inflated instead: each black shape rises from its edge towards its middle, as if blown up, and the white round it is not the model. Set Kind to Inflated in the picture’s settings, or:

chipload model add sign.chipload logo.png --inflate --width 120 --depth 4
chipload model set sign.chipload 1 --profile straight --reach 3mm
chipload relief add sign.chipload name.png --inflate --reach 2mm --width 200 --depth 5 --tool 2
  • Threshold: greys darker than this are the shapes; Automatic picks it from the picture. Invert: white is the shape.
  • Profile: Round rises steeply at the edge and is flat on top, a cushion; Straight rises evenly, to a ridge along each stroke, as if chiselled.
  • Reach: how far in from the edge a shape keeps rising. Automatic takes the widest shape’s half-width, so nothing levels off and narrow shapes stay lower than wide ones. Set it, and everything wider levels off at the full depth: for lettering, about half a stroke’s width gives letters all of one height.
  • Smoothing takes the pixels’ steps off the edges.

A letter’s hole is an edge too, so an “O” rises from both sides of its ring.

From an iPhone: Portrait photos carry a depth map, but inside the HEIC photo, which Chipload doesn’t read (it says so). Save the depth map as a PNG first, with an app or a Shortcut that exports it; it is small (about 768 × 576) and soft at the edges, so a little smoothing and a background level help. For an object you can walk round, a 3D-scanning app’s STL gives far more.

Turn and cut

When a model is added, Turn and cut opens (and later Turn and cut… in its properties): the model in 3D on the left, the relief it gives seen from above on the right, both redrawn as you change them.

  • Drag the model to look at it from any side.
  • Face up: which of the model’s sides faces up: Z, Y, X or one of them reversed.
  • Turn on the board: a quarter turn either way.
  • Keep from top: the cut level (below). The part kept is coloured, the part cut away grey, and the plate is the level.
  • Width and Depth, as in the properties; the size it gives is under the relief.

OK changes the model as one undo step and draws its outline again; Cancel leaves it as it was. The views are drawn from a light copy of the model (at most 50 000 triangles), so a large file turns smoothly; the outline, the toolpath and the G-code always come from the full model.

Keeping the front: the cut level

A model made to be seen from every side (a figure, a head, an animal) would be carved from its top all the way down to its lowest point: most of the depth goes into its far side, which the tool can’t reach anyway, and the side you see comes out flat. Keep from top in its properties (model set sign.chipload 1 --keep 50%) cuts it level at that share of its height and keeps the part above: the front half of a head keeps its whole outline, and its depth and width are now that half’s. So set the cut level first, then the depth the board allows; a depth you typed stays when the cut level changes. Where the cut goes through the model, the relief is cut to its floor.

Its outline

The model’s footprint seen from above becomes a closed shape on its layer, drawn again whenever the model is moved or changed. Select it for a profile to cut the relief out of the board, or for a pocket that clears round it: click the outline, Ctrl+click a shape round it, then Pocket, and the outline stays an island in the pocket. The outline keeps its shape ids when it is drawn again, so the pocket still finds it after the model moves.

Changing and removing

In the window the model is a row of the Browser’s Drawing folder; a click selects its outline and shows its properties: the file, its size on the board, Position (the lower-left corner before the turn), Rotation, Width, Depth with Keep proportions, Up, Keep from top and Layer. Each change draws the outline again, as one undo step. Selected on the canvas it has a picture’s handles: drag the middle to move it, the round handle to turn it, a corner to size it. In the 3D view it shows faintly as its surface, until a simulation is shown. Remove (in its properties or on its row) takes the model and its outline; a relief of it stays, with a problem, until it carves another model.

chipload model set sign.chipload 1 --rotate 15 --depth 4
chipload model set sign.chipload 1 --proportional     # the depth follows the width again
chipload model list sign.chipload                     # file, up axis, place and size
chipload model remove sign.chipload 1                 # the model and its outline

chipload set sign.chipload drawing.models.0.width=100 works as well, and draws the outline again.

Carving it: the Relief operation

In the window: New relief in the model’s properties or on its row, Relief in the ribbon’s OPERATION group (the small button under Surface), or Operation > New Relief; the last two carve the selected model, or the first. A relief is made in the active part, with a ball nose when the project has one.

From the command line:

chipload tool add "Ball 3" --type ballnose --diameter 3     # then `tool use` it in the project
chipload op add sign.chipload relief --model 1             # a relief of model 1, within its outline
chipload set sign.chipload operations.0.stepover=0.1 operations.0.angle=0.785

A Relief carves the model in parallel lines across its area, each point as low as the tool can go without cutting into the model: the tool is lowered onto the model’s triangles until it first touches one. A ball nose gives the smoothest finish; a tapered ball nose reaches finer detail; a bull nose (an end mill with round corners) finishes a wide relief faster; a square end mill or a V-bit works too, with their own marks.

  • Area: with --model, the model’s outline, with its rim round it. Other closed shapes can be selected for the area instead: a drawn rectangle, say. Without a rim the tool’s centre stays inside the area, which leaves a groove the tool’s width round the model and the board beyond untouched.
  • stepover: the distance between lines as a share of the tool; smaller is smoother and slower. With a ball or bull nose a new relief takes the widest stepover that leaves ridges of 0.02 mm between its lines on flat ground, which wood doesn’t show: 20 % for a 2 mm ball, 16 % for 1/8“, 12 % for 6 mm. The relief’s form shows the ridges for the stepover you set. Finer only takes longer: a 2 mm ball at 10 % leaves ridges of 0.005 mm and takes about three times as long. Other tools start at 10 %.
  • angle: the lines’ direction in radians from X (0 by default). Lines at an angle to the model’s long edges leave fewer marks.
  • zigzag: back and forth (true, the default), or every line the same way, lifting between them.
