  • start_depth: how far below the stock top the model’s top lies: a little on an unplaned board, so its uneven top doesn’t reach the relief. Its lowest point is the start depth plus the model’s depth, which the checker holds within the stock. The roughing pass still starts at the stock top, and a rim’s ramp still climbs to it.
  • Depth: the model’s depth. Changed in the relief’s properties, it is the model that changes, and every relief of it; a depth typed there turns the model’s Keep proportions off.

In the project file a relief reads:

{ "type": "relief", "name": "Relief 1", "enabled": true, "part": 1, "material": null,
  "model": 1, "select": { "model": 1, "shapes": null, "layer": null }, "tool": 2,
  "start_depth": 0.0, "end_depth": 5.0, "stepover": 0.1, "angle": 0.0, "zigzag": true,
  "feed": 1500.0, "plunge_feed": 500.0 }

model names the model it carves; end_depth is made again from the model’s depth whenever the project is read or changed.

A rim and a ramp

Round the model a relief cuts a rim: a flat at its full depth, then a ramp up to the board’s surface, so the model sits in a shallow dish instead of a trench:

 surface ─────╮                                   ╭───── surface
               ╲ ramp                       ramp ╱
                ╲        ╭── model ──╮          ╱
                 ╲______╱             ╲________╱
                   flat                  flat

Every line starts at the surface and runs down the ramp, so the tool never plunges to full depth at the area’s edge, and its shank stays clear of the outer wall. The roughing pass clears the rim with the rest.

  • Round: the model’s outline (the flat follows it, round at the corners) or a box, the rectangle round the model, with rounded corners if you like.
  • Flat width: how far the flat reaches from the outline or the box. 0 starts the ramp right at it.
  • Fuller (outline only): how far the outline is filled out before the flat goes round it. At 0 % the rim follows every notch of the model’s outline; higher fills narrow notches first, then wider ones, bridging them with round edges, and joins pieces that lie close together; at 100 % it goes round the model like a rubber band, its convex hull. The ramp keeps its angle all the way round.
  • Ramp: its slope from horizontal, 10° to 90°. At 45° it reaches out as far as the relief is deep; 90° is a straight wall.

A new relief has a rim 5 mm round its outline with a 45° ramp. Relief… and relief add make the flat wider when there is a roughing pass: its end mill’s width and the allowance on both sides, rounded up to a whole mm, so the end mill reaches the floor all round the model. When a flat is narrower than that, the plan notes it. In the window it is the relief’s Rim section; untick it for none. While the relief or one of its passes is open, the canvas shows the rim as dashed lines in the relief’s colour: the outline as Fuller fills it out (bolder), the flat’s outer edge and where the ramp reaches the top. They follow the settings as you change them, without generating the toolpath. From the command line:

chipload set sign.chipload 'operations.0.rim={"shape":"box","width":8,"ramp":0.785,"corner_radius":4}'
chipload set sign.chipload operations.0.rim.width=3
chipload set sign.chipload operations.0.rim.hull=0.5   # fuller: halfway to the hull
chipload set sign.chipload operations.0.rim=null      # no rim

ramp is in radians (0.785 is 45°). Holes in the outline are cut to the depth. A board fitted to the drawing grows to hold the rim; on a board of fixed size, the plan notes when the ramp reaches past its edge, where it is cut off.

Along each line the tool stays within 0.01 mm of where the model needs it: more points are added where the surface bends. When the program is made, the path is checked against the model once more by a simpler test of its own; if anything would cut into the model, nothing is made and the report says where.

Roughing first

A finishing ball nose is small, and a relief can be deeper than it should cut in one go (the plan and the relief’s form warn when the relief is deeper than the tool is wide). A rim’s ramp spares the tool the plunges, but on the way down each line still shaves a strip as tall as the depth there. A roughing pass takes most of the material first with a larger tool. In the window: Add roughing pass ▸ on the relief’s row in the Browser (right-click), with your project’s and library’s end mills and plain ball noses; the pass shows under the relief, with its own properties. Remove on its row, or Delete with it selected, takes it away. From the command line:

chipload op add-roughing sign.chipload --op 1 --tool 1 --allowance 0.5mm
chipload set sign.chipload operations.0.roughing.step_down=2.5
chipload op remove-roughing sign.chipload --op 1
  • The tool: an end mill or a plain ball nose (not a V-bit or a tapered ball nose). Its feeds, step down and stepover come from the tool library’s cutting data, or else from the relief, half the tool’s diameter and 0.4.
  • Levels: it goes down one step_down at a time from the stock top to the relief’s lowest point, along the relief’s lines (stepover apart, at the relief’s angle), and cuts only where material is left above each level.
  • allowance: what it leaves on the model for the finish, 0.5 mm by default, all round, not only above: on steep sides too.
  • Never wider than the finish: its area is the relief’s, shrunk so its edge stays where the finishing tool reaches. The finish takes the narrow ring left inside the outline itself.

It is cut just before the relief, as pass Operation 1.1 (Relief 1, roughing), with its own tool; the program is checked to keep the allowance off the model. In the project file it is the relief’s roughing:

"roughing": { "tool": 1, "feed": 1500.0, "plunge_feed": 500.0, "step_down": 3.0,
  "stepover": 0.4, "allowance": 0.5, "enabled": true }

Smoothing the steps

Along its lines the roughing end mill follows the surface, but between them, on ground that rises across the lines, its flat bottom leaves a stair: steps one stepover wide and as tall as the slope makes them, 2.4 mm on a 45° ramp at 40 % of a 6 mm end mill. Smaller step-downs don’t change them. A small ball nose would take each step in one bite.

A smoothing pass takes them off: after the roughing, the roughing end mill follows the surface once more, no levels, with its lines across the roughing’s, and leaves the same allowance. Ground that rises along either set of lines comes out clean, so a box rim’s four ramps do; on curved ground the steps shrink to the smoothing’s stepover times the slope. A larger ball nose works too: it leaves small, even ridges everywhere, for one more tool change. A roughing end mill with a corner radius (a bull nose) leaves smaller steps to begin with, and smooths them better.

In the window: Add smoothing pass on the relief’s or the roughing pass’s row in the Browser (right-click), or in the roughing pass’s properties; on a relief without a roughing pass it is greyed out, its hover saying to add one first. The pass shows under the relief after the roughing, with its own properties (tool, stepover, the lines’ angle, allowance, feeds); Delete with it selected removes it. From the command line:

chipload op add-smoothing sign.chipload --op 1                 # the roughing tool
chipload op add-smoothing sign.chipload --op 1 --tool 3 --allowance 0.3mm
chipload op remove-smoothing sign.chipload --op 1
  • Defaults: the roughing pass’s tool, feeds and allowance; lines a quarter turn from the relief’s; stepover 15 % with an end mill, or with a ball nose the widest that leaves 0.1 mm ridges on flat ground.
  • Only after roughing: it follows the surface in one go, so it needs the roughing pass, ticked, before it; removing the roughing pass removes it too.

It is cut between the roughing and the relief, as Operation 1.2 (Relief 1, smoothing); the program is checked to keep the allowance off the model. In the project file it is the relief’s smoothing:

"smoothing": { "tool": 1, "feed": 1500.0, "plunge_feed": 500.0, "stepover": 0.15,
  "angle": 1.5708, "allowance": 0.5, "enabled": true }

Signs and frames

An engraved sign is a few words with a frame round them, cut out of a board. New sign… (below) makes the whole sign, its operations too. Draw > Frame… only draws a frame: round the text or shapes you have selected, centred on them, with border lines inside it, or to a size as a blank, for a frame you cut with operations of your own. A sign you will make again can be kept in the shape library. (Design: docs/specs/shape-library.md.)

A sign in one step

New sign… (Sign in the ribbon’s WIZARDS group, or Wizards > New sign…) makes a whole sign: the words, a frame round them, and every operation that cuts it, in a part of its own named after the words (a new project’s empty part becomes it). The dialog draws the sign as it will be and lists the operations it adds. With no project open, New sign… on the start page starts a new project for it, with the choices last made in the new project dialog (the library’s defaults the first time), without asking for them again: the sign fits the board round itself. Cancel the sign and the empty project stays. (Design: docs/specs/sign-maker.md, sign-size-and-start.md.)

  • Words, Font, Height: the text, centred on the board. The font list is the text tool’s: your recently used fonts first (the last one picked is chosen), then the built-in fonts and your computer’s (Text). With shapes selected when you open it (a traced picture, a text, drawn shapes or a DXF’s), the sign goes round the selection instead: those shapes are carved with the V-bit (an operation named Carving) and the frame follows them, a picture traced again too. Write words instead goes back to new words. A raised sign round a picture’s or a DXF’s shapes can’t be made yet (its background pocket needs them grouped with the frame, and a group takes only drawn shapes and texts).
  • Frame: as for Frame… below: the kind, margin, corner, borders and their groove width. The frame follows the words afterwards.
  • Words (with the Oval frame): Straight, or Round for a round plaque: the first line goes along the top of a circle, the second along its bottom, both on one radius (text on an arc), and any further lines straight in the middle. The radius is the least that keeps the longer line within 150° of the circle and the middle clear; the frame is the circle round their ring, the margin out from the letters, and it follows them when you change the texts’ radius, words or size.
  • Size: Fit the words (the sign grows round letters of the Height given) or Fixed, for a sign that has to fit a place: a door plate of 300 × 100 mm, a round plaque of 200 mm. Fixed asks for the Outer size (for round words on the round frame, a Diameter) instead of the height: that is the outline that is cut out, and the letters get the largest height that fits inside the borders, gap and margin, to 0.1 mm, shown as Letters while you type. The frame keeps that size afterwards: changing the words later doesn’t grow it or refit the letters, so change the text’s height yourself if they no longer fit. Round shapes already drawn, the frame is that size round them.
  • Style:
    • Engraved: the letters V-carved; in a single-line font, a Follow path of the V-bit down each stroke instead, at the Engrave depth (1 mm by default; --engrave-depth on the command line). Raised and Inlay need an outline font;
    • Raised: the background inside the innermost border cut down round the letters with the end mill (Background depth, 3 mm by default), the letters and the border left standing; it needs a border. Inside corners round the letters keep the end mill’s radius;
    • Inlay: the letters as a V-carve inlay, the plug in a board of its own.
  • Edge: a chamfer of that width round the outline, cut with the V-bit before the sign is cut out: its flank on the outline, its tip in the waste. Signs made before 0.37.0 keep their edge as a follow path centred on the outline.
  • V-bit, End mill: the project’s tools, then under From your library: your tool library’s (marked +). A library tool is copied into the project when you click Make sign, not before. A project without such a tool starts on the library’s. The V-bit carves the letters and the grooves, the end mill cuts the sign out (and clears a raised background).
  • Board: Fit round the sign (on in an empty project) fits the stock round the drawing with the margin, at the thickness.

It adds, in cutting order: Letters (or Background, or the inlay’s pocket), Borders (a follow path on the border lines as deep as the groove width needs), Edge chamfer, and Cut out (a profile outside the outline, through, with tabs). Feeds come from the tools’ cutting data. The words and the frame are one group: a click selects the sign, a drag moves it. All of it is one undo step, and every part of it is ordinary shapes and operations afterwards.

Signs from a list

Name plates, door signs, place cards and drawer labels are the same sign with different words. Make them all at once from a list: in New sign…, choose Signs: From a list and type or paste the list, one sign per line, with | in a line to start that sign’s second line, with or without spaces round it (ANNA BERG | ROOM 12); the dialog says so under the list. A list starts on Size: Fixed; the preview shows the grid and the count, drawn alongside while you type (typing never waits for it). The operations are listed once the signs are made. Make sign makes them alongside too: a long list in a script font takes a few seconds, and the dialog says Making the sign… until they are in the project.

  • Every sign the same: the font, frame, borders, style, edge and tools are the ones set. With Size: Fixed every sign is that size and every sign gets the same letter height, the largest that fits the longest line of the list, so the signs match. With Fit the words, each frame follows its own words.
  • One part, cut at once: the signs go in one part named after the first and the count (“ANNA BERG +19”). Its operations cut every sign together: all the letters, then all the borders and edges with the V-bit, then every cut-out with the end mill, so the tool changes once, not twice for every sign. The operations select the batch’s layers (ANNA BERG +19 letters, … borders, … outline), so a name corrected later is still cut.
  • Each sign stays a sign: its words and frame are grouped, and the frame follows its words.
  • Laid out in a grid 10 mm apart: as near square as the count allows when the board is fitted round the signs; on a board you keep, as many to a row as fit, from its top left, and a list that doesn’t fit is refused with the board size it needs.
  • Styles: engraved (single-line fonts too) and raised (one background pocket per sign, all with the end mill). Inlaid signs are made one at a time: each plug needs a board of its own.
  • On the command line: chipload sign add P --list names.txt --size 200x60 --vbit 2 --end-mill 1, or --list-words "ANNA BERG|ROOM 12\nBO LIND|ROOM 14"; --list-gap sets the space between signs.

A frame round a text

  1. Write the text (Text) and leave it selected, or select any shapes, a traced picture for instance: the frame goes round all of them.
  2. Right-click the selection and choose Frame round the selection…, or Draw > Frame… (the way to a frame to a size, with nothing selected). Its values open under Selection, and the frame shows on the canvas as you change them.
  3. Pick a frame from the row of pictures, set the values, and click Add. The frame is added as one undo step, and its shapes are selected.

Clicking a shape while the tool is open makes it what the frame goes round (Ctrl+click adds one); a click on empty space, or on the previewed frame, changes nothing. Esc or Cancel ends the tool. The tool keeps your values until you close Chipload.

The frames:

FrameIts corner size is
Rectangledepends on its Corners (below)
Oval(an ellipse touching the four sides)
Arched tophow far the top arc rises above the top corners
Arched top and bottomhow far each arc rises beyond the corners
Gablehow far the pointed top rises above the top corners
Shieldhow far below the straight sides the point is; from half the width down, the sides curve into a point, shallower the bottom is one arc
Plaquehow far the short sides bulge out
Bannerhow deep the V cut into each short end is (forked ends)
Octagon(the corners cut off at 45°, the cut sides as long as the shorter sides)

A rectangle’s Corners:

CornersIts corner size is
Sharp(none)
Roundthe corners’ radius
Notchthe radius of the quarter circles cut into each corner
Chamferhow far each corner is cut off at 45°, along each side
Stepthe side of the square cut out of each corner (at most a third of the shorter side)

The values:

  • Margin: from the box round what is selected to the innermost line, so the words sit evenly inside the border nearest them (10 mm by default). The frame is centred on that box and made just large enough: an oval grows in proportion, an arched top grows taller, a plaque grows along its long side. Its size shows under the values.
  • Corner: the corner size (above). Unticked, it is a tenth of the frame’s shorter side, shown greyed; tick it to set your own. One too large for the frame is refused, with the largest that fits.
  • Borders: 0 to 3 lines inside the outline, each Gap further in (5 mm by default). They follow the outline’s shape: a notched frame’s borders are notched too.
  • Border corners (a rectangle with borders): the borders’ own corners, at the same gap, such as a round sign with a notched line inside. As the outline follows its shape.
  • Groove width: how wide a border’s groove will be at the surface: the V-bit’s width at the depth it cuts. The margin is then measured to the inner edge of the innermost groove, so the words keep their distance from the cut, not from the line. 0 (the default) measures to the line.
  • Double lines: each border two grooves, twice the groove width apart, which leaves a ridge as wide as a groove between them. It needs the groove width. The gap is then from one border’s inner line to the next border.
  • Layer, Border layer: the outline goes on frame, the borders on border, so that each can be cut by its own operation.

A frame is ordinary drawn shapes: a rounded or plain rectangle, a circle for a round oval, otherwise a polyline of lines and arcs. Operations cut it like any other shape.

The frame follows its text

A frame remembers what it goes round. Change the words, the font or the height of the text, or move it, and the frame is drawn again round it, with its borders, in the same undo step. Its shapes keep their ids, so the operations that cut them go on cutting them. A frame round a picture follows it when it is traced again, at another size or place; one round other shapes follows them when they move. A frame round only some of a text’s letters or a picture’s shapes keeps those shapes by id, and lets go when they are made again.

Select the frame (its outline or a border) and its values show under Selection, in a Frame card: the kind, margin, corner, borders and gap. Change them there and the frame is drawn again. A frame drawn to a size shows its width and height instead, and follows nothing.

The frame lets go, and becomes plain shapes that follow nothing, when:

  • you edit one of its shapes by hand: move it alone, drag a corner, change its values in its own form (moving the frame with its text keeps it following);
  • one of its shapes is deleted, or everything it went round is;
  • Detach in its card, on purpose.

A note on the status bar says so each time. Undo brings the frame back as it was.

An older Chipload refuses to open a project with a frame that follows something, and says why; detach the frame first to open the project there.

A frame to a size

With nothing selected, Draw > Frame… asks for the outline’s Width and Height instead of the margin, and the frame is centred on the stock: a blank for a sign, or a plaque to write on afterwards.

Cutting the sign

A sign is usually three operations, in this order:

  1. a V-carve on the text’s layer, with a V-bit;
  2. a Follow path on the border layer with the same V-bit: a groove along each border line, as wide as the V-bit is at that depth. Set its Groove width to the frame’s, and its end depth follows. (A V-carve of a border would carve the whole area inside it.);
  3. a Profile outside the frame layer with an end mill, to cut the sign out, with tabs.

The stock can sit round the drawing, sticking out equally on every side (Stock and work zero), so the board needs to be only as large as the sign.

From the command line

chipload text add sign.chipload "SIGN" --height 30 --at 150,80 --anchor center
chipload frame add sign.chipload --kind rounded --around-text 1 --borders 1 --corner 8 --margin 12
chipload frame add blank.chipload --kind oval --width 300 --height 150 --borders 2
chipload frame add sign.chipload --corners notch --around-text 1 --borders 1 --double --border-width 2
chipload frame add plate.chipload --kind banner --width 300 --height 90 --corner 25
chipload sign add sign.chipload "THE NÄSLUNDS" --font FreeSerif --frame rectangle --corners notch \
    --borders 1 --double --style engraved --edge chamfer:3 --vbit 2 --end-mill 1 --thickness 18
chipload sign add plaque.chipload "MAKER SPACE\nEST. 2026" --frame oval --round --height 12 \
    --borders 1 --vbit 2 --end-mill 1 --thickness 12
chipload sign add door.chipload "SMITH\n12" --size 300x100 --vbit 2 --end-mill 1   # letters as high as fit
chipload frame kinds
chipload frame list sign.chipload                              # the frames that follow, with their values
chipload frame set sign.chipload 7 --margin 15 --borders 2     # change them: drawn again
chipload frame detach sign.chipload 7                          # plain shapes from now on

sign add takes the words, or instead --around, --around-text or --around-image to make the sign round what is drawn; it takes the frame’s options as frame add does (--frame for the kind), and --style engraved|raised|inlay, --relief, --edge none|chamfer:WIDTH, --vbit, --end-mill (project tool ids), --thickness and --board-margin (or --keep-stock); --json gives the new part, text, outline, borders, group and operations. --kind is one of the frames above (rectangle, oval, arched, double-arch, gable, shield, plaque, banner, octagon); --corners (sharp, round, notch, chamfer, step) picks a rectangle’s corners, and the older names rounded, notched, chamfered and stepped still work as kinds. --border-corners, --double and --border-width set the borders; frame set takes the same, and --border-corners-off to make the borders follow the outline again. --around takes shape ids, --around-text text ids (chipload text list), --around-image picture ids (chipload image list); without either, --width and --height give the outline’s size and --at its centre (the stock’s centre by default). The new shapes’ ids are printed, the outline’s first; --json gives them with the frame’s size and corner. frame set and frame detach take the id of the frame’s outline or of one of its borders. Every command that changes a text or a shape draws the frames that follow it again before it saves, and prints a line for any frame it let go of.

The shape library

Shapes you use again and again, such as a frame you like, a house-number plate or a logo with its border, can be kept in your shape library and put into any project. An entry holds drawn shapes and texts; a text stays a text, so a saved sign can get new words. (Design: docs/specs/shape-library.md.)

In the window

The Shapes panel is the library, at the start a tab beside Properties on the right (arranged as you like). It holds a search field, then the entries as small pictures with their names, the ones in no group first and each group under a header that folds.

  • Insert: drag an entry onto the canvas. Its outline follows the pointer, snapped as a drawn point is, and where you let go the middle of its box lands. Or double-click it: it goes in the middle of the view. Either way the new shapes are selected, as one undo step. An entry of more than one shape or text comes in as a group named after it, so one click selects it again later, to V-carve it for example. Esc while dragging lets go of it.
  • Save: select shapes and texts, then drag a drawn shape of the selection, or a line of the selected DXF, picture or text, onto the Shapes tab (onto its name, or onto a group to save there), or click Save selection…, or right-click on the canvas: Save to shape library…. A name field opens, with the text’s words or the layer’s name in it; Enter saves. A name that is taken asks before it is replaced.
  • Right-click an entry: Insert in the middle of the view, Insert round the selection (scaled to fit round what is selected, with the frame tool’s margin), Rename…, Move to a group, Delete…, Show in folder.
  • Groups: New group; drag an entry onto a group to move it there (or to the top for none). Right-click a group’s header to rename it, delete it (only when empty) or show its folder.
  • The tab stays open while you select shapes on the canvas. Clicking something in the Browser, or starting a tool with values (Frame…, Array…, a text), shows Properties again.

Where it is kept

The library is a folder, shapes in your settings folder (chipload shape library path says where). Each entry is one file, NAME.chipload-shapes, so an entry is backed up, shared or deleted like any file. Groups are folders in it: shapes/Signs/ is the group Signs.

DXF and SVG files you copy into the folder are in the library too, inserted as an import would read them (a DXF needs its units set). Imported shapes saved to the library become drawn shapes; the link to their file isn’t kept. A text whose font isn’t on the computer it is inserted on keeps its letters, as in Text.

On the command line

chipload shape save sign.chipload 5 6 --texts 1 --name "Signs/Welcome"   # shapes S5, S6 and text 1
chipload shape library list                                # the entries, by group (also just `shape library`)
chipload shape insert part.chipload "Signs/Welcome" --at 150,80
chipload shape insert part.chipload "Signs/Welcome" --around 3,4 --margin 10   # scaled round S3, S4
chipload shape insert part.chipload "Signs/Welcome" --width 300 --layer sign
chipload shape library rename "Signs/Welcome" "Signs/Hello"   # another group moves it
chipload shape library move "Signs/Hello" Doors             # "" for no group
chipload shape library remove "Doors/Hello"
chipload shape library group add Doors
chipload shape library group rename Doors "Front doors"
chipload shape library group remove "Front doors"           # only an empty one
chipload shape library path                                # the folder
  • save: the shapes and texts given; a letter of a text saves the whole text. An entry of the same name is replaced only with --replace, and kept as .bak.
  • insert: new shapes and texts with new ids, the middle of the entry’s box at --at (the stock’s middle without it), at the size it was saved, or scaled: --width, --height, --scale, or --around shapes with a --margin round their box. Shapes keep the layers they were saved on, or all go on --layer.
  • remove moves the file to the folder’s .deleted, where it can be taken back. A group is removed only when it is empty.
  • list names an entry that can’t be read and why; it is never changed.

Groups

A group keeps drawn shapes and texts together: a sign’s frame, border and words, or a logo inserted from the shape library. One click selects all of it, and it moves, turns, scales and is deleted as one. An operation made from a group cuts what is in the group, also shapes added to it later. (Design: docs/specs/shape-groups.md.)

What can be in a group: drawn shapes and texts (a text whole, with its letters). A DXF’s shapes and a traced picture’s already belong to their file or picture, which keeps them together; explode the DXF, or put the picture through the shape library, to group its shapes. A shape is in one group at most, and groups don’t hold groups.

Inserting a library entry of more than one shape or text makes a group named after it.

In the window

  • Group: select shapes and texts (Ctrl+click, or a box), then right-click > Group, or Ctrl+G. With one group among them, the rest join it; with several, they become one. Right-click > Add to group ▸ adds the selection to a group you pick.
  • Select: a click on any shape or letter of a group selects all of it, and so does its row in the Browser (under Drawing, with its texts and shapes under it; a grouped text or shape isn’t listed again elsewhere). Ctrl+click on shapes of the selected group leaves them out of the selection and back in.
  • One shape or text inside: double-click it on the canvas, or click its row under the group in the Browser. Then right-click > Remove from group takes it out of the group; it stays where it is.
  • Move, turn, scale: drag any of its shapes, or the handles of its box: the texts in it move, turn and scale too. The box turns with the group and stays turned.
  • Align and distribute: a group lines up as one, with its texts: alone with To the stock, or with other shapes (Ctrl+click them).
  • Ungroup: right-click > Ungroup, or Ctrl+Shift+G. Del deletes the group with its shapes and texts. Properties shows its name, which you can change, what it holds, and Ungroup.
  • Copy and paste: a whole group of drawn shapes pastes as a new group.

Cutting a group

Select the group and choose an operation, V-carve for example: it cuts the group as it is each time, so a shape added to the group later is cut too, and one taken out isn’t. Ctrl+click on shapes of the selected group leaves them out of the selection and back in, as for a picture or a DXF: the operation cuts the rest. In the project file the operation’s selection is {"group": 1}, or {"group": 1, "exclude": [7]}.

Ungrouping keeps what an operation made from the group cuts: it gets the group’s shapes by id. A group with texts in it can’t be ungrouped while an operation cuts it (an operation can’t name a text’s letters by id): choose that operation’s shapes again first, or take the texts out of the group.

On the command line

chipload group add sign.chipload 5 6 --texts 1 --name Sign   # prints the group's id
chipload group list sign.chipload
chipload group set sign.chipload 1 --name "Door sign" --add 9,10 --add-texts 2 --remove 6 --remove-texts 3
chipload group move sign.chipload 1 --dx 10 --dy 0
chipload group turn sign.chipload 1 --angle-deg 90           # about its middle, or --about X,Y
chipload group scale sign.chipload 1 --factor 0.5            # its texts' height scales too
chipload group remove sign.chipload 1                        # ungroup: the shapes stay, free
chipload group delete sign.chipload 1                        # the shapes and texts too

group add with shapes of one group among them adds the rest to that group; with shapes of several groups, those groups become one.

Instances

Each part can be cut several times. Every extra time is an instance of the part: the same shapes and operations, cut again somewhere else, moved, turned and perhaps mirrored. Instances are linked to the part: change the part, and every instance follows.

In the window:

  • Right-click the part in the Browser: Add instance places it again beside what it cuts, 10 mm away; Add mirrored instance does the same, mirrored left to right.
  • On the canvas, instances are placed in their setup’s tab (the canvas): each drawn where it is cut, with a badge below its bottom left: a four-way arrow, its part’s name and its number (Side #2), and a mirror mark when it is mirrored. One that is off is dashed. Click an instance (its badge or outline) to select it, drag it to move it. Double-click it to open its part in Drawing. A selected instance has a round turn handle with a curved arrow: drag it to turn the instance about its middle. Under the pointer an instance’s outline thickens and the pointer becomes a hand, so you see what a drag takes. Hold Shift while moving to keep it to 15° steps from where you grabbed it (straight across, up or at 45°). Turning snaps to quarter turns within 3°; hold Alt to turn freely. Escape during a drag puts it back; each drag is one undo step.
  • Drag the part in: drag a part’s row from the Browser onto a setup’s tab of the canvas: its outline follows the pointer, on the grid’s steps (hold Alt to place it freely), and letting go places a new instance there, not turned or mirrored, selected. Dropped on another setup’s sheet beside the board, it goes on that setup. From Drawing, hold the dragged part over a setup’s tab for half a second: the tab opens. Escape during the drag places nothing. A board cut in pieces that already holds the part refuses it, and the Report says why.
  • More of them on the canvas:
    • Right-click an instance (its badge, or its outline): Duplicate, Turn 90° left, Turn 90° right, Mirror, Reset to where drawn, Don’t cut (or Cut) and Remove. Reset to where drawn puts it back where the part is drawn, not turned; its mirror and whether it is cut stay.
    • Duplicate places a new instance, turned and mirrored like this one and on the same board, beside everything the part cuts, and selects it. Duplicate again and the next goes beside that one.
    • Ctrl+drag (⌘ on a Mac) an instance to drag a copy of it off; the original stays. Escape during the drag drops the copy.
    • Ctrl+C, then Ctrl+V, with an instance selected, works as Duplicate, as many times as you paste. It copies the placement, not the shapes.
  • In the Browser, each instance has a row with its tick under its setup (“Side #2”), never under its part: copies and placing are setups’ work, so they are listed under the setup that cuts them, even with one board (Setup 1). Right-click it for the same menu as on the canvas, and Move to setup ▸; Delete removes the selected one.
  • Several setups: drag a part’s row onto a setup to cut it there, every instance of it moved (right-click it for Move to setup ▸); hold Ctrl (⌘ on a Mac) as you drop to place it there too, a new instance where the part is drawn (Place on setup ▸). Drag an instance’s row onto another setup to cut it there, its number and place kept. The part’s properties list Where it is cut, its instances by setup.
  • Its properties set whether it is cut, its mirror, its rotation (any angle) and how far it is moved.
  • A grid: the part’s properties make one under Instances.

On the command line, chipload part instance places them one by one. With several parts, give --part.

chipload part instance add part.chipload                  # beside the part, 10 mm from its cut
chipload part instance add part.chipload --angle 90 --mirror left-right
chipload part instance add part.chipload --at 300,0       # moved 300 mm right of the part
chipload part instance add part.chipload --like 3         # turned, mirrored and on the board of instance 3
chipload part instance list part.chipload                 # their numbers, places and turns
chipload part instance set part.chipload 3 --angle 45     # turn instance 3 (also --at, --mirror, --off, --on)
chipload part instance set part.chipload 3 --reset        # back where the part is drawn, not turned
chipload part instance remove part.chipload 3             # or --all: the part is cut once
chipload part instance array part.chipload --grid 3x2 --gap 10   # a grid in place of them
chipload part instance move part.chipload 3 --setup 2     # instance 3 cut from setup 2's board instead
chipload part set part.chipload 1 --order by-instance     # every operation on one instance, then the next

  • Boards: each instance is cut from one setup’s board. add and array put new instances on the board the part is cut from, or on the one --setup names; move puts an instance on another board, keeping its number and place. With several setups, list says each instance’s board.

  • Numbers: the part as first placed is instance 1 (placed or where drawn); the others are numbered from 2. Every instance is alike: instance 1 too can be mirrored, switched off, moved to another setup or removed, and numbers are kept when one is removed or moved.

  • Place: an instance is mirrored first (left-right or top-bottom), then turned (--angle, degrees counter-clockwise), both about the middle of the part’s box, then moved by --at from where the part is drawn. A new instance without --at goes beside everything the part cuts, 10 mm away, its bottom level with the part’s.

  • Climb stays climb: a mirrored instance is cut in the same direction as the part, not as a plain mirror image would be. Tabs, starts, tabs along X or Y and a pattern’s lines turn with the instance, so every instance comes out the same.

  • A grid: part instance array (or the Instances section) makes the part the grid’s first cell and the other cells its instances, numbered row by row from 2, in place of the instances it had. Pitch is the distance from cell to cell; gap works the pitch out from the part’s real cut, tool included. A negative pitch grows the grid left or down. chipload copies is the same command under its old name, kept for scripts: copies --grid 3x2 --gap 10, copies --order by-instance, and copies --off removes every instance.

  • Order:

    • by operation (the default): each operation on every instance, then the next, so tool changes happen once;
    • by instance: every operation on one instance, then the next. The instances are cut in their listed order, a grid’s row by row, back and forth. This was called by copy, and by part before Chipload 0.17; --order by-copy and --order by-part still work.

  • Checked:

    • every instance is planned and checked like the part;
    • instances whose cuts would overlap each other, or another part, are refused when the project is run, and the commands warn as soon as you place one there;
    • instances have to fit the stock and the machine like anything else.

  • Limits: up to 100 columns or rows in a grid, and 1 000 instances.

  • Display: the setup’s tab draws the instances where they are cut, the setup sheet faded. The setup sheet and the playback say which instance is being cut, and the sheet says where a part placed elsewhere than drawn is cut.

Placing the part

The part’s first instance can be placed too: cut moved and turned, while its shapes stay where they are drawn. Its shapes are what you edit; every instance, the first included, is a placement of them. This lets you lay parts out on a sheet without moving the drawing, for instance when several parts come from one DXF.

In the window:

  • Right-click the part in the Browser, Place…: its first instance is selected in its setup’s tab, with its round turn handle, and its form opens with its rotation and how far it is moved. Drag it, turn it by its handle, or press Q for the Compass. Reset placement puts it back where drawn.
  • On the canvas, once placed, it is #1 in its setup’s tab where it is cut, picked, dragged and turned like any instance. Its shapes stay where they are drawn, in Drawing; edit them there, and every instance follows. Drawing shows no board behind a part that isn’t cut where it is drawn.
  • In the Browser, its instances are under Setup 1 (“Side #1”, “Side #2”), the setups listed once the part is placed.
  • The Compass (Q) works on a selected instance, the first included: drag an arrow to move it along, the ring to turn it, the centre to put it on a point, or type a value. It moves and turns instances only; copies and arrays are for shapes.
chipload part place part.chipload --at 300,0 --angle 90   # the part cut 300 mm right, turned
chipload part place part.chipload --reset                 # back where drawn
chipload part instance set part.chipload 1 --at 350,0     # the same, as instance 1
  • The part’s own mirror (part set --mirror) mirrors its shapes, so every instance; an instance’s mirror mirrors that one only.
  • Its instances stay where they were: they are placed from the part as drawn, not from instance 1.
  • The board: a board that fits the drawing holds the part where it is cut, not its shapes where they are drawn.
  • Dowel holes belong to the board: they are cut where drawn, once, never placed or repeated per instance.
  • Checked like an instance: a placed part may not cut into another part. Parts cut where they are drawn may still overlap each other, as before.
  • In a setup cut in pieces the part can’t be placed yet.

Instances aren’t nested automatically yet: to fit different parts on one sheet, place each part and its instances, or arrange them in CAD.

Projects saved before Chipload 0.38 kept a grid of copies; they open with that grid as instances, cut in the same order, so the G-code stays the same.

Nesting

Nesting places every instance of a setup’s parts on its board, as close together as they fit, so a sheet holds as many parts as it can. Every part that is on takes part, instance 1 included.

Chipload nests what the tools really cut: each instance’s toolpath with the width of its tool, tabs and ramps included, not only the drawing. Between two cuts it leaves the gap, a web of board that keeps the sheet stiff and the tabs holding; at the board’s edge it leaves the margin, room for screws or clamps. Only the instances’ places change: the drawing stays where it is, and every operation follows its instances. The result is planned and checked as any project is before it is kept.

In the window:

  • Right-click the setup in the Browser and choose Nest…, or Wizards > Nest… for the active setup. The canvas shows that setup’s tab, where the preview is drawn. The window shows the board’s size, the gap and the margin, the goal, and a row per part with how many times it is cut and how it may turn.
  • The goal says what the nest makes as small as it can, once it has placed as much as it can:
    • Compact (the default): the smallest box round the parts, a block in the lower-left corner. Good when the rest of the board is waste.
    • Keep the top free: the parts as low as they go, across the whole width, so a strip across the top is left to saw off and keep.
    • Keep the right side free: the parts as far left as they go, up the whole height, so a strip down the right is left.
  • The preview is worked out as soon as the window opens, and again with Preview after a change: the instances show on the board where they would go, outlined in the selection colour, the board dashed, and the boards taking what doesn’t fit in a row to its right. An instance larger than an empty board is parked beside them in amber and named in the window. Nothing changes until you choose Apply.
  • Apply places them as one undo step (Ctrl+Z puts everything back) and lists in the Report what went onto other boards and what was switched off; click an instance’s line to select it. Apply before a preview nests and applies in one go.
  • Nest again, in the setup’s right-click menu once it has been nested, nests it again with the same gap and margin and each part’s turns, the instances there are now, without the window: after you change a part, or moved instances by hand. It searches on from the board as it is and never makes it worse: when it finds nothing better, nothing moves and the status says “No better nest found; the board is as it was.” Press it again after a better nest and it searches on from there.

On the command line:

chipload nest part.chipload                         # the active setup, on its board
chipload nest part.chipload --all                   # every setup again, one pool per stock
chipload nest part.chipload --count 2=4             # part 2 cut four times: instances added first
chipload nest part.chipload --turns 2=none          # part 2 never turned (wood grain); others quarter turns
chipload nest part.chipload --gap 5 --margin 15     # more room between parts and at the edge
chipload nest part.chipload --goal low              # keep the top of the board free (also compact, left)
chipload nest part.chipload --dry-run               # say what would go where, write nothing
chipload part set part.chipload 2 --turns half      # part 2 turned 180° at most, kept for the next nest
  • Nest all setups again, in the project’s menu, the Setups folder’s menu and Wizards, with two boards or more that can be nested: every board of one stock is nested as one pool, the first filled first, then the next, each with its own gap, margin and goal; what fits on none goes onto new boards. Parts you removed or counts you lowered free room on the first boards, and instances move back to them. It keeps the new layout when it places more, or as many on fewer boards; otherwise each board is nested again on its own, as Nest again, never worse. A board left empty is kept (the Report says “Setup 3 is empty now”); remove it if you don’t need it. One undo step.
  • How it fills: from the board’s lower-left corner, by the goal: for compact each part where the box round everything placed grows least, for the others as low (or as far left) as it fits. It tries many orders of the parts and turns of each, for a moment (the window shows how far it has got), and keeps the layout that places the most and best meets the goal. The same project always gives the same layout. Nesting a setup that was nested before searches on from the board as it is and keeps it unless it finds better.
  • Turns: none, half (0° or 180°), quarter (the default) or every-15, for every part (--turns quarter) or one (--turns 2=none). They are kept in the project.
  • Counts: --count ID=N cuts part ID N times: instances are added (not mirrored), or the highest-numbered ones that are on removed; at least one stays. The count covers this board and the other boards of its stock that took what didn’t fit: raised from 8 to 9 with two already on Setup 2, one more is cut, and it goes onto Setup 2 while there is room. Lowered, those other boards give up theirs first.
  • Mirrored instances stay mirrored; nesting turns and moves them, never mirrors them.
  • What doesn’t fit goes onto more boards, as a slicer adds plates: to the next setup with the same stock and a size of its own, else to a new one made like this board (its size, work zero, gap and margin), nested there in turn, and so on until everything is placed. This is the same in the window, with Nest again and on the command line. The preview shows each board in a row to the right; the Report says “2 more on Setup 2, a new board like the first”. Instances keep their numbers. Don’t want a board it made? Remove that setup.
  • Larger than an empty board: an instance no board can take is switched off, kept where it was, and named (“Part 2, instance 4 is larger than an empty board: switched off”). Its row in the Browser turns amber with a dot, saying so when you hover it. Make the board larger, allow turns, or make the part smaller. A part whose stock isn’t this board’s goes to a board of its own stock instead.
  • The board must have a size of its own. A board that follows the drawing (fitted to it or centred on it) is given one with --board 1220x610: it becomes that size, its corner at the drawing’s origin.
  • The machine’s travel, when it is set in the machine, limits how much of a larger board is used, measured from the side where work zero is. A board that follows the drawing starts in the window at the travel; with a board surface, less the surface tool’s diameter along the board’s longer side, since facing runs from beside the board to beside it. A board whose surface would run past the travel is refused before nesting, naming the surface.
  • The gap, margin and goal (3 mm, 10 mm and compact until you choose) are kept with the setup, so the next nest uses them.
  • The result says how many were placed and the box they take: “8 of 8 placed, in 742 × 380 mm of the board’s 1200 × 590 mm (40 %)”, the board less its margin.
  • The setup sheet says the setup was nested: how many instances, the gap and margin, and the box the cuts take from the board’s lower-left corner, so you know what is left of the sheet.
  • Not yet: a setup cut in pieces or a two-sided setup can’t be nested, and small parts don’t go inside another part’s holes.

Everything chipload nest says is also in --json: what went where (placed, each part, instance, angle in radians and move in mm), what didn’t fit (unplaced), the goal (goal), the box round what was placed and the usable board (extent, usable, X and Y in mm, and used_pct, the box’s share), whether the board was given a size, and whether a nest again found nothing better (unchanged).

Parts

The operations of a project are grouped into parts. A part is a name, the operations that cut it, its instances, and an on/off switch. Most projects have one part. Use several when one sheet holds different things, for example 4 lids and 2 boxes.

  • What a part cuts: its operations select shapes, DXFs and layers as usual. A part owns no geometry, so one DXF with a layer per part can feed several parts.
  • Order: part by part. Each part is cut with all its instances before the next one starts, and within a part its operations run in order. Operations are numbered across the whole project (1, 2, 3 …), as the G-code comments and the report number them.
  • Tool changes: a tool used in two parts is changed back for the second part. Two parts that each use T1 and then T2 change tool three times. The report counts the changes.
  • Off: a part that is off is left out with all its operations.
  • The active part: new operations go into it. Adding a part makes the new one active.
  • A part or a setup is shown, never both. Select a part, or one of its operations, in the Browser: the canvas shows Drawing (the canvas), that part alone, once, where it is drawn, and Generate, the automatic update, the simulation and playback are of it, so a change to part 2 doesn’t plan part 1 again. The status bar says “part 2 of 2 · cycle …”. Other parts’ shapes are dimmed. Select the setup, the Stock, the Work zero or an instance (or click a setup’s tab or sheet) to see every instance on the board again. Selecting the project or a tool leaves the canvas as it is.
  • Its board: a part drawn in place is shown on its own board. A part placed elsewhere, or cut only as copies, is shown where it is drawn on a board fitted round it, its own thickness when it asks for one.
  • Statistics: the last section of a part’s properties gives its time, lengths and lifts, its operations’ summed with all of its instances (Statistics of a part).
  • Export writes the whole setup, every part on it that is on. When one part is shown alone, Export plans the setup first, then writes it.
  • One part’s G-code: right-click a part in the Browser and choose Export G-code of this part…. You get the program the project gives with every other part switched off: that part with all its instances, the same stock, work zero and machine, checked as every program is. It works for a part that is off too. The file name suggested is the project’s and the part’s (box-lid.nc), and the setup sheet, when one is written, is of that part and names it. The project isn’t changed. On the command line, --part ID on run, sheet and plan does the same, and can be given more than once.
  • Stock: all parts share the project’s stock and work zero. Parts cut from a different material belong in another project.
  • Safety:
    • Instances of a part must not cut into each other.
    • An instance must not cut into another part. The parts as drawn may overlap, for example an engraving over a cut-out.
    • Overlaps are refused.
  • In the G-code: with two parts or more, a comment marks where each part starts: (Part 2: box, cut 2 times).
chipload part list part.chipload                        # id, name, instances, operations
chipload part add part.chipload --name box              # an empty part, now active
chipload op part part.chipload --op 4 --part 2          # move operation 4 into part 2
chipload op move part.chipload --op 4 --to 2            # operation 4 cut second (joins that part)
chipload op duplicate part.chipload --op 2              # a copy right after it, "… (copy)"
chipload op remove part.chipload --op 3                 # as Delete in the app
chipload part instance add part.chipload --part 2   # part 2 placed again (see Instances)
chipload part set part.chipload 2 --name lid --off      # rename, turn off (--on)
chipload part activate part.chipload 1
chipload run part.chipload -o lid.nc --part 2           # only part 2's G-code
chipload part remove part.chipload 2 --with-operations  # without the flag: refused while it has operations

In the app, the Browser lists the parts under their setup, with their operations. Click a part to see its instances in Properties. Right-click it to activate, export, place again (an instance), move or delete it, or right-click its setup for a new one. Drag an operation onto a part to move it there.

A part’s stock

A part can say what it is cut from: a material from your library, a thickness, or both. Set it in the part’s properties under Stock, or with part set --material and --thickness. Left as Any board, as it starts, it asks for nothing.

  • Nothing moves by itself: setting it only records it. When the part’s board isn’t of that stock, its properties say so, This part’s board isn’t of its stock, with one button: Move to Setup 2 (6 mm) when a setup has it, else Move to a new 6 mm setup, made like the part’s board (its size or fit, its work zero, Z zero and overcut). Every instance moves; the status bar says where it went: “Made Setup 2 (6 mm) for Bottom”. One undo puts it back. On the command line, part set … --move.
  • Through cuts follow: a cut through the stock goes through the new board.
  • Boards are yours: Chipload never removes a board; set the part back to Any board and it stays where it is. The report notes a part on a board of another stock: “Bottom is to be cut from 6 mm, but this board is 18 mm”. A two-sided board or one in pieces never offers the move.
  • Nesting onto more boards puts each part on a board of its stock.

A DXF with a side and a bottom, the bottom from 6 mm plywood, the rest from 18 mm:

chipload import box.dxf -o box.chipload                 # one board, 18 mm
chipload part set box.chipload 2 --thickness 6mm --move # "Made Setup 2 (6 mm) for Bottom"
chipload run box.chipload -o box.nc                     # box-S1.nc and box-S2.nc, a sheet each

Mirrored parts

A part can be cut mirrored, left to right or top to bottom, while the drawing stays as you drew it. Set Mirror in the part’s properties, or chipload part set --mirror. Use it for left- and right-hand pieces from one drawing, the plug of an inlay, a stamp or branding iron whose text must read backwards, or a sign cut from the back of clear acrylic.

  • About its own centre: the part is mirrored about the centre line of the shapes its operations cut, so it stays where it is on the board and a board that fits its parts still fits. To cut a mirrored twin somewhere else, mirror a copy of its shapes in the drawing instead (shape mirror).
  • Settings mean what they say: inside stays inside, climb stays climb. Chipload plans the part as drawn with the cutting direction turned, then mirrors it, so a climb cut is still a climb cut on the mirrored part. Tabs placed by hand, dogbone corners and ring starts stay on their feature.
  • On the canvas: the toolpath and the simulation show the part as it is cut, and its shapes are drawn there dashed and faded. You still edit the shapes as drawn; the start and tab markers stay on them. The Browser marks the part mirrored.
  • What isn’t mirrored: a surface pass and dowel holes belong to the board, not the part.
  • Instances: each instance is the mirrored part, placed.
  • Text: a mirrored part’s text reads backwards on the board, and the report says so. On the back of a board the two mirrors cancel and it reads right.
  • The setup sheet says which parts are cut mirrored.
chipload part set part.chipload 1 --mirror left-right   # also top-bottom; none: as drawn

Profile

A profile cuts along a closed contour:

  • outside it, to cut a part out;
  • inside it, to cut a hole;
  • on the line.

Select the contours, then Profile (ribbon, OPERATION). See Side and direction and Depths and oversize for what every profile shares.

A new profile of outlines goes outside and through with tabs, of holes inside and through; an outline selected with its holes gives two profiles, the holes first (What a new operation starts with). A shape drawn inside another part’s outline isn’t taken for a hole of it: it is an outline of the part you add the profile to, cut outside with tabs. Inside an outline of the same part, or one no operation cuts yet, it is a hole.

Open contours (centre lines, construction lines) in the selection are skipped with a note. To cut along an open line, use Follow path.

The spiral ramp

A profile always goes down as one forward spiral along the contour: no plunge into the part, so there is no plunge mark, and any endmill can cut it, centre-cutting or not. In the operation’s Cut settings, right after the step down, the ramp angle (3° by default, at most 30°) says how steeply:

  • each pass goes down at the ramp angle, then runs level for the rest of its lap, and never more than one step down per lap (on small contours the angle alone decides, so a pass may take more than one lap);
  • at the end only the ramped stretch is cut level, so the floor is flat;
  • the tool rises out along the cut path, never back and forth.

A steeper angle makes the ramp shorter: 4 mm at 3° takes 76 mm of contour, at 10° 23 mm. In the file the angle is ramp_angle, in radians.

A tool marked not centre-cutting needs a ring at least its diameter long to ramp on (a small hole profiled inside can be shorter): its centre doesn’t cut, and on a shorter ring it never moves off the material under it. Such a ring is refused; use a centre-cutting tool.

Holding the part

Holding chooses how a part that is cut out stays in place: none, tabs, onion skin, or onion skin and tabs. Only the chosen kind’s settings show.

  • Tabs keep the part attached at a few points until you cut it free.
  • An onion skin holds it all round during the roughing; the last lap then cuts through the skin, and the part is free during that lap.
  • Both: the skin holds it all round during the roughing, and the tabs keep it from moving while the last lap cuts through the skin.

Tabs

Tabs are bridges of material left at the bottom of the cut, so a part cut out doesn’t come loose on the last pass.

Setting
counttabs per contour
placementaround: spread evenly along the contour; along X: on its bottom and top edges; along Y: on its left and right edges (below)
widththe bridge left on the part, along its edge (the flat top for sloped tabs)
heightthe bridge’s thickness, up from the bottom of the cut
flank angle90° for straight tabs (the tool goes straight up and down); less for sloped ones. A ramped profile gets 30° by default and follows the slope, which is gentler on the tool.
  • Around: tabs slide off corners to straight stretches, by up to a quarter of their spacing.
  • Spacing: they keep a tool diameter from each other and from the start of the contour.
  • Shown highlighted on the canvas.

Tabs along X or Y

To line the tabs up with the wood’s grain, choose placement along X or along Y. Along X puts them on each contour’s bottom and top edges, the edges that run in X: half of count on each (an odd one more at the bottom), evenly across the contour’s width, so 4 tabs on a rectangle sit at a quarter and three quarters across, two on each edge. Along Y does the same on the left and right edges. On a round part they go near its lowest and highest (or leftmost and rightmost) points.

  • Which one: with the grain along the tabs’ edges, a tab’s fibres run across its bridge and it cuts loose easily with a knife or chisel; with the grain across them, the fibres run along the bridge and it holds stronger. X and Y name the edges, so either works.
  • A tab that would cover the contour’s start moves along it, just clear. Tabs that don’t fit (too close together on a small part) stop generating with the reason: fewer tabs, or around.
  • Holes cut through with tabs get the same rule on their own edges.
  • A contour with tabs you placed by hand keeps them (below). The setup sheet says where the tabs are. In the file it is tabs.along, "x" or "y" (left out for around); on the command line chipload op tabs part.chipload --op 2 --along x (--around to go back), and a style keeps it.

Tabs placed by hand

You can put a contour’s tabs where you want them instead. Select the profile after generating: each tab has a small square on the canvas, hollow where Chipload spaced it and filled where you placed it.

  • Drag a tab along its contour; it generates again when you let go. A tab dropped where it can’t be (on the contour’s start, or too close to another tab) goes back where it was, and the status bar says why; drag it somewhere else. The same goes for a start point dropped onto a tab.
  • Right-click a tab to remove it, or to place that contour’s tabs again as the placement says.
  • Right-click the contour’s toolpath for Add a tab here.
  • Under Tabs in Properties, 5 tabs placed by hand counts them, and Reset places every contour’s tabs again as the placement says. Each change is one undo step.

On the command line, list them and add or remove one at a point on (or near) the contour:

chipload op tabs part.chipload --op 2
ring 1  (146.551, 132.176)  spaced
…
chipload op tab part.chipload --op 2 --at 162,91            # add one
chipload op tab part.chipload --op 2 --at 146.5,132 --remove
chipload op tab part.chipload --op 2 --at 162,91 --even     # this contour placed again
chipload op tab part.chipload --op 2 --reset                # every contour
  • The first tab you add or remove on a contour keeps its other tabs where Chipload had put them; from then on that contour has exactly the tabs you placed, and count is for the contours you haven’t touched.
  • A tab placed by hand stays where you put it, also on a corner (the report notes it).
  • Generating stops, naming the tab, if two tabs come closer than their width plus two tool diameters, if a tab covers the contour’s start (move the tab or the start), or if a tab is no longer on any contour of the operation (the drawing changed).
  • The points move with the drawing when you place it or move, turn or scale the drawn shape, and every copy gets them. In the file they are tabs.at, points in drawing coordinates.

Start point

Each contour’s cut starts at a point Chipload picks. To move it, select the operation, then drag the start marker along the contour on the canvas; right-click the marker to reset it. Tabs Chipload spaces move with the start; tabs placed by hand stay. In the file, starts holds the points.

Finishing pass

Finishing pass with a stock value:

  • the roughing laps leave that much on the wall;
  • one light lap at the wall at full depth then takes it off.

The wall comes out clean: the tool isn’t loaded on that lap, so it doesn’t deflect, and there are no step marks from the passes. The stock must be less than the tool radius.

Onion skin

Onion skin with a thickness:

  • the passes stop that far above the end depth;
  • one last lap cuts through the skin.

Small parts stay held until the very end, on a vacuum table or instead of tabs. The skin is at most one step down, and thinner than the tab height when there are tabs (onion skin and tabs under Holding).

Dogbones and T-bones

A round tool leaves a fillet of its radius in every inside corner, so a square tenon won’t go into the mortise. Corners reaches into them:

  • dogbones: the tool goes into the corner along its bisector;
  • T-bones: the tool goes in along the longer wall, so the notch is in the shorter wall, out of sight.

up to sets the sharpest corner angle that gets one (100° by default, so 90° corners drawn slightly off count). A corner the tool can’t reach at all (a notch narrower than the tool) is left round, with a note.

A bone that also cuts into another contour that is cut (a thin wall between this cut and another), or into another wall of its own (a short step next to the corner), is cut all the same, with a warning that names the corner: check the part there, or choose another bone for that corner. Lines that don’t count:

  • lines no enabled operation cuts (construction lines, a layer nothing cuts, a disabled operation’s shapes);
  • a line that meets what the operation cuts near the bone: on its own wall (a shared edge), crossing it, or, in a pocket, running into the pocket. It has been cut through there already.

Corner by corner

Any corner can have its own choice, whatever Corners says: round, dogbone, T-bone along the longer wall or T-bone along the shorter wall. It goes past up to too. So you can turn one T-bone the other way, leave out the one corner where a bone doesn’t fit, or leave Corners on round and give bones only to the corners that need them.

With the operation selected, every corner on the tool’s side has a small diamond on the canvas: filled, with the tool’s circle where its bone goes, when it gets one; hollow when it stays round; amber when the last generation’s tool couldn’t reach into it. A ring round the diamond marks a corner with its own choice. Click a diamond (or right-click it) for its menu: As the operation, Round, Dogbone, T-bone along the longer wall, T-bone along the shorter wall. Hovering an entry draws its bone; clicking it generates again. Each choice is one undo step. Under Corners in Properties, 2 corners chosen one by one counts them, and Reset gives every corner the operation’s setting again.

On the command line, list an operation’s corners and what each gets, then choose:

chipload op corners part.chipload --op 2
(11.214, 301)  90.0°  dogbone
(511.214, 301)  90.0°  dogbone
…
chipload op corner part.chipload --op 2 --at 11.214,301 --bone tbone --along shorter
chipload op corner part.chipload --op 2 --at 511.214,301 --bone none
chipload op corner part.chipload --op 2 --at 511.214,301 --as-operation   # its choice removed
chipload op corner part.chipload --op 2 --reset                           # every choice removed

A choice is kept as the corner’s point on the drawing, as start points are, and moves with the DXF when you place it. Every copy gets it. If the drawing changes so a chosen corner is gone, generating stops and says which choice to remove. The setup sheet says how many corners were chosen one by one.

In the file

"type": "profile" in the project file. Lengths are in mm, feeds in mm/min, angles in radians.

FieldSetting
nameName
enabledthe tick on the operation’s row in the Browser
partthe part it belongs to
selectthe geometry: {"layer": "NAME"} or {"shapes": [ids]}
toolTool: the project tool’s id
start_depthStart depth
end_depthEnd depth
sideSide
directionDirection
clearanceOversize
step_downStep down
feedFeed
plunge_feedPlunge feed
ramp_angleRamp angle, radians
tabsTabs: count, width, height, flank_angle, and at (tabs placed by hand, [[x, y], …]; left out when there are none), or null
startsstart points, [[x, y], …] in drawing coordinates
finishFinishing pass: {"stock": …} or null
skinOnion skin: {"thickness": …} or null
cornersCorners: {"type": "dogbone" or "tbone", "max_angle": …}, or null
corner_choicescorners with their own choice, each {"at": [x, y], "bone": "none" or "dogbone" or "tbone", "along": "longer" or "shorter" (T-bones only, else null)}; left out when there are none

Pocket

A pocket clears everything inside closed contours, down to the end depth, leaving islands standing. Select the contours, then Pocket (ribbon, OPERATION).

What gets cleared

  • Each selected outline is its own pocket. Two that cross are cleared separately and never merged: the second cuts air where they overlap.
  • Islands: a selected contour wholly inside a pocket is left standing. A contour inside an island is a pocket again.
  • Not selected, not counted: contours that aren’t selected don’t matter, even inside a pocket. A circle you’ll drill later doesn’t become an island.
  • Same shape twice: two selected contours that are the same shape are refused, since one can’t be both outline and island.

Rings

The pocket is cleared in rings:

  • Order: rings are offsets of the walls, cut from the centre outwards, with the wall ring last at each depth. Round islands, each island’s rings are cut one after another, out to the island, before the next island’s, so the tool doesn’t cross the pocket between rings. Where an island is close to the wall (less than a tool diameter), its rings and the wall’s take turns there, so no ring meets more than a stepover of material.
  • Stepover: the distance between rings, as a fraction of the tool diameter (0.4 by default).
  • Full-width rings: the first ring at each depth (and the first of each branch, where the pocket parts in two) has no ring inside it, so the bit cuts its whole width, as in a slot. The others take only the stepover. So the full-width rings have their own Slot feed and Slot step down: at each depth, they go down in slot steps first, and then the other rings cut the whole step down at once. For example, with a step down of 5 mm and a slot step down of 2.5 mm, the first ring is cut at 2.5 and then at 5 mm, and the rings out to the wall at 5 mm. Left empty, they are the feed and step down: every ring cuts the same, as before. They usually come from the tool’s cutting data.
  • Rounded inner rings: every ring but the wall ring has rounded corners (up to two stepovers in radius), for smoother, faster motion. Where rounding would leave material, the corners are rounded less.
  • Spiral: each ring steps out onto the next over its last stretch instead of closing, so the rings of a lobe are one continuous spiral with no stop and no sideways move into material. Round an island, its rings spiral out towards it the same way. The stepped-out stretch is checked against the walls and islands like a ring; where it wouldn’t fit (against a sharp corner, or onto a ring with a lead or a chosen start) the ring closes and is linked.
  • Links: where a ring closes, the tool feeds across to the next at depth when the move stays clear of the walls and is at most one tool diameter, which it checks; otherwise it retracts. There is nothing to set: a checked link is always better than a retract.
  • Between depth passes the tool stays down too: it lifts 0.5 mm off the floor it just cut, feeds back to where the next pass starts, and goes down from there, which saves the trip to safe Z in a deep pocket or hole. It does so only when that pass left no uncut material and the move back keeps clear of the walls and islands; otherwise it retracts. After the last pass it always retracts.

What a round tool leaves is normal: inside corners keep the tool’s radius, and slots narrower than the tool stay uncut. The simulation shows it. Chipload also warns about two things:

  • Uncut material: the rings don’t pass close enough to some material (a stepover too large for the shape). Lower the stepover.
  • A narrow part: the tool doesn’t fit through a narrow part of the pocket. The pocket is cleared as separate areas, and material stays in between; a rest pass with a smaller tool can clear it.

Entry

  • Plunge: straight down at the innermost ring. It needs a centre-cutting endmill.
  • Ramp: down along the first ring, out and back, never steeper than the ramp angle.
  • Helix: down round a small circle at the pocket’s widest point, as helical G2/G3.
    • The radius is a quarter of the tool diameter by default, and must be less than the tool radius. Where there’s no room, it shrinks.
    • With no room for even a tenth of the tool’s diameter, the pocket ramps in, with a note.
    • New pockets use a helix unless the tool is marked centre-cutting.
    • When the pocket’s tool changes to a smaller one, a helix too wide for it becomes a quarter of its diameter.

Start point and lead

As on profiles: drag the start marker of each wall ring, and add a lead from the cleared side onto the wall.

Dogbones

Corners puts dogbones or T-bones into the pocket wall’s inside corners, as on profiles, and any corner can have its own choice (corner by corner). Not with rest machining.

Finishing pass

Finishing pass roughs the pocket with a little material left, then takes it off in light cuts with the same tool: cleaner walls without a step mark at every depth, a cleaner floor, and a true size for a fit (an inlay, a lid, a recess for a hinge or a magnet).

  • Wall allowance (0.3 mm when ticked): left on every wall and island by the roughing. Less than the tool radius.
  • Floor allowance (0.2 mm when ticked): the roughing stops this far above the end depth. At most the step down; 0 on a pocket cut through. Either can be 0, not both.
  • For each area of the pocket, in turn: the roughing, then one level pass over the floor at the end depth (the roughing’s rings once more), then one lap of each wall at full depth. The lap goes down in the roughed area and onto the wall along the lead, or straight across the allowance, and leaves the same way.
  • Dogbones are cut by the roughing, the allowance short, and the lap skims them.
  • Where the pocket is too narrow to rough with the allowance (a gap between an island and a wall only a little wider than the tool), the lap would cut it full width at full depth, so the pocket is refused with the place: use a smaller allowance or a smaller tool.
  • Not on a rest pocket, which is a light clean-up already. Rest passes still come after a finished pocket and clear what it leaves.
  • On the command line: chipload set part.chipload 'operations.0.finish={"wall":0.3,"floor":0.2}', or null for none. The setup sheet says “finishing: walls 0.3 mm, floor 0.2 mm”.

Rest machining

A pocket can have rest passes: smaller tools that clear only what its tool left, shown under it in the Browser. See Rest machining.

In the file

"type": "pocket" in the project file. Lengths are in mm, feeds in mm/min, angles in radians.

FieldSetting
nameName
enabledthe tick on the operation’s row in the Browser
partthe part it belongs to
selectthe geometry: {"layer": "NAME"} or {"shapes": [ids]}
toolTool: the project tool’s id
start_depthStart depth
end_depthEnd depth
directionDirection
clearanceOversize
step_downStep down
stepoverStepover, a fraction of the tool diameter
slot_feedSlot feed: the full-width rings’ feed; null: feed
slot_step_downSlot step down: how deep each step of a full-width ring goes; null: step_down
feedFeed
plunge_feedPlunge feed
entryEntry: plunge, ramp, or {"type": "helix", "radius": …, "ramp_angle": …}
startsstart points
leadLead
cornersCorners (dogbones, T-bones)
corner_choicescorners with their own choice, as on profiles; left out when there are none
restRest after: {"after_tool": id} or null
finishFinishing pass: {"wall": …, "floor": …}, the allowances; left out when there is none
rest_passesIts rest passes, each {tool, feed, plunge_feed, step_down, stepover, slot_feed, slot_step_down, entry, enabled}; left out when there are none

Projects from before Chipload 0.14 that used the removed trochoidal or adaptive clearing are cut as ring pockets. Projects from before 0.18 that had the tool retracting between rings or sharp inner rings now get links and rounded rings: the same rings, cut faster.

Rest machining

A large tool clears a pocket fast, but leaves material where it doesn’t fit:

  • inside corners rounded to its radius;
  • slots and gaps narrower than the tool.

A rest pass with a smaller tool cuts only that material, instead of clearing the whole pocket again. (Design: docs/specs/rest-passes.md.)

Adding one

  • Right-click the pocket in the Browser, then Add rest pass ▸, and pick a smaller endmill: the project’s tools come first, then those in your tool library. A library tool is copied into the project’s Tools. When the entry is greyed out, point at it to see why: no smaller endmill in the project or your library, or the pocket has dogbone corners. Command line: chipload op add-rest part.chipload --op 1 --tool 2 (copy a library tool in first with chipload tool use).
  • It belongs to the pocket: it shows as a leaf under it, numbered after it (1.1, 1.2), and takes the pocket’s shapes, depths, oversize and direction. Change the pocket and the pass follows; turn the pocket off and its passes aren’t cut either.
  • Its own: the tool, feed, plunge feed, step down, stepover, entry, and on/off. Click the pass to change them; its right-click menu removes it.
  • Feeds and step down come from your tool library’s values for the small tool when it has them. Otherwise they’re copied from the pocket: check them.
  • Several passes: a pocket can have more than one, each with a smaller tool than the one before (12, then 6, then 3 mm). Each clears what the one before it left.

Command line: chipload op rest-pass part.chipload --op 1 lists a pocket’s passes, and with --pass 1 changes one (--tool, --feed, --plunge-feed, --step-down, --stepover, --enabled false) or removes it (--remove).

When it is cut

A rest pass waits for the pockets next to its pocket that use the same tool. After the last of them come all their rest passes, the larger tools first, then the operations after them. So with two pockets and an outline, all cut with a 12 mm tool, and a 4 mm rest pass on each pocket:

CutTool
Pocket 1, Pocket 212 mm
Rest passes 1.1, 2.14 mm
Outline 312 mm

The small tool comes in once, and the outline, which frees the part, stays last. The report, the G-code headers and the setup sheet show the passes where they are cut.

What it cuts

  • Worked out from the tool’s size: Chipload works out what a tool of the larger tool’s size can’t reach in the pocket’s shapes. It looks at the size, not at what the pocket’s toolpath did.
  • Rings: around each piece of that material, the small tool clears a small pocket with rings, within its own diameter. Along the real walls it follows the walls, so the corners come out with the small tool’s radius.
  • Pieces too small for the small tool too are skipped, with a note giving their size.
  • Nothing left: if the large tool reaches the whole pocket (a circle wider than it), the pass cuts nothing and says so.

Simulate (F6) shows the result: the corners cut by the small tool.

Rules

  • A smaller endmill: a pass’s tool must be an endmill smaller than the tool before it.
  • No dogbones: a pocket with dogbones can’t have rest passes; its corners need no rest.

A rest pocket on its own

When the large pocket is cut elsewhere, in another project or another program, a pocket can rest after a tool by itself: Clearing > Rest after in its properties (command line: chipload op rest part.chipload --op 2 --after-tool 1). It is an ordinary operation in the list, cut where it stands. Chipload warns when no earlier, enabled pocket cuts the same shapes with that tool at least as deep; it is still safe, it just leaves the bulk of the material.

A rest pocket that copies an earlier pocket of the project (same shapes, depths, oversize and direction, after its tool), as Add rest pocket made them before rest passes, can become that pocket’s rest pass: right-click it, Make it a rest pass of operation N (command line: chipload op make-rest-pass part.chipload --op 2). It is then cut after the run of pockets it belongs to.

V-carve

A V-carve engraves closed shapes, such as lettering (converted to outlines in CAD), logos and inlay outlines, with a V-bit:

  • the bit follows each shape’s centre line;
