  • it goes as deep as makes the cut exactly as wide as the shape at the surface.

Narrow strokes come out shallow and wide ones deep, and sharp outside corners such as serif tips stay sharp. Select the closed contours, then V-carve (ribbon, OPERATION). A logo or sketch that is only a picture can be traced into shapes first.

The V-bit

The tool must be a V-bit with its angle set: the included angle, e.g. 60° or 90°. Tip is the flat tip’s diameter, 0 for a pointed bit. Chipload never guesses the angle; a V-bit without one is refused.

Settings

Setting
Start depthwhere the carved surface is: 0 for the stock top, more to carve into a floor an earlier pocket cut
End depththe deepest the tip may go: a limit, not the depth cut. Strokes that need more get walls (see Wide strokes). The bit’s own size is a limit too: it can’t go deeper than its cutting edge reaches. A new V-carve starts at the bit’s full cone depth, at most half the stock, so letters carve fully; a deeper limit than the strokes need does no harm, since only the depth actually cut is checked against the stock
Depth passeshow the depth is split into passes: Equal load or Step down (see Passes)
Passeswith Equal load: how many passes to the end depth, 1 for one. Each removes as much as the others, so they get shallower on the way down. Their depths are shown under the field
Step downwith Step down: the deepest each pass goes. The passes are equal, the last at the end depth, as in a pocket. Their depths are shown under the field
Ignore kinkscorners where the outline bends less than this angle (20° for a new V-carve) get no branch of the centre line, so the bit doesn’t rise into every kink of a curve (see Smooth carving); 0° carves every corner. In the project file: ignore_kinks_deg
Feed, Plunge feedmoves steeper than 45° use the plunge feed

Regions work as in pockets: an outline minus the selected contours inside it (the inside of an O stays standing). Open contours are skipped.

Passes

A V groove gets wider as it gets deeper: twice as deep removes four times as much. Passes of the same depth get heavier and heavier, the last one the hardest on the bit and the machine. Depth passes chooses how the depth is split:

  • Equal load (a new V-carve’s choice): you say in how many passes, and each pass removes the same amount, so the steps get smaller on the way down. 5 mm in 6 passes goes to 2.04, 2.89, 3.54, 4.08, 4.56 and 5 mm; 3 mm in 3 passes to 1.73, 2.45 and 3 mm. More passes are lighter on the bit and take longer.
  • Step down: you say how deep each pass may go, as for a pocket, and the passes are equal steps. 5 mm with a 2 mm step down goes to 1.67, 3.33 and 5 mm. Each pass removes more than the one before (with three, 1 : 3 : 5), so pick a step the last pass can take.

Either way, the passes share the depth the carve actually goes to: with the end depth at 10 mm and strokes that need 4.4 mm, 3 passes go to 2.54, 3.59 and 4.4 mm, and the Report says so. The depths under the field are counted from the end depth, so they are what you get when the carve goes that deep. Within one carve, a stroke shallower than the deepest is done in fewer passes: the deeper ones find nothing left to cut there. Switching from one to the other keeps the count of passes, for when you switch back.

The carving is done area by area: each letter (or each closed part of a drawing, such as the dot of an i) gets all its passes, and its walls, before the bit moves to the nearest next one. A loop of the centre line (an O, the bowl of an a) has no corner to go in at, so the bit goes in where the stroke is narrowest, the shallowest point of the loop.

Within a letter the bit lifts as little as it can. The short branches the centre line runs into sharp corners (shorter than 5 mm) are cut out and back as the bit passes them, instead of each with a trip up to safe Z. In the passes after the first, where two pieces to cut lie at most 10 mm apart along the centre line, the bit goes from one to the next through the groove already cut instead of lifting. The carving is the same; there are fewer lifts (in a script font about half).

Smooth carving

A curve in a drawing or a font is made of short pieces, and each joint is a small kink. The centre line runs a branch out to every corner, so the bit would rise into each kink and come down again, and the depth would wobble by hundredths of a millimetre along the stroke. Both make the Z axis stop and start, the slowest axis on most routers.

  • The depth is smoothed along each cut: wobbles smaller than the V-carve’s tolerance (0.005 mm at the edge of the cut; 0.009 mm in depth for a 60° bit) are taken out, never deeper than the exact cut, so nothing is carved wider than drawn.
  • Ignore kinks (20° for a new V-carve) leaves out the branches into corners where the outline bends less than that angle. A kink bending θ in a stroke whose centre is r from its edges keeps a sliver of at most r · (1/cos(θ/2) − 1): at 20°, 0.05 mm in a stroke 6 mm wide; at 60°, 0.46 mm. A real corner, such as the 90° corner of a block letter, keeps its branch at any setting up to 90°. Try a few values and look at the toolpath and the simulation: the change shows at once.
  • A traced picture has its pixel steps in every curve: kinks of 20° to 30°. On a traced ring, 30° took the bit’s turns up and down from 48 to none and the time by a tenth.
  • Only kinks go. A branch is left out only when the rest of the carving covers what it would cut, but for the sliver above. The centre line of a whole stroke that happens to end in a kink (the rounded, traced end of a leg of an H, the tapered end of a script stroke) stays at any angle.

Projects from before Chipload 0.31 open with Ignore kinks at 0°, so they carve every corner as before; their depth is smoothed.

Projects from before Chipload 0.29 had a step down instead. They open with as many passes as that step down took to reach the end depth (3.5 mm with a 1.6 mm step down: 3 passes), each now no heavier than the old last one.

Wide strokes

Where a stroke is wider than the bit can carve within the end depth, the bit goes down to the end depth and follows the outline, just far enough in for its cone to touch the drawn edge at the surface. The walls come out as drawn, sharp, at the bit’s angle; narrow parts of the same shape are still carved along their centre line, and corners are carved up into their tips.

Without more, the floor between the walls is left standing at the surface: an outline carving. The report says so in a note, once for the operation, listing the shapes it concerns. It isn’t a warning, since an outline carving can be just what you want.

The floor pass

To clear the floor flat, give the V-carve a floor pass: an end mill that clears the floor at the end depth, kept clear of the sloped walls. It belongs to the V-carve, as a rest pass belongs to its pocket: it takes the V-carve’s shapes and depths, has its own tool, feeds, step down and stepover, and is cut first, so the end mill takes the bulk and the V-bit only cuts the walls. Where the end mill can’t reach (corners of the floor, parts narrower than it), the V-bit cleans up along the centre line at the end depth.

In the window, right-click the V-carve in the Browser: Add floor pass ▸ lists the project’s end mills, then your library’s. The floor pass is a leaf under the V-carve; click it for its settings.

If the end mill is too wide for part of the floor, the plan says how much it clears and the largest end mill that would clear it all, and names the end mills in your library that do (in the floor pass’s settings too, under its tool):

note: Operation 1 (Logo): contours #2, #4: with the 6 mm end mill, 85 % of the floor is cleared; one of at most 0.9 mm would clear all of it
Operation 1: end mills in your library that clear the floor: 0.8 mm flat (0.8 mm)

Specks lower than 0.2 mm, or less than 1 % of a shape’s floor, don’t count: no round end mill reaches into a sharp corner. The advice is information only; the job runs either way.

chipload op add-floor sign.chipload --op 1 --tool 2   # the floor pass with project tool 2
chipload op remove-floor sign.chipload --op 1

Inlays

An inlay sets one wood into another: a dark word flush in a light board, without paint. The artwork is V-carved twice, with the same V-bit:

  • a pocket in the base board, an ordinary V-carve;
  • a plug in a second board: the rest of that board is carved away, so the artwork stands up as a tapered plug, mirrored. It is glued face down into the pocket, and when the glue has set, its back is sawn or planed off down to the base’s surface.

The pocket’s walls and the plug’s walls slope at the bit’s angle, so they wedge together and centre themselves, sharp inside corners included. No dowels or corners are needed to line them up.

          plug board's back, g above the base
        ┌───────────────────────────┐
        └──────┐             ┌──────┘   ← the base's surface
   ────────────\    plug     /──────────
                \___________/            ← the plug's face, D deep
                 glue space               ← the pocket's floor, P deep
   ────────────────────────────────────

Four numbers set it up:

NumberWhat it doesDefault
Pocket depth Phow deep the pocket is5 mm
Plug start depth Dthe plug’s outline is taken this deep in its board: its face lies D deep in the pocket, leaving P − D under it for glue3 mm
Glue gap gthe plug board is carved g deeper than D, so its back stands g above the base and squeezed-out glue has room1.5 mm
Plug board thicknesswhat is left under the plug’s channel holds it together until it is planed off10 mm
 
chipload op add-inlay sign.chipload --text 1 --tool 3 --floor-tool 2   # also --layer, --shapes, --group
chipload op add-inlay sign.chipload --layer WORD --tool 3 --depth 5 --plug-start 3 --gap 1.5 --plug-thickness 10

In the app, select the artwork and click Inlay… (ribbon, WIZARDS), or Wizards > New inlay…. The dialog asks for the V-bit, the four numbers and the floor end mill, with the project’s first V-bit and smallest end mill chosen, and draws the two cuts with the numbers marked. Both tool lists also offer your tool library’s tools under From your library: (marked +); one chosen is copied into the project when you click Make inlay, not before. A project without a V-bit starts on the library’s. Make inlay makes the pair as one undo step. On the command line, op add-inlay does the same.

Both make the pocket in the active part, and a new setup, Inlay plug board, whose board fits the artwork with a 10 mm margin, holding a part of its own, mirrored left to right, with the plug. With a floor end mill, both get a floor pass. Give the plug one: its channel is mostly floor. Afterwards both are ordinary V-carves: change their depths, feeds and passes as for any other. The plug’s properties have a Plug section with its start depth and glue gap; changing the gap moves its end depth. Both select the same shapes, so editing the artwork changes both. The plug’s setup sheet says it is mirrored and lists the steps below.

The plug’s checks warn when:

  • no V-carve of the same shapes with a bit of the same angle is in another setup;
  • the pocket isn’t deeper than D (the plug would sit on the pocket’s floor);
  • the plug’s part isn’t mirrored;
  • the glue gap is under 0.5 mm, or less than 2 mm of board is left under the channel;
  • the pocket ignores kinks;
  • there is no floor pass;
  • a floor pass’s end mill doesn’t reach all of the floor, and what it leaves stands more than 0.2 mm above the plug’s face (in the pocket) or above the outline level (in the plug’s channel). A smaller end mill for the floor pass clears it. The pocket gets this warning too.

A flat-tipped V-bit is fine. A note says how far from a sharp corner plug and pocket fall short of meeting: 0.14 mm for a 0.2 mm tip at a right angle, which the glue and the clamp take up. Refused:

  • a plug deeper than its V-bit cuts with its cone;
  • a plug that ignores kinks;
  • instances of the plug’s part (the plug carves its whole board);
  • a floor end mill wider than the V-bit (it would cut off the board’s edges).

Steps:

  1. Cut the pocket in the base board.
  2. Clamp the plug board on the machine (setup 2) and cut the plug.
  3. Glue it face down into the pocket and clamp or press until it seats on its walls.
  4. Let the glue set. Saw or plane the plug board’s back off down to the base’s surface.

Limits

  • A flat tip: it can’t reach into the last bit of a sharp corner, and strokes narrower than the tip are left out, with a warning.
  • Text: text from the DXF must be converted to outlines (curves) in CAD first. Chipload doesn’t read TEXT entities or fonts.

Every V-carve move is checked so the cone never reaches past the outline at the surface.

In the file

"type": "vcarve" in the project file. Lengths are in mm, feeds in mm/min, angles in radians.

FieldSetting
nameName
enabledthe tick on the operation’s row in the Browser
partthe part it belongs to
selectthe geometry: {"layer": "NAME"} or {"shapes": [ids]}
toolTool: the project tool’s id
start_depthStart depth
end_depthEnd depth
passesPasses: how many, 1 to 100, for equal load
step_downStep down, mm: left out for equal load. At most 100 passes: at least a hundredth of the depth
feedFeed
plunge_feedPlunge feed
floorthe floor pass, left out when there is none: tool (an end mill), feed, plunge_feed, step_down, stepover (a fraction of the end mill’s diameter), entry and enabled
plugan inlay’s plug, left out for an ordinary V-carve: start_depth, where its outline is taken (D). Its end depth is D plus the glue gap

Follow path

Follow path puts the tool’s centre exactly on the selected lines, open or closed, down to the end depth in passes. Use it for engraving, V-grooves, score and fold lines, or a single cut through thin material.

Select the shapes, then Follow (ribbon, OPERATION). A picture of handwriting or line art can be traced into centre lines for it.

  • Open lines are cut back and forth, pass by pass.
  • Closed shapes go round, as a profile on the line does.
  • Order: the paths are cut nearest first.

It cuts only what it selects. Profiles and pockets keep skipping open lines, so a centre line on a cutting layer is never cut by them. A follow path that selects a whole layer does cut that layer’s centre lines.

Setting
Start depth, End depth, Step downas on every operation
Groove widthwith a V-bit only: how wide the groove is at the surface. Change it and the end depth is set to give that width (a border of a sign is set this way). Greyed when the path cuts through.
Entryplunge, or ramp along the path. A pointed V-bit counts as centre-cutting. A tool marked not centre-cutting ramps only along a path at least its diameter long.
Feed, Plunge feedmm/min

There is no side, oversize, tabs, lead or start point: the tool is on the line.

With a V-bit, the groove’s width at the top follows from the depth and the bit’s angle; the report and the setup sheet give it. A V-bit goes no deeper than its cone (12.7 mm at 60° with a 0.2 mm tip: 10.8 mm deep): deeper, its shank would rub, so such a path is refused. Use an end mill for a deeper slot.

In the file

"type": "path" in the project file. Lengths are in mm, feeds in mm/min, angles in radians.

selectthe geometry: {"layer": "NAME"} or {"shapes": [ids]}

start_depthStart depth
end_depthEnd depth
step_downStep down

Chamfer

Chamfer breaks the top edge of closed shapes with a V-bit: the edge of a box side, a tray, a shelf or a sign, or the rim of a hole or a pocket. You give the width you want at the surface; the depth follows from the bit’s angle.

Select the shapes, then Chamfer (ribbon, OPERATION, the small button under Styles), or press S over the canvas, then C.

How it cuts

The bit’s flank runs on the line and its tip stays in the waste:

  • the tool’s centre runs half the bit’s flat tip outside the line, on the waste side (on the line itself with a pointed bit);
  • it goes down as deep as the width over the tangent of half the bit’s angle: with a 90° bit, as deep as the width (a 45° chamfer); with a 60° bit, 1.73 × the width (a steeper one).

So the chamfer comes out as wide as you typed, and a flat-tipped bit leaves no step on the part. The other flank grooves the waste, which the cut-out or the pocket takes away.

  • The part’s outside corners are turned round as a cone, as a chamfer bit would.
  • Its inside corners meet in a mitre.
  • Open shapes are skipped, with a note: use a follow path for them.

Order: a new chamfer goes at the end of its part, like any new operation, after the cut-out. That works: the tabs or the onion skin hold the part, the cut-out’s slot is on the waste side, and a chamfer stays far above the tabs. Drag it before the cut-out if you’d rather have the part held by the board while it is chamfered; with the V-bit between two end mill operations, that costs a tool change.

Settings

Setting
Toola V-bit with its angle; any other tool is refused
Sidewhere the waste is: outside the shape (a part’s outline) or inside it (a hole’s or a pocket’s rim). A new chamfer takes it from the shapes, as a new profile does: inside for holes, outside for outlines, two chamfers when both are selected
Widthat the surface, from the line into the part; 1 mm for a new one. At most half the bit’s cone (its diameter less its tip, halved), or the shank would rub: 6.25 mm with a 12.7 mm bit with a 0.2 mm tip
Depthshown beneath: “2 mm with the 90° bit”
Start depthwhere the edge is: 0 for the stock top, more for the edge of a step an earlier operation cut
Step downoff: one pass. On: equal passes no deeper than this, the last at the full depth
Directionclimb or conventional
Feed, Plunge feedmm/min; from the V-bit’s cutting data
Entryplunge (a V-bit goes down on its point), or ramp along the path

The setup sheet says, for example, “Chamfer 2 mm wide, outside, climb”, with its depths.

New sign… adds a chamfer for its edge.

Command line

chipload op add part.chipload chamfer --shapes 1,2 --width 2
chipload op add part.chipload chamfer --layer OUTLINE --side outside --tool 3
chipload set part.chipload operations.4.width=3

--width is the width at the surface (1 mm if left out); --side sets the waste side in place of the one the shapes tell. A chamfer takes no --depth or --through.

In the file

"type": "chamfer" in the project file. Lengths are in mm, feeds in mm/min. A project with a chamfer opens in Chipload 0.37.0 and later.

enabledthe tick on the operation’s row in the Browser

selectthe geometry: {"layer": "NAME"} or {"shapes": [ids]}

Flute

Flute cuts a decorative groove along open shapes that dives in gradually at its start, runs at full depth, and comes up gradually at its end. Flutes are cut down the face of columns, table legs and pilasters, across panels, as the rays of a sunburst, or along a frame. With a ball nose or a V-bit, each end narrows to a point.

Draw the flutes as lines, arcs or open polylines, select them, then Flute (ribbon, OPERATION, the small button beside Chamfer), or press S over the canvas, then L.

How it cuts

The tool’s centre follows each shape on the line, as a follow path does. Along the shape, the depth goes:

  1. from the start depth at the shape’s first end,
  2. down over the start ramp to the depth,
  3. at the depth through the middle,
  4. up over the end ramp to the start depth at the shape’s last end.

A ramp of 0 makes a square end: the flute is at full depth right at that end. Use it for a flute that runs off the edge of the board.

  • First and last end are the ends the shape was drawn from and to. Most flutes have the same ramp at both ends, so this rarely matters. A shape drawn the other way round swaps its ramps.
  • Ramps longer than the shape (both together): both are shortened in the same proportion, so the flute reaches its depth at one point only. A note names the shape.
  • Closed shapes are skipped with a note. A closed groove is a follow path.

Passes: deeper than the step down, the flute is cut in equal passes. Each pass follows the same ramps, cut off at its own depth, so the ramps keep their slope and the tool never has more than one step of material under it. The tool goes back and forth along the shape, with no retract between passes; at a square end it goes straight down one step at the plunge feed.

Settings

Setting
Toola ball nose (a round-bottomed flute), a V-bit (a V-groove, no deeper than its cone) or an end mill (a flat-bottomed groove). A drill is refused
Start depthwhere the flute’s ends are: 0 for the stock top, more for a flute in the floor of a pocket
Depththrough the middle. The width at the top is shown beneath: “6 mm wide with the 6 mm ball nose”
Step downoff: one pass. On: equal passes no deeper than this, the last at the depth
Start ramp, End ramphow far along the shape, from each end, the flute reaches its depth, mm; 20 mm for a new flute. 0: a square end
Rampstraight: an even taper. Curved: steepest at the surface, easing into the floor, so no line shows where the ramp meets the flat
Feed, Plunge feedmm/min. The feed is used along the whole shape, ramps included; the plunge feed only going straight down

A new flute takes the project’s first ball nose (else its first V-bit), as deep as the ball’s radius for a half-round flute (3 mm with another tool, no deeper than a V-bit’s cone).

The report says how wide the flute is at the top: “the flute is 6 mm wide at the top”. The setup sheet says, for example, “Flute, ramps 20 / 20 mm, straight ramps”, with its depths.

Command line

chipload op add part.chipload flute --shapes 4,5,6 --depth 4 --ramp 20
chipload op add part.chipload flute --layer FLUTES --ramp-start 0 --ramp-end 30 --ramp-shape curved
chipload set part.chipload operations.2.ramp_end=0

--ramp sets both ends; --ramp-start and --ramp-end one each; --ramp-shape is straight or curved. A flute takes no --through.

In the file

"type": "flute" in the project file. Lengths are in mm, feeds in mm/min. A project with a flute opens in Chipload 0.37.0 and later.

Pattern

Pattern engraves a texture of grooves inside closed shapes: lines, a cross-hatch, rings or waves. Use it for the background of a sign behind raised letters, a door panel, a box lid or a coaster. The grooves stop at the shape’s edge and leave its holes alone.

Select the shapes, with the shapes inside them that should stay untouched (the letters of a sign), then Pattern (ribbon, OPERATION, the small button under Flute), or press S over the canvas, then E.

What it fills

The shapes are taken as a pocket takes them: a selected shape is filled, and the selected shapes inside it are holes. Shapes inside that aren’t selected don’t count. Open shapes are skipped with a note.

  • Inside the edge (on for a new pattern): the grooves stop half a groove’s width in from the line, so none cuts past the shape or into a hole. Off: they run up to the line, centred on it; use it when the edge is cut away or covered later.
  • Pieces shorter than a groove’s width are left out.

Patterns

PatternSettingsGrooves
LinesAngle, Spacingparallel lines
Cross-hatchAngle, Spacing, Angle betweentwo sets of lines: 90° between gives squares, 60° diamonds
RingsSpacingthe outline repeated inwards, and round the holes
WavesAngle, Spacing, Wavelength, Heightparallel waves; the height is from top to bottom

Patterns of short strokes, for a fluted texture: each stroke is a groove of its own, so with ramped ends (see Ends below) every stroke tapers to a point at both ends, as a flute does.

PatternSettingsStrokes
DashesAngle, Spacing, Stroke length, Gaprows of strokes with gaps between, every other row shifted half a stroke and gap, as bricks
HerringboneAngle, Spacing, Stroke lengthcolumns of strokes at 45°, the next column mirrored, meeting in chevrons
ScalesAngle, Spacing, Radiusrows of half circles side by side, every other row shifted by a radius
SunburstSpacing, Stroke length, Pitchround the middle of each shape: rings of strokes pointing outwards, pitch apart round the ring, every other ring turned half a pitch

  • Whole strokes only (on for a new stroke pattern): a stroke the shape’s edge or a hole would cut is left out, so every stroke keeps both tapered ends. Off: strokes are clipped at the edge, as lines are.

  • Choosing a stroke pattern fills in sizes from the groove’s width (strokes three widths long, at least 4 mm) and, if the ends were square, ramps of a third of a stroke.

  • Spacing is from one groove’s centre to the next. Narrower than a groove, the grooves overlap (a note says so) and the area comes out roughened.

  • Angle is the lines’ direction from the X axis, counter-clockwise.

  • Lines and waves are laid out from the drawing’s origin, so a pattern carries on unbroken from one shape to the next.

How it cuts

Every groove is cut at one depth, in passes if a step down is set. Each groove is cut like a flute: back and forth through its passes without lifting, then the tool retracts and moves to the next groove. Rings go down where they start, as a closed follow path.

Ends: square, the tool going straight down; or ramped in and out over a length, as a flute’s, so with a V-bit each groove ends in a point and the texture looks softer.

A pattern with more than 50 000 grooves is refused: widen the spacing.

Settings

Setting
Toola V-bit (the usual, no deeper than its cone), a ball nose or an end mill. A drill is refused
PatternLines, Cross-hatch, Rings or Waves, and their settings above
Inside the edgegrooves inside the line (on) or up to it (off)
Start depth0 for the stock top, more for a pattern in the floor of a pocket
Depththe grooves’ depth; each groove’s width at the top is shown beneath
Step downoff: one pass. On: equal passes no deeper than this
Ends0 for square ends, or the length each groove ramps in and out
Feed, Plunge feedmm/min

A new pattern takes the project’s first V-bit, 0.5 mm deep (no deeper than its cone), lines at 45°, the spacing twice the groove’s width (at least 1 mm), inside the edge, with square ends.

The setup sheet says, for example, “Pattern, cross-hatch 4 mm apart at 45°, 90° between, inside the edge”.

Command line

chipload op add part.chipload pattern --shapes 1,2,3 --pattern cross --spacing 4 --angle 45
chipload op add part.chipload pattern --layer PANEL --pattern waves --spacing 5 --wavelength 25 --height 4 --ramp 3
chipload op add part.chipload pattern --shapes 4 --pattern rings --spacing 3 --on-line
chipload set part.chipload operations.0.pattern.spacing=5

--between sets a cross-hatch’s angle between (90 if left out); --wavelength and --height a wave’s (4 spacings and one spacing if left out); --ramp the ends; --on-line turns off Inside the edge. A pattern takes no --through.

Stroke patterns: --pattern dashes|herringbone|scales|sunburst, with --length, --gap, --radius and --pitch; --clip turns off Whole strokes only. Without --ramp they are fluted:

chipload op add part.chipload pattern --shapes 1,2,3 --pattern dashes --length 6 --gap 3 --angle 30
chipload op add part.chipload pattern --shapes 4 --pattern sunburst --pitch 5

In the file

Prism

Prism leaves closed shapes standing as roofs: their sides slope up from the edges to a sharp ridge along the centre line, as hand-chiselled raised letters. It is the opposite of a V-carve, which sinks the shape into the board, and it uses the same drawing and the same V-bit.

Select the shapes, with the shapes inside them that are holes (an O’s counter), then press S over the canvas and R, or choose Operation > New Prism, or New operation in a part’s right-click menu in the Browser.

How it cuts

The V-bit’s tip runs once round each outline, going as deep as the shape is wide there: the radius of the largest circle inside the shape that touches the outline at that point, over the tangent of half the bit’s angle. The bit’s inner flank then reaches the surface exactly on the centre line, so one lap round the outline cuts the whole roof.

  • A stroke 10 mm wide comes out as a ridge 5 mm high with a 90° bit, 8.7 mm with a 60° bit.
  • At a sharp outside corner the edge comes up to the surface, so corners end in points, as a chiselled serif does. Where strokes meet, the junction rises to a peak.
  • With a flat-tipped bit, the tip runs half its width outside the line, so the edge has no step.
  • Each lap starts at its shallowest point, at the surface where the shape has a corner.

Wider than the bit: where a shape is wider than the bit’s cone can bring to a ridge, its edges go as deep as the cone allows and the middle stays flat, at the surface. A note says which shape. Max depth does the same on purpose: bevelled raised shapes with a flat top, every edge no deeper than you set.

Round the shapes: the bit’s other flank cuts outwards, leaving a V-shaped trench round each shape as deep as its edge. Leave it for a carved-in look, or clear the background first with a pocket as deep as the deepest edge (the report says how deep) for shapes standing on a flat background. Where shapes stand so close that one’s trench reaches the next, that shape’s lower edge is cut into: a note names the two shapes and how much deeper the edge goes there; space them further apart, or set a max depth.

Open shapes are skipped with a note.

Settings

Setting
Toola V-bit with its angle; any other tool is refused
Start depthwhere the surface is: 0 for the stock top, more for a prism in the floor of a pocket
Max depthoff: as deep as the shapes and the bit’s cone need. On: no deeper than this, for bevelled shapes with a flat top. Beneath it, how deep the edges can go with the bit
Step downoff: one lap. On: equal passes, each the same lap no deeper than its level
Directionclimb or conventional
Feed, Plunge feedmm/min

A new prism takes the project’s first V-bit, no max depth, the tool’s step down, climb.

The report gives the deepest edge (“the deepest edge is 6 mm”). The setup sheet says “Prism, climb”, with the depths “0 to at most 6.35” (the bit’s cone or the max depth).

Command line

chipload op add part.chipload prism --shapes 1,2,3
chipload op add part.chipload prism --layer LETTERS --max-depth 3
chipload set part.chipload operations.0.max_depth=2.5

--max-depth gives a flat top; a prism takes no --depth or --through, as its depth follows from the shapes.

In the file

"type": "prism" in the project file. Lengths are in mm, feeds in mm/min. A project with a prism opens in Chipload 0.37.0 and later.

FieldSetting
nameName
enabledthe tick on the operation’s row in the Browser
partthe part it belongs to
selectthe geometry: {"layer": "NAME"} or {"shapes": [ids]}
toolTool: the project tool’s id, a V-bit
start_depthStart depth
max_depthMax depth; null for as deep as needed
step_downStep down; null for one pass
directionDirection: climb or conventional

plunge_feedPlunge feed

Moulding

Moulding sweeps a profile along a path: a cove or an ogee round a table top’s face, a bead along a door frame, a round-over beside a raised panel, a decorative band across a lid. You give the path (the line the moulding runs along) and the profile (its cross-section); a ball nose cuts it in passes along the path.

Select the paths (open or closed shapes), then press S over the canvas and M, or choose Operation > New Moulding, or New operation in a part’s right-click menu in the Browser.

The profile

ProfileSettingsCross-section
CoveRadiusa hollow quarter circle: as deep as the radius at the path, at the surface the radius in
Round-overRadiusthe edge rounded: a convex quarter circle
OgeeWidth, Depthan S: a cove into a round-over
BeadRadiusa raised half round, its top at the surface
FluteRadiusa half-round groove
Drawn shapeShapeany open shape you draw, read as the section

A drawn profile is the section as you would draw it on paper: across to the right (away from the path), up the page. Its leftmost point is at the path, its highest point is the surface; where it doubles back, the lowest part counts. Draw it as construction geometry (so it is never cut itself), anywhere on the canvas. Change the shape, and the moulding follows.

The side and the ends

  • Side: outside or inside a closed path, left or right of an open path as it was drawn. A new moulding takes outside for an outline, inside for a hole, left for an open path.
  • Corners: at a corner turning away from the profile, the moulding turns round it; at a corner turning towards it, the two meet in a mitre, as a mitred frame’s corner.
  • Ends of an open path:
    • square: the full profile to the path’s end, for a moulding that runs off the board’s edge;
    • round: the profile turns a quarter round the path’s end point, as a moulding bit stopped and lifted out leaves it;
    • tapered: the moulding fades into the board over a length, straight or curved, as a stopped flute does. Along the taper the tool rises and falls with the section.

How it cuts

For each distance from the path, the tool goes as deep as its own shape allows without cutting below the profile: Chipload drops the tool’s shape onto the section. Each pass is the path’s offset at that distance, cut at that depth, one stepover apart across the profile, so the finish runs along the moulding. With a step down, a deep profile is cut in levels, each pass no deeper than its level. The tool lifts between passes.

Where the profile has a curve tighter than the tool, or a wall the ball can’t reach (the sides of a bead, a vertical step in a drawn profile), the tool bridges it; a note says how far it stays above the profile there. A smaller ball, or an end mill, reaches further.

The first pass runs on the path itself, so with a ball nose half the tool cuts beyond the path at the profile’s depth there: right for a cove, ogee or round-over at a part’s edge, where beyond the path is waste. A profile that starts at the surface (a bead, a flute, a drawn band) cuts nothing beyond the path.

Settings

Setting
Toola ball nose (the usual) or an end mill; a V-bit or a drill is refused
Profilethe shape and its size, or the drawn shape. The list shows each profile’s section beside its name, and a drawing of the chosen one, true to scale, sits under its size: the board with the profile cut into it, the path at the mark, the tool at its deepest pass and a dot where each pass’s tip goes
Side, Endsas above; Taper length and its ramp shape for tapered ends
Start depthwhere the surface is: 0 for the stock top
Stepoverthe distance between passes across the profile; a new moulding takes the widest that leaves ridges of 0.02 mm with a ball nose, a quarter of an end mill
Step downoff: one level. On: equal levels no deeper than this
Directionclimb or conventional
Feed, Plunge feedmm/min

A new moulding takes the project’s first ball nose (else its first end mill), a 6 mm cove, square ends, the tool’s step down, climb. The report gives the profile’s depth and the number of passes. The setup sheet says, for example, “Moulding, cove R 6, outside, stepover 0.6, climb”.

Command line

chipload op add part.chipload moulding --shapes 1 --profile cove --radius 8
chipload op add part.chipload moulding --shapes 2 --profile ogee --width 12 --depth 6 --side right
chipload op add part.chipload moulding --shapes 3 --ends taper --taper 30 --ramp-shape curved
chipload op add part.chipload moulding --shapes 4 --profile-shape 7 --ends round
chipload set part.chipload operations.0.profile.radius=6

--side is outside, inside, left or right; --stepover sets the stepover. A moulding takes no --through.

In the file

"type": "moulding" in the project file. Lengths are in mm, feeds in mm/min. A project with a moulding opens in Chipload 0.37.0 and later.

FieldSetting

enabledthe tick on the operation’s row in the Browser
partthe part it belongs to
selectthe paths: {"layer": "NAME"} or {"shapes": [ids]}
toolTool: the project tool’s id
profileProfile: {"kind": "cove", "radius"}, {"kind": "roundover", "radius"}, {"kind": "ogee", "width", "depth"}, {"kind": "bead", "radius"}, {"kind": "flute", "radius"} or {"kind": "shape", "shape": ID}
sideSide: outside, inside, left or right
endsEnds: {"kind": "square"}, {"kind": "round"} or {"kind": "taper", "length", "shape"} (straight or curved)
start_depthStart depth

step_downStep down; null for one level
