plunge_feedPlunge feed

Holes

Holes cuts every selected circle, either drilled with a drill or bored with a helix with an endmill. Select the circles (a whole layer is fine: other shapes are skipped with a note), then Holes (ribbon, OPERATION). It takes a drill of the project that fits the circles, if there is one, and goes through. Circles drawn in Chipload count, and so do imported circles, including ones drawn as two half arcs.

The tool decides how:

ToolMethodWhich circles
Drilldrilled at the centrethe drill’s diameter, within ±0.1 mm
Endmillhelical bore at the walllarger than the tool
V-bitrefused

Drilling

  • The drill feeds straight down at the hole’s centre.
  • Pecks (optional): it drills that many mm, rapids up to clear the chips, rapids back down to 0.5 mm above the bottom, and drills on. Turned on, they start at one drill diameter. A hole deeper than three diameters drilled in one go gets a note: the chips can pack and the drill bind.
  • End depth is the depth of the drill’s point. The full diameter ends a little higher, about 0.3 × the diameter for a 118° point.
  • Through takes that into account: the point goes its length deeper, so the full diameter goes Through below the stock (an 8 mm drill in 12 mm stock with 0.5 mm: point at 14.9 mm). The max depth below stock is for the full diameter; the point goes below it.
  • Plain moves: the holes are written as plain G0/G1 moves, not canned cycles, so they run the same on every controller and the checker reads them. The checker allows exactly one kind of rapid below the stock top: straight back into the hole just drilled.

Helical boring

  • The path: the endmill goes down a helix at the hole’s wall, at most step per turn per turn and never steeper than the ramp angle. Then it cuts one level circle at the bottom, for a flat floor and a clean wall.
  • Size: the hole comes out at its drawn size.
  • Any endmill: the helix never plunges, so a tool that isn’t centre-cutting is fine.
  • Too small: a hole must be larger than the tool by at least a fifth of its diameter (a 6 mm endmill bores holes from 7.2 mm). Smaller holes are skipped, with a note that says the smallest; when none is large enough, the operation is refused, saying so.
  • Larger holes: more than twice the tool’s diameter, the helix cuts a ring and leaves a core.
    • In a through hole, the core comes loose at the last turn, with a note. Hold it down, or use a pocket.
    • A blind hole that would leave a core standing is refused: use a pocket.

Order

Holes are cut nearest first, from where the tool is. Where the Holes operation sits in the list decides whether holes come before or after the profiles.

In the file

"type": "holes" in the project file. Lengths are in mm, feeds in mm/min, angles in radians.

FieldSetting
nameName
enabledthe tick on the operation’s row in the Browser
partthe part it belongs to
selectthe geometry: {"layer": "NAME"} or {"shapes": [ids]}
toolTool: the project tool’s id
start_depthStart depth
end_depthEnd depth; for a drill, the depth of its point
feedFeed: the bore’s helix and circle
plunge_feedDrill feed, or the bore’s descent to the start depth
method{"type": "drill", "peck": … or null} or {"type": "bore", "step_down": …, "ramp_angle": …, "direction": …}
dowelsdowel holes for turning the board over (Setups): their through is how far the pins go into the spoilboard, deeper than Max depth below stock allows other cuts; missing: false

Surface

Surface takes a top down flat with a wide endmill. It comes in two kinds:

  • The whole board (a board surface): flatten your spoilboard, or face a board before cutting it. It needs no selection. Right-click the setup, Face the board…; on the command line, chipload op add-surface part.chipload (--setup ID for another setup). It goes first in its setup, since it faces the stock before anything else is cut, and is cut once, whatever parts and instances the setup has.
  • A part’s area (a part surface): select the part’s outline (closed shapes) and add a Surface, as you would a pocket; on the command line, chipload op add part.chipload surface --shapes 1 --margin 3. It faces each selected shape grown by a margin, and is cut under every instance of the part, moving and turning with it. Other parts’ areas are left alone.

A new surface takes the project’s widest endmill and 0.5 mm off the top in one pass; a part surface’s margin starts at the tool’s radius.

How the whole board is cut

  • Back and forth along the stock’s longer side, in straight passes one stepover apart. Half the passes climb, half are conventional: usual for a shallow facing pass, and about half the time of cutting one way only.
  • Past the ends: each pass starts and ends beside the stock, the tool’s edge just clear of it, so the tool goes down in the air, never into the stock. No ramp or helix is needed. Between passes it moves across beside the stock, without lifting.
  • Past the sides: the first and last passes reach past the stock’s long sides by a quarter of the tool, so the edges come out clean even when the stock is a little larger than entered.
  • Depths: with a step down smaller than the end depth, each depth comes back the other way, going down beside the stock where the one before ended.
  • Once: the stock is faced once, even when the part has instances.

The setup sheet says how far the tool runs past the stock’s ends (its radius): keep clamps and fences clear of that.

How a part’s area is cut

  • The area: each selected closed shape grown by the margin; areas that overlap are joined, and a shape inside another is faced over, not left standing.
  • Rings, as a pocket: the area is cleared in rings one stepover apart, the tool going down each depth by a helix, never plunging. Its edge reaches the grown outline.
  • Under each instance: cut for the part and each of its instances, where they lie.

Settings

  • End depth: how much comes off, from the stock top. At most the stock thickness.
  • Step down: the deepest each pass goes. Usually the whole depth in one pass.
  • Stepover: how far each pass moves across, as a fraction of the tool diameter, from 0.1 to 0.9. A new surface takes 0.75.
  • Feed and plunge feed: the plunge feed is for the moves down beside the stock.

The other operations’ depths don’t change: they still count from the stock top as entered. Facing 0.5 mm off means their first 0.5 mm cuts air, which is safe. A surface placed after other operations of its part gets a warning: it would face what they already cut.

Flattening the spoilboard

  1. Make a project whose stock is the spoilboard’s area, Z zero at its top (Stock and work zero).
  2. Copy your surfacing bit into the project (Tool library).
  3. Add a Surface, with 0.3 to 0.5 mm off: enough to take out the dips.
  4. Export, zero Z on the spoilboard, and run it.

In the file

"type": "surface" in the project file. Lengths are in mm, feeds in mm/min.

FieldSetting
nameName
enabledthe tick on the operation’s row in the Browser
partthe part it belongs to
selectthe shapes a part surface faces; none for the whole board
marginMargin: how far a part surface’s area reaches past its shapes, mm
toolTool: an endmill, the project tool’s id
start_depthStart depth
end_depthEnd depth: how much comes off
step_downStep down
stepoverStepover, a fraction of the tool diameter (0.1 to 0.9)
feedFeed
plunge_feedPlunge feed: down beside the stock

Relief

A relief carves a 3D model into the board from the top: an STL file, a picture read as a depth map, or a picture’s black shapes inflated into rounded ones. The tool follows the model in parallel lines, each point as low as it can go without cutting into the model, so a ball nose leaves a smooth surface; nothing under an overhang is reached.

Everything about it is on the 3D models page, as a relief is made from a model:

  • A relief in one step: the Relief… wizard, or chipload relief add.
  • Adding a model, a relief from a picture and inflated shapes.
  • The Relief operation: its lines, stepover, angle, start depth and depth.
  • A rim and a ramp: a flat round the model and a ramp up to the surface, Fuller filling the outline out.
  • Roughing first and smoothing the steps: the passes before the finish.

Tool library

Your tools, kept between projects in tools.json in your settings folder. A project copies the tools it uses, so its G-code never depends on the library: it cuts the same on another computer, or after you change the library.

In the app

Your tools are under Library > Tools at the top of the Browser. Library on the ribbon opens it, as does Library > Tool library.

  • Click a tool to edit it in Properties. Changes are a draft, marked unsaved in the Browser, until you press Save; Revert drops them.
  • Drag a tool onto the project’s Tools (or right-click it > Add to project) to copy it into the project.
  • Right-click a tool for Make default (new projects start with it) and Delete, or Library > Tools for New tool, Import… and Export….

Project tools and the library

Each project tool remembers the library tool it was copied from, by an id that stays when the library tool is renamed. Its row in the Browser shows that tool’s name (“← 6 mm 2F”). In its properties, Library tool names it in a dropdown, with a line under it that says how they stand:

It saysMeaningYou can
same as in your librarysame valuesnothing to do
differs from your library: rpm 18000 -> 16000one of them was changedUpdate from library (copies the library’s values into the project), or Save to library (the other way)
library tool “6 mm 2F” is not in your librarydeleted there, or the project comes from another computerSave to library… under a name
not from your librarymade in the projectSave to library… under a name
  • Pick another tool in the dropdown to give the project tool its values.
  • ⋯ beside it: Save as new… keeps the project tool’s values under another name; Show in library opens the library tool.
  • A new operation gets the library tool’s cutting data while the project tool still has the library tool’s values.
  • After you change a library tool, its properties list the open project’s copies that now differ (“T3 in this project differs: diameter 6 mm -> 6.35 mm”), each with Update in this project.
  • When you open a project, the report lists each tool that differs from its library tool; click a line to open the tool, or Update all from library (also in the right-click menu of the project’s Tools, and on the Tools page). Nothing changes until you choose: a project keeps the tools it was made with, so a proven job cuts as it did.
  • A spindle speed that the setup’s material set from the tool’s cutting data is not a difference, and updating keeps it.

What a tool has

The project tool’s values (Tools and tool changes), plus:

  • Name: how you find it, e.g. “6 mm 2F upcut”.
  • Tool number: the id it gets in a project, when that number is free there.
  • Flutes: used for the chip load.
  • Cutting data (optional): your own feeds and step downs, one row per material. See below.

Cutting data

How hard a bit can work depends on how wide it cuts. A slot, the full width of the bit, loads it most: a profile through the stock, a follow path, a V-carve, a bore, and a pocket’s first ring at each depth. A cut at a stepover, the rest of a pocket’s rings and a surface, takes only part of the width, so it can go deeper at once. So a row of cutting data has two sets of values:
Spindle speedthe speed the row is for; The tool’s when it’s the tool’s own
Plunge feedgoing straight down, and drilling
Slot feed, Slot step downfull-width cuts
Clearing feed, Clearing step down, Stepovercuts at a stepover

There is one row per material you cut with the tool, and one for Any material, used without a material or for a material the tool has no row for. In the tool’s properties, Cutting data lists the rows, each folded to a summary (slot 1500 / 2.5 · clearing 1800 / 5 × 0.4); open one to edit it, Remove it, or Add a material… for another row. A new row starts from the Any material row.

The values fill an operation when you choose a material for it, change its tool, or add it, never by themselves. Each operation takes the ones it uses: a profile its slot values, a pocket both (its full-width rings go down in slot steps, see Pocket), a surface the clearing ones. Choosing a material for a whole setup also sets the project tool’s speed from the row. When an operation’s own material is for another speed than the tool runs at, its Material row says so.

Without cutting data, a new operation copies the nearest operation above it with the same tool, or starts from the tool’s diameter: 250 mm/min per mm (a 6 mm tool: 1500 mm/min), a third of that to plunge, 1 mm step down. Check the chip load before cutting.

Libraries from before Chipload 0.32 had one set of values per tool, and the values per tool in each material. The first time 0.32 reads such a library, they become rows: slot and clearing the same, so nothing cuts differently until you change them. The old files are kept as .bak, and the report says what moved.

On the command line

chipload tool list                                     # the tools, and where the file is
chipload tool add "6 mm 2F" --diameter 6 --flutes 2 --rpm 18000
chipload tool cutting "6 mm 2F" --material any --plunge-feed 400 \
  --slot-feed 1500 --slot-step-down 2 --feed 1800 --step-down 4 --stepover 0.4
chipload tool cutting "6 mm 2F" --material Oak --rpm 20000 --slot-feed 1200   # a row there keeps the rest
chipload tool cutting "6 mm 2F" --material Oak --remove
chipload tool add "V 60" --type vbit --diameter 12.7 --angle 60 --tip 0.2
chipload tool add "5 mm drill" --type drill --diameter 5 --rpm 3000
chipload tool set "6 mm 2F" --rpm 20000                # or --rename
chipload tool default "6 mm 2F"
chipload tool use "V 60" part.chipload                 # copy into a project; prints its id there
chipload tool save part.chipload --tool 2 --name "3 mm downcut"   # links tool 2 to it
chipload tool compare part.chipload                # each project tool: same, differs, not in the library, or not from it
chipload tool update part.chipload [--tool 3]      # tools that differ take the library's values
chipload tool remove "5 mm drill"

A new row needs every value but --rpm (without it, the tool’s own speed); a row that is there keeps the values you don’t give. tool list shows each tool’s rows.

Sharing

chipload tool export tools.json [--name …] writes your tools, or some of them, to a file. chipload tool import FILE adds a file’s tools:

  • tools you don’t have are added;
  • tools with the same name and the same values are left alone;
  • each tool keeps its id, so projects made on the other computer stay linked;
  • tools with the same name but other values are listed, and kept as yours unless you add --replace.

The app has the same: right-click Library > Tools for Import… and Export…. The file format is documented, so a script can make one, e.g. from a supplier’s catalogue.

Machine profiles

A machine profile keeps a machine’s values, so new projects start with them:

  • the post-processor;
  • safe Z, the travel, the max feed;
  • the spindle direction;
  • how tool changes are written;
  • the work zero.

Profiles are kept in machines.json in your settings folder. As with tools, a project copies the values; the profile is only where new projects start.

In the app

Your profiles are under Library > Machines in the Browser. Library > Machines opens it, or Library on the ribbon (with the tools).

  • Click a profile to edit it in Properties; Save writes it.
  • Right-click it for Use in this project, Make default and Delete.

In Machine:

  • Profile names the project’s profile in a dropdown; pick another to give the project its machine values. Its safe Z, work zero and max depth below the stock stay as they are.
  • While the project’s machine is the same as its profile, the machine’s values (post, spindle, max feed, tool changes, travel) are shown greyed under From the profile. Edit here makes them editable for this project; it stays linked.
  • When they differ, each value that differs has a ↶ button: hover it for the profile’s value, click it to take it. Update from library takes them all.
  • Safe Z, the work zero and the max depth below the stock belong to the job: they’re always editable and never count as differing. A profile only gives a new project its first values for them; choosing another machine later keeps them.
  • A line under the dropdown says how the machine stands to its profile, as for tools, with Update from library and Save to library when they differ. ⋯ beside the dropdown has Save as new… and Show in library.

A profile whose post is one of your own post-processors copies that post into the project.

On the command line

chipload machine list
chipload machine add "Shapeoko" --post grbl --safe-z 10 --travel 838,838,95 --max-feed 5000
chipload machine set "Shapeoko" --safe-z 15        # or --rename
chipload machine default "Shapeoko"
chipload import part.dxf -o part.chipload --machine "Shapeoko"   # start from a profile
chipload machine use "Shapeoko" part.chipload                   # its machine values into an existing project
chipload machine save part.chipload --name "Shapeoko 4"         # links the project to it
chipload machine compare part.chipload                          # same, differs, not in the library, or not from a profile
chipload machine export machines.json ; chipload machine import machines.json
chipload machine remove "Shapeoko 4"

chipload machine add --help lists every value. The file format is documented.

Materials

A material is a name, “Plywood”, “Oak”, “MDF” or “Plywood careful”, that you choose for an operation or for a whole setup. Its numbers are your own: each tool keeps them in its cutting data, one row per material. Choose a material, and the operations cut with their tools’ rows for it.

Chipload has no built-in materials and doesn’t calculate feeds. Catalogue values are made for rigid industrial machines; a hobby router wants far less. As an example, a Shapeoko cutting wood with a 6 mm 2-flute bit runs about 1500 mm/min with a 2–2.5 mm step down. Start from what you know works on yours.

A material is about feeds only: choosing one never changes the stock or any depth.

Making one

Library > Materials > New material, then name it. Its page lists the tools with values for it; click one to open its cutting data. To give it values:

  • From a job that cut well: choose the material in an operation’s Material list, under its tool. Cut, and adjust the operation’s feeds or step down until they’re right. Its Material row then says Changed from Plywood: click Save to Plywood. The values the operation uses go into its tool’s Plywood row (a profile its slot values, a pocket both); the row’s others stay. A tool without a Plywood row gets one.
  • By hand: open the tool (Library > Tools), and under Cutting data, Add a material….

The tool must be in your tool library: the values belong to that library tool, so every project that uses it finds them. A tool only in the project is saved to the library first (Save to library… on the tool).

Choosing a material

For one operation: its Material list, under Tool. The operation takes its tool’s row for the material, else its row for Any material: the values it uses (a profile the slot values, a pocket both, a surface the clearing ones and its own wide stepover). A material the tool has neither row for is listed as “Plywood (no values for this tool)”; choosing it changes nothing yet, and Save to Plywood fills it from the operation. None leaves the operation’s feeds as they are.

For every operation: the setup’s Material, or Use for all operations on a material. As one step (one Undo takes it back) every operation takes the material and its values; an operation whose tool has no values there keeps its own, and the report says so. Each project tool also takes its row’s spindle speed. Operations you add later start with this material.

For new projects: Make default on a material (chipload material default Plywood, --none to clear it). A new project, empty or imported, starts with it, as it starts with the default machine and tool; the report says so.

The board’s thickness for new projects: a material can have its usual Thickness (tick it in the material’s form, or chipload material set Plywood --thickness 18, 0 to clear). The new project dialog fills it in when the material is chosen. Choosing a material for a project never changes its board. For materials without one, New projects: stock thickness on Library > Materials gives the thickness (chipload material thickness 18, --none for the built-in 10 mm).

Changing an operation’s tool takes the new tool’s values for its material, when it has some.

Each operation’s material row says where its values stand: From Plywood, Changed from Plywood (with Use Plywood’s and Save to Plywood), or Plywood has no values for this tool (with Save to Plywood). Editing a feed by hand keeps the material; the row then says Changed from Plywood. When the tool’s Plywood row is for another spindle speed than the tool runs at, the row says that too.

The project keeps only each material’s name (material on each operation, and on the stock for the operations added later). The values are already in the operations, so the project cuts the same on a computer without that material.

On the command line

chipload material add Plywood
chipload tool cutting "6 mm 2-flute" --material Plywood --plunge-feed 500 \
  --slot-feed 1500 --slot-step-down 2.5 --feed 1800 --step-down 5 --stepover 0.4
chipload material list                         # every material with its tools' rows
chipload material show Plywood
chipload material default Plywood              # new projects start with it (--none: no default)
chipload material thickness 18                 # and with stock 18 mm thick (--none: 10 mm)
chipload material use part.chipload Plywood    # every operation, and those added later (--none: no material)
chipload material use part.chipload Plywood --op 2   # operation 2 alone
chipload material save part.chipload --op 2    # operation 2's values into its tool's row (--material NAME)
chipload material set Plywood --rename "Birch ply"
chipload material remove Plywood               # and every tool's row for it
chipload material export materials-backup.json # (--name NAME, repeatable)
chipload material import materials-backup.json [--replace]

chipload op tool on an operation with a material takes the material’s values for the new tool too.

Export and import work as for tools: Import… and Export… on Library > Materials. Importing shows first what it would add, leave alone and keep, with Import, Replace when some differ, or Cancel. A material keeps its id, so the tool rows for it on the other computer still match. Only the materials travel: their rows are in the tools’ file, so export the tools too.

Removing a material removes every tool’s row for it. Materials, names only, live in materials.json in the settings folder, beside the tool library; see the Materials file. The rows live in tools.json.

Styles

A style is one of your operations saved by name, to use again on other shapes in one go: “Cut out 18 mm ply”, “Pocket 6 mm”, “Drill 5 mm”. CamBam users know these as machining styles.

A style keeps the operation’s settings: side, direction, depths (a number, or Through, which follows the next project’s stock), tabs, finishing pass, onion skin, corners, entry, hole method, V-carve passes, rest and floor passes, and the library tool it cuts with. It doesn’t keep the shapes, start points, tabs or corners placed by hand, or the operation’s name and part.

Feeds come from the tool. A style keeps no feeds or step downs of its own: when it is used, they come from its tool’s cutting data for the setup’s material, so one style works in every material your tool has values for, and a better feed saved in the tool reaches every style that uses it. Only a tool without cutting data cuts with the feeds the operation had when it was saved.

Saving a style

Set an operation up as you want it, in any project, then save it under a name: right-click it in the Browser, or the button under its properties, Save as style…, and give the name (a name you already have replaces that style). On the command line:

chipload style save part.chipload --op 3 --name "Cut out 18 mm ply"
chipload style save part.chipload --op 3 --name "Cut out 18 mm ply" --replace   # in place of the old one
  • Its tools must be in your library: the operation’s, and its rest or floor passes’. A project tool that isn’t is refused with “tool 2 isn’t in your library: save it to the library first”.
  • A profile’s side is kept as “by the selection”: the style cuts outlines outside and holes inside, as a new profile does. A profile on the line keeps that.
  • To change a style, open it under Library > Styles (see below), or save an operation again under its name.

Using a style

Select the shapes on the canvas, then one of:

  • Style at the end of the ribbon’s OPERATION group: its menu lists your styles;
  • right-click the canvas, Apply style ▸;
  • press S over the canvas, then the style’s digit: the quick menu numbers the first nine styles 1 to 9 in the order of the Styles list, so moving a style up the list changes its digit;
  • right-click the style under Library > Styles, or open it there: Apply to the selection.

On the command line:

chipload style use part.chipload "Cut out 18 mm ply" --dxf 1       # also --layer, --shapes, --group
  • The operations go at the end of the active part, as a new operation of the kind would, with the style’s settings: a profile style on an outline with its holes gives two, Cut out 18 mm ply (holes) inside first, then (outline) outside with the style’s tabs; the holes get no tabs.
  • The tool: the project’s copy of the style’s library tool, or, when the project has none, the library tool copied in (the command says so).
  • The name is the style’s, numbered when the project already has it (“Pocket 6 mm 2”).
  • Nothing in the project remembers the style afterwards: changing the style later doesn’t change operations made from it.

Layer rules

If you draw each kind of cut on its own layer (CUT_OUTSIDE, DRILL_5, POCKET), a style can do its job by itself: give it a layer pattern, and every layer whose name matches gets the style’s operations, selecting the layer.

  • The pattern: * stands for any run of characters, ? for one, upper or lower case alike: CUT*, DRILL_?, pocket. Nothing else is special.
  • When: on importing a DXF or SVG as a new project (one Ctrl+Z takes the operations away again; chipload import … --no-rules leaves them out), and whenever you choose Operation > Apply layer rules or chipload style apply part.chipload (--dry-run says what it would add). Updating a drawing doesn’t apply them.
  • Order: the styles’ order in Library > Styles, then the layers by name. Every style that matches a layer adds its operations, so a rough and a finish pass can both come from rules.
  • Already cut: a layer an operation already cuts, by its layer or by one of its shapes, is skipped, so applying the rules twice adds nothing.
  • The report says what each rule added, which layers it skipped and why, and which layers no rule matched.

Set the pattern in the style’s properties, Layers (empty for none), or:

chipload style set "Cut out 18 mm ply" --layer "CUT*"
chipload style set "Cut out 18 mm ply" --layer ""          # no rule

A styles file with a layer pattern can’t be read by Chipload 0.36 or older.

Managing styles

Library > Styles in the Browser lists them; hover over one for what it does. Click one for its properties: its name (Rename), Apply to the selection, Delete (also the Del key; the library has no undo, styles.json.bak keeps the file as it was), and below them the style’s operation in the same form an operation has: its tool from your library, a profile’s side (by the selection or on the line) and its sections, folded. Change what you want, then Save (or Revert); until then it says not saved, and Apply waits. Changing a style changes only the style: operations already made with it stay as they are. Its feeds are used only where the tool has no cutting data for the project’s material.

On the command line:

chipload style list                       # every style in a few words, and where the file is
chipload style show "Pocket 6 mm"         # one style, every value by path (--json: all of it)
chipload style set "Cut out 18 mm ply" tabs.count=6 end_depth=18.5   # values by path, as `chipload set`
chipload style rename "Pocket 6 mm" "Pocket 6 mm MDF"
chipload style remove "Pocket 6 mm MDF"
chipload style export styles-backup.json  # --name NAME (repeatable) for some
chipload style import styles-backup.json  # --replace overwrites styles of the same name
chipload style set "Cut out 18 mm ply" --tool "6 mm 3F"   # another tool of your library
  • From another computer: a style whose tool isn’t in your library is imported with a note, and lists as not in your library. A project that already has a copy of that tool can use it; another is refused until you give the style a tool with style set … --tool.

The file

Styles are kept in styles.json in the settings folder, beside tools.json. Each style’s operation is stored as a project stores it, with the version of the project format it was written in: when a later Chipload changes operations, the styles are carried over by the same steps as projects, the old file kept as styles.json.bak. A style that can’t be carried over is named and left out; the others load.

Post-processors

A post-processor turns Chipload’s toolpath into the G-code dialect your controller reads. Five are built in:

PostForFilesTool changes
grblGRBL 1.1, grblHAL, FluidNC and Carbide Motion routers (and senders such as UGS, CNCjs, gSender).ncone file per tool, or one file with T… M6 for a sender that handles the change
linuxcncLinuxCNC.ngcone file per tool
mach3Mach3 and Mach4.tapone file per tool
massoMASSO G2 and G3.ncone file per tool
acornCentroid Acorn and other CNC12 controls.cncone file per tool

chipload post list names the controllers each post is for, and so does the Post list in the app.

Only the GRBL post has been run on a real controller so far. The others’ programs say so in a header comment: check the program and air-cut before you cut. If you run one of them successfully, please report it.

Every post writes arcs as G2/G3, mm and absolute coordinates, and waits 3 s after starting the spindle. Each writes its controller’s own dialect, from the controller’s own manual: MASSO’s wait is written in milliseconds (G4 P3000), as MASSO reads it; the Centroid post comments with ;; neither writes G91.1, since both read I and J from the arc’s start anyway. The post-processor reference lists every post’s settings.

Choosing the post

  • App: Machine > Post. The list shows the built-in posts, then yours.
  • Command line: chipload post use part.chipload --post linuxcnc.
  • Machine profile: a profile names its post, and a project made from the profile gets it.

Your own post

A post is two files in the posts folder of your settings folder:

  • NAME.toml, the settings (reference);
  • the template the settings name (template = "NAME.nc.j2"), a Jinja text that writes the program.

The easiest start is a copy of a built-in post, from the crates/chipload-post/posts/ and templates/ folders of the source. Rules:

  • Names: a post can’t take a built-in name (grbl, linuxcnc, mach3, masso, acorn).
  • Controllers and the wait: controllers = ["My router"] names what the post is for, shown by post list; dwell_unit = "ms" writes the spindle’s wait in milliseconds, for a controller whose G4 reads P that way (seconds otherwise).
  • Copied into the project: a project that uses your post keeps its own copy of both files, so it gives the same G-code on another computer or after you change the files. When your files differ from the project’s copy, the app shows Update next to the Post box (command line: run post use again).
  • Sandboxed: the template sees only the values below, can’t read files, and stops with an error if it loops without end.
  • The checker still decides. Whatever your template writes, the checker reads it back as a controller would. It accepts only the codes it knows: G0–G3, G4, G17, G20, G21, G90, G91.1, G94, the setup codes G40, G49, G80, G54, G61 and G64 P (at most 0.05 mm; P is in the program’s units, so at most 0.002 under G20), M3–M6, M7–M9 and M30, and % lines. Anything else, such as G41/G42 cutter compensation, G43 tool lengths, G28 or G53 machine moves, or G91 relative moves, makes the check fail, and nothing is exported.

chipload post list shows every post and why one can’t be used, and chipload post show NAME what one writes (extension, decimals, arcs, tool changes). In the app they are under Library > Post-processors in the Browser. chipload render writes a bare toolpath through a post, for trying a template (the result isn’t checked: use run to cut).

What the template sees

Value
post_namethe settings’ name
units_wordG21 or G20: the machine’s G-code units
unitmm or inch, for comments
tool_id, tool_diameter, rpmthe (first) tool, its diameter in the program’s units, its spindle speed
spindle"cw" or "ccw"
safe_zthe safe height, in the program’s units
dwellthe wait after starting the spindle, in seconds (or milliseconds with dwell_unit = "ms"), or none
work_zerowhere the work zero is, as text for a comment, or none
filewith one file per tool: number, count, tool (text) and operations (text); otherwise none
toolswith one file and tool changes: a list of the tools as text; otherwise none
segmentsthe program in parts: one, or one per tool with tool changes
segment.changebefore every part but the first: comment, tool_id, rpm; otherwise none
segment.movesthe moves: code (G0, G1, G2, G3), and x, y, z, i, j, f (each none when unchanged)
move.commenttext to write as a comment before the move, made safe for G-code: where each part starts, with two parts or more; otherwise none

Numbers come already formatted in the program’s units: with the settings’ decimals in mm, inch_decimals (4 if missing) in inches.

Inches. A post writes inch G-code only if its settings say its template can: units = ["mm", "inch"], and the template writes {{ units_word }} where it would write G21. A post without units writes mm only, and a project whose machine is set to inch is refused with that post, so a template that writes G21 itself never gets inch numbers.

The built-in GRBL template shows how they fit together:

{{ units_word }} G90 G17 G94
{{ "M3" if spindle == "cw" else "M4" }} S{{ rpm }}
{% if dwell is not none %}G4 P{{ dwell }}{% endif %}
{% for s in segments %}
{% for m in s.moves %}
{% if m.comment is not none %}
({{ m.comment }})
{% endif %}
{{ m.code }}{% if m.x is not none %} X{{ m.x }}{% endif %}{% if m.y is not none %} Y{{ m.y }}{% endif %}{% if m.z is not none %} Z{{ m.z }}{% endif %}{% if m.i is not none %} I{{ m.i }} J{{ m.j }}{% endif %}{% if m.f is not none %} F{{ m.f }}{% endif %}
{% endfor %}
{% endfor %}
M5
M30

Problems

The app checks the project while you edit, before anything is generated:

  • On the field: a field with a problem turns red, e.g. an end depth above the start depth, an oversize as large as the tool radius, or a tool without a spindle speed. Hovering it says why.
  • On the operation: its row in the Browser gets a red dot, and hovering it shows the first problem.
  • Above the form: a strip lists them all; click one to go to its operation.

Generating is blocked until they’re fixed; warnings don’t block it. On the command line, chipload check-project part.chipload lists the same problems with the path of each field (operations.1.end_depth), and chipload set can fix it by that path (Scripting).

Problems and warnings

Blocks generatingExamples
Problemyesdepths out of order; a cut deeper than Max depth below stock; a feed above the machine’s max feed; a helix radius at least the tool radius; a V-bit without an angle; a pocket with a drill
Warningnoa cut into the spoilboard; a tool of unknown centre-cutting with a plunge; material the pocket’s rings miss; a tab that couldn’t move off a corner; open contours skipped

Warnings appear in the report under the canvas after generating, on the command line as warning: … lines, and on the setup sheet under Watch out.

Generating

  • F5 (Generate at the ribbon’s right end) plans the toolpath, writes the G-code and runs the checker.
  • With View > Update toolpath automatically (on by default), this happens by itself half a second after each change, in the background. The app stays responsive, and a running generation can be cancelled: Cancel takes Generate’s place meanwhile.
  • Out of date: a toolpath on the canvas that no longer matches the project is drawn faded until the next generation.

Export (Ctrl+E) is only possible once the current project has generated and passed the check.

3D view, simulation and playback

3D view

3D, on the canvas’s navigation bar, shows the toolpath at its real depths.

  • Turn: left-drag.
  • Pan: right-drag.
  • Zoom: the scroll wheel.
  • Back to top view: Top on the navigation bar, View > Top view or T.
  • Straight at a side: click a face of the cube in the canvas’s top right corner (TOP, FRONT, RIGHT, …).
  • Shallow cuts: View > Z scale in 3D stretches depth, for cuts too shallow to see.

Simulation

The simulation cuts the stock with every move of the program and shows the result, in Simulate mode: the Edit | Simulate switch on the canvas’s navigation bar, View > Simulate or F6, and Edit or F6 to go back (Edit and Simulate).

  • Top view: a shaded picture.
  • 3D: the board as a solid you can turn: holes cut through are open, and the board stands on its sides.
  • Tool shapes: each tool’s real shape is used: a flat endmill, a V-bit’s cone with its tip, and a drill’s 118° point.
  • When: in the background, when you enter Simulate and after each new toolpath while you’re in it; in Edit nothing is simulated. Until it’s done, the canvas says Updating the simulation… with a bar and how far it has got, and still shows the stock as the last toolpath cut it. A toolpath being made shows the same way, Updating the toolpath…: a relief’s lines are counted as they are done. Back in Simulate without changes, the last simulation shows at once. With no toolpath yet, entering Simulate makes one.
  • What makes a new toolpath: a change that can change the cut. Drawing a shape that no operation selects doesn’t, unless the board fits the drawing and grows with it, or the shape lies off the stock.
  • Without the toolpath: View > Show toolpath (also Toolpath on the navigation bar and a checkbox on the canvas legend) hides the toolpath’s lines, plunges and tabs. During playback, the tool still shows where it is.
  • The stock alone: turn off Drawing on the navigation bar (or View > Show drawing) as well, and only the simulated stock is left.

Use it to see what the toolpath really leaves:

  • material in corners;
  • a missing pass;
  • a cut into the wrong place;
  • the floor of a V-carve.

The report gives the volume removed and the deepest cut.

On the command line, chipload simulate part.chipload prints the same numbers and writes a shaded top view (part-sim.png). --after N stops after the first N moves.

Both sides of a board. A back side is simulated on its front’s result: the front is cut first, then the back from below, so you see the finished part. The report adds the area cut through and, where neither side alone goes through but the two together do, The two sides meet with its area: a plain note, as that can be meant. chipload simulate part.chipload --setup 2 --face back draws the back face, the board as it lies turned over; --face front the front. A back setup’s picture shows its own face by default.

In the window, Simulate mode has Turn over on the navigation bar for a board with two sides: it makes the other side’s setup the active one, as clicking that setup does. The back is shown back face up, as it lies for its setup, in the top view and in 3D, its toolpath and shapes turned with it; pressed again, the front is active and face up. In Edit mode the board is always seen from the front, where you draw.

Playback

Under the canvas, a slider runs through the program by time, like a 3D printer’s preview:

  • Dragging: the moves done so far are drawn, the rest faint, and the tool is shown where it is.
  • With the simulation on: the stock is shown as cut up to that point, also part of the way along a long straight move.
  • Marks on the slider show where each operation starts and where the tool changes.
  • The readout shows the time so far and in all, the operation and copy, the tool’s X, Y and Z, and the feed.

Keys:

  • ← / → one move;
  • Shift + ← / → to the next mark;
  • Home / End the start or the end.

▶ plays it at ×1, ×10 or ×100 the estimated speed.

Cycle time and chip load

Cycle time

After generating, the report gives the program’s estimated time, split into cutting, rapids and spindle start-up, with one line per operation. With several parts, each part’s time comes first, with its operations under it. With several tools, it also gives a time per file and the number of tool changes. Hovering an operation in the Browser shows its time, and so does the setup sheet.

Hover the total to see what it leaves out:

  • Feeds as programmed: the estimate uses the programmed feeds, and moves at the full feed from start to end.
  • Real time is longer: a real machine slows down in corners and while accelerating. Take the estimate as a lower bound for jobs with many short moves.
  • Rapids move at the machine’s max feed (Machine). Without one, the estimate assumes 5 000 mm/min and says so: set the max feed for a better estimate.

chipload plan part.chipload prints the same, and --json gives it as numbers.

Statistics of an operation

The last section of an operation’s properties, Statistics, says what the operation does, from the last run. Folded, it shows the operation’s time. Open, it shows:

  • Time: the total.
  • Cutting: the length of the feed moves that go sideways: lines, arcs, ramps and helices, measured along the move (a ramp counts its way down too), and their time.
  • Plunging: the length of the feed moves straight down or up: plunges and drilling, and their time.
  • Rapids: the length of the rapid moves (G0), up and down included, and their time.
  • Lifts: how many times the tool goes up at rapid: between paths, and at each peck of a peck drill. Many lifts in an operation can mean time spent in the air.

The numbers are for all of the part’s instances, and a rest pass or floor pass has its own. After a change they are out of date: the section asks you to generate (F5), and says Generating… while the live preview runs. An operation that is off says so.

Statistics of a part

A part’s properties end with the same Statistics section: the sum of its operations’ numbers (and their rest and floor passes’), all of its instances included, and how many operations were counted. It’s what one part costs in time, cutting and lifts, without adding up its operations by hand. The moves from one operation to the next and the tool changes aren’t in it; they are in the job’s total in the report.

While one part is shown alone, the other parts weren’t planned: their section asks you to generate, and the part shown counts the one cut you see, not its copies. Select the setup or the project to count them all. A part that is off says so, and a part without operations says No operations.

chipload run ends its time lines with the whole job’s lengths and lifts (In all: …), the moves between operations included. chipload plan gives each operation’s numbers on its line, and each part’s on its line when there are two parts or more. --json has them for each operation and the job: cutting_mm, plunge_mm, rapid_mm, lifts, and the seconds cutting_s (plunges included), plunge_s and rapid_s; and in estimate.parts, for each part that was planned, with its id, name, seconds and operations.

Chip load

For each operation, Chipload shows the chip load: how thick a chip each cutting edge takes, in mm per tooth:

chip load = feed / (spindle speed × flutes)
  • Too thin and the tool rubs and heats up.
  • Too thick and it may break.

The right value depends on the material, the tool and the machine, so Chipload shows the number and doesn’t judge it. As a rough guide:

  • wood and plastics with 3–12 mm tools often take 0.05–0.25 mm per tooth;
  • aluminium takes 0.02–0.08.

Check the tool maker’s figures.

Setting it up:

  • Where: in an operation’s Feeds, on a Chip load row under the feed.
  • Flutes and spindle speed: the tool needs both, on the tool (click it under Tools in the Browser) or in the tool library. Until then the row says which is missing.
  • Feed: a drill’s chip load uses its feed down; everything else uses the cutting feed.

Setup sheet

With the G-code, Chipload writes a setup sheet for the operator: an HTML page next to it, part-sheet.html. Open it in a browser, or print it. It holds:

  • Time: the estimated cycle time.
  • Stock and zero: the stock’s size and thickness, where X0 Y0 and Z0 are, and the instances. With several parts, each part with its instances. Which parts are cut mirrored, and for an inlay’s plug how to glue it into its pocket and take its back off.
  • A top view: the stock, the part, each file’s or tool’s cuts, the tabs, and the X0 Y0 mark.
  • Files and tools in order: for each file or tool change, the tool to put in, the operations it cuts, and the time.
  • Operations:
    • with several parts, under each part’s name;
    • their kind, side and direction;
    • their depths and passes;
    • feeds with the chip load;
    • entry, tabs and time.
  • Watch out: every warning and check warning.
  • Turn the board over, on a back side’s sheet (Setups): how to turn the board and put it back in place; its picture shows the board as it lies, back face up.

The sheet is written:

  • in the app, by File > Export G-code when Preferences > Setup sheet in the Browser is on (the default; it applies to every project);
  • on the command line, by chipload run unless you add --no-sheet;
  • alone, by chipload sheet part.chipload -o sheet.html.

Its lengths and feeds are in the app’s units (the command line writes mm). When the machine’s G-code is in inches, the top of the sheet says so: G-code in inches (G20).

Its words are in the app’s language; the command line writes English unless you add --lang de to chipload sheet (or --sheet-lang de to chipload run; also fr, es, it, sv). Names, numbers and the G-code are never translated.

Scripting Chipload

Everything Chipload does can be driven from scripts: the command line takes every action, --json gives results a program can read, get/set change any value of a project, and JSON Schemas describe the files. (Design: docs/specs/scripting.md.) An AI assistant can drive the same commands through MCP.

JSON output

Add --json to any command, before or after it (chipload --json plan part.chipload or chipload plan part.chipload --json). The command then prints exactly one JSON object on stdout, on one line, and nothing else. This holds when it fails too.

Every object has these keys:

Key
chipload_json1: the version of this format. It goes up if a key changes meaning or is removed.
oktrue when the command did what it was asked (exit code 0).
notesWarnings and notes: the lines the command prints without --json.
writtenFiles written, absolute paths, in order.
errorOnly when ok is false: {"message": "…", "problems": [{"path", "short", "text"}, …]}.

problems are the project’s problems, as the GUI shows them, each with the path of the field it is about (operations.0.rest). Pass that path to set to fix the field.

The exit codes are the same with or without --json:

  • 0: done;
  • 1: failed, including a refused check or problems found;
  • 2: the command line itself is wrong (clap’s message, not JSON).

Each command’s own keys

CommandKeys
planprogram, groups, pockets, material_left, ring_starts, tabs, estimate, chip_load
runfiles (path, tool, operations), sheet (path or null), estimate
simulatenumbers (removed_cm3, deepest_z, below_bottom_mm, cell_mm, …), image (path or null)
inspectunits, issues, bbox, contours, … (the import report)
importreport (as inspect)
updateunchanged, changed, added, removed (shape ids), lost (operation indexes)
checkerrors, warnings (line, message)
shape add, shape copyids
place (no options)placement, size
getvalue
set, check-projectproblems
tool list, machine listpath, default, tools / machines (as in the library file)
tool useid, added
tool import, machine importadded, same, differing, replaced
post listpath, posts (name, builtin, problem)
rendergcode
licensestext

Operations and moves are indexed from 0 in JSON. Times are in seconds and lengths in mm. Object keys come out sorted.

schema prints the schema itself, with or without --json (see below).

Reading and changing values: get and set

A path is keys and array indexes joined by dots, as in the project file, counting from 0: stock.thickness, operations.1.end_depth, tools.0.rpm. Note that --op on op tool and op rest counts from 1, as Chipload numbers operations on screen.

The stock and the work zero belong to a setup (setups.0.stock.thickness, setups.0.work_zero); stock.… and machine.work_zero still work and mean the active setup’s, so older scripts keep working.

A part’s instances belong to the setups too, each naming its part and number (setups.0.instances.1.at, setups.0.instances.1.angle); the part as first placed is its instance 1. The paths of Chipload 0.37 and before (parts.0.at, parts.0.angle, parts.0.instances.0.at, parts.0.setup) are refused, with the path that holds the value now.

chipload get part.chipload operations.1.end_depth          # 6.0
chipload set part.chipload operations.1.end_depth=6.5 operations.1.name=Corners
chipload set part.chipload 'operations.1.rest={"after_tool": 1}'
chipload set part.chipload operations.1.rest=null           # turn an optional setting off
chipload set part.chipload "operations.3=$(chipload get part.chipload operations.0)"   # with 3 operations: append a copy of the first
chipload check-project part.chipload                        # problems with their paths

How set works:

  • Values: each value is JSON if it parses (6.5, true, null, {"after_tool": 1}), and text otherwise (Corners). For text that looks like JSON, quote it: name='"6"'.
  • Adding: setting the index one past the end of a list appends to it. A missing optional field is added.
  • Checked before writing: the whole project is loaded again before anything is written. A misspelt field, a wrong type, or a value the format refuses stops it, and the file stays as it was.
  • Problems don’t stop it: a project that loads but would be refused at generation (a rest tool smaller than the pocket’s, say) is written, and its problems are listed, as the GUI keeps an edit with a red field.
  • Fixed fields: schema_version and written_by can’t be set.

For one run only, run, plan, simulate and sheet take --set PATH=VALUE, which you can repeat. The project file isn’t changed:

chipload run part.chipload --set stock.thickness=18 -o part-18mm.nc

JSON Schemas

chipload schema project  > chipload-project.schema.json
chipload schema tools    > chipload-tools.schema.json
chipload schema machines > chipload-machines.schema.json

The same files are in this repository under docs/schemas/.

What they cover:

  • Current version only: they describe the files at the current version. Older projects still load; Chipload upgrades them, and saving writes the current version.
  • What they check: field names and types, required fields, and the allowed values of choices.
  • What they don’t check: ranges and rules between fields, such as “stepover between 0 and 1” or “clearance less than the tool radius”. Use chipload check-project for those.
  • Descriptions: every field has one, taken from Chipload’s source.

VS Code (completion and checking while editing a project by hand): in your settings,

"files.associations": { "*.chipload": "json" },
"json.schemas": [
  { "fileMatch": ["*.chipload"], "url": "file:///path/to/chipload-project.schema.json" }
]

Recipes

Chipload has no batch command: the shell does that better, in parallel too. These need jq for the JSON.

Every project in a folder to G-code, listing failures (bash):

for p in *.chipload; do
  chipload run "$p" -o "${p%.chipload}.nc" --json |
    jq -r --arg p "$p" 'if .ok then empty else "\($p): \(.error.message)" end'
done

In parallel, four at a time:

ls *.chipload | xargs -P 4 -I{} sh -c 'chipload run "{}" -o "$(basename "{}" .chipload).nc" > /dev/null || echo "failed: {}"'

One project at several stock thicknesses:

for t in 12 15 18; do
  chipload run part.chipload --set stock.thickness=$t \
    --set operations.0.end_depth=$(echo "$t + 0.3" | bc) -o "part-${t}mm.nc"
done

Cycle times into a CSV:

echo "project,seconds" > times.csv
for p in *.chipload; do
  chipload plan "$p" --json | jq -r --arg p "$p" 'select(.ok) | "\($p),\(.estimate.total_s)"' >> times.csv
done

Check every project (for example after changing a post-processor):

for p in *.chipload; do chipload check-project "$p" --json | jq -r 'select(.ok | not) | .error.message'; done

PowerShell (Windows), every project to G-code:

Get-ChildItem *.chipload | ForEach-Object {
  $r = chipload run $_.FullName -o ($_.BaseName + ".nc") --json | ConvertFrom-Json
  if (-not $r.ok) { "$($_.Name): $($r.error.message)" }
}

And in parallel (PowerShell 7):

Get-ChildItem *.chipload | ForEach-Object -Parallel {
  chipload run $_.FullName -o ($_.BaseName + ".nc") --json | ConvertFrom-Json
} -ThrottleLimit 4 | Where-Object { -not $_.ok } | ForEach-Object { $_.error.message }

AI assistants (MCP)

An AI assistant such as Claude can work in Chipload for you. You describe the part (“cut the outline out of 12 mm plywood with four tabs, and pocket the slots 4 mm deep”), and the assistant makes the project, sets up the operations, checks them, plans and simulates them, and writes the G-code when you ask.

It does this through chipload mcp, a server for the Model Context Protocol (MCP), which assistants use to call programs’ tools. (Design: docs/specs/mcp.md.)

Setting it up

The assistant starts chipload mcp itself and talks to it over its input and output. Nothing listens on the network.

  • Claude Code: in your project’s folder, run

    claude mcp add chipload -- chipload mcp

    Give the full path to chipload if it isn’t on your PATH. Paths the assistant uses are relative to the folder Claude Code runs in.

  • Claude Desktop and other MCP clients: add a server whose command is chipload with the argument mcp. In Claude Desktop’s claude_desktop_config.json:

    {
  "mcpServers": {
    "chipload": { "command": "/path/to/chipload", "args": ["mcp"] }
  }
}

    On Windows, the command is the full path to chipload.exe, which the installer puts beside the app: C:\\Users\\YOU\\AppData\\Local\\Programs\\Chipload\\chipload.exe (in JSON each \ is written twice). Ask the assistant to use full paths to your files there, as it has no project folder.

What the assistant can do

Every command is a tool with the same options, apart from mcp, reference and render. Each tool gives the same JSON as the command with --json. So the assistant:

  • imports DXFs and pictures, and places them;
  • reads and changes any value with get and set (an operation is added by setting a whole one);
  • reads the project’s problems with their paths, and fixes them;
  • plans and simulates. With an output picture, simulate also shows the assistant the cut stock;
  • writes G-code with run, which, as always, writes only programs that pass the checker.

Tools that only read (inspect, plan, get, check-project, the lists…) are marked so, and your assistant may run them without asking you. For the others, it may ask first.

Safety

The assistant can do what the chipload command can do, and no more.

  • It can’t move your machine. Chipload never does.
  • render, which writes unchecked G-code for testing posts, isn’t offered.
  • A saved project keeps the previous file as .bak.

Look at what it made before you cut:

  • open the project in the app;
  • check the toolpath and the simulation;
  • read the setup sheet;
  • do an air cut.

An assistant can misread a drawing, or choose a side, a depth or a tool you didn’t mean.

Watching it work

Open the project in the app while the assistant works on it. Each time the assistant saves the file, the app loads the new version within a couple of seconds, so you see each change on the canvas, with its toolpath and simulation. Ctrl+Z goes back a step. If you’ve changed something in the app yourself and not saved it, the app asks before loading (The window).

Limits

  • The assistant works on the project’s file, not in the app. What you change in the app reaches the assistant only once you save.
  • It does one thing at a time; planning a big project takes a few seconds.

Command line

Every command takes --json: one JSON object instead of text (see Scripting). chipload --help and chipload COMMAND --help print the same help in the terminal.

chipload inspect

Dump units, entities, contours and issues of a DXF, an SVG or a project

chipload inspect <FILE> [OPTIONS]
ArgumentMeaning
<FILE>A .dxf or .svg drawing or a .chipload project.
--units <UNITS>Units of a DXF that does not declare them. One of: mm, inch.
--px-per-inch <PX_PER_INCH>Pixels per inch of an SVG that gives no real size: 96 (Inkscape, browsers) or 72 (older Illustrator)

chipload new

Create an empty project, to draw in or import a DXF into later (update --from)

chipload new [OPTIONS]
ArgumentMeaning
-o, --output <OUTPUT>The project to write (.chipload) Required.
--machine <MACHINE>The machine profile to start from (default: your default machine)
--material <MATERIAL>The material (default: your default material)
--no-materialNo material, even if your library has a default one.
--thickness <THICKNESS>The board’s thickness (default: the material’s, else your default thickness)
--size <SIZE>The board’s size, X×Y, centred on the drawing (e.g. 600x400, 24inx12in)
--fit <FIT>The board fitted round the drawing with this margin (the default, 10 mm)
--z-zero <Z_ZERO>Where Z zero is: the board’s top, or the spoilboard. One of: top, spoilboard.
--tools <TOOLS>Library tools by name, comma-separated, numbered in this order (default: your default tool); “” for none.

chipload import

Create a project from a DXF or an SVG

chipload import <DXF> [OPTIONS]
ArgumentMeaning
<DXF>The DXF or SVG (.svg, .svgz)
--units <UNITS>Units of a DXF that does not declare them. One of: mm, inch.
--px-per-inch <PX_PER_INCH>Pixels per inch of an SVG that gives no real size: 96 (Inkscape, browsers) or 72 (older Illustrator)
-o, --output <OUTPUT>The project to write (.chipload) Required.
--machine <MACHINE>The machine profile to start from (default: your default machine)
--material <MATERIAL>The material (default: your default material)
--no-materialNo material, even if your library has a default one.
--thickness <THICKNESS>The board’s thickness (default: the material’s, else your default thickness)
--size <SIZE>The board’s size, X×Y, centred on the drawing (e.g. 600x400, 24inx12in)
--fit <FIT>The board fitted round the drawing with this margin (the default, 10 mm)
--z-zero <Z_ZERO>Where Z zero is: the board’s top, or the spoilboard. One of: top, spoilboard.
--tools <TOOLS>Library tools by name, comma-separated, numbered in this order (default: your default tool); “” for none.
--no-rulesDon’t apply your styles’ layer rules (docs/specs/fewer-clicks.md §4); without this, each style with a layer pattern adds its operations for the layers it matches.

chipload update

Replace a project’s imported drawing with a new version of the DXF or SVG

chipload update <PROJECT> [OPTIONS]
ArgumentMeaning
<PROJECT>The project (.chipload)
--from <FROM>The new DXF or SVG. Required.
--units <UNITS>Units of the new DXF, if they differ from the last import. One of: mm, inch.
--px-per-inch <PX_PER_INCH>Pixels per inch of an SVG without a real size, if they differ from the last import.
--dxf <DXF>Which DXF of the project (as dxf list shows); needed when it has several.
-o, --output <OUTPUT>Where to write the result; default: the project itself.

chipload plan

Plan a project’s toolpath without writing G-code

chipload plan <PROJECT> [OPTIONS]
ArgumentMeaning
<PROJECT>The project (.chipload)
--setup <ID>Only this setup (as setup list numbers it); default: every setup for run, the active one otherwise (docs/specs/setups.md §8)
--part <ID>Only this part (as part list numbers it), as if every other part were switched off; may be given more than once (docs/specs/part-export.md) Repeatable.
--set <PATH=VALUE>For this run only: set a project value by path, e.g. stock.thickness=18 (see chipload set). The project file isn’t changed. Repeatable.

chipload run

Generate checked G-code from a project

chipload run <PROJECT> [OPTIONS]
ArgumentMeaning
<PROJECT>The project (.chipload)
-o, --output <OUTPUT>Where to write the G-code. With several tools and one file per tool: part.nc gives part-1-T1.nc, part-2-T2.nc, … Required.
--tool-change <TOOL_CHANGE>For this run only: one file per tool, or one file with M6 tool changes (default: the project’s machine setting) One of: files (One G-code file per tool group), program (One file with T<n> M6 at each tool change).
--no-sheetDon’t write the setup sheet (by default part-sheet.html next to part.nc)
--sheet-units <UNITS>The setup sheet’s lengths and feeds: mm (default) or inch. The G-code’s units are the machine’s (machine.gcode_units) One of: mm, inch.
--sheet-lang <LANG>The setup sheet’s language (default: English). The command line itself stays English. One of: en, de, fr, es, it, sv, ru.
--setup <ID>Only this setup (as setup list numbers it); default: every setup for run, the active one otherwise (docs/specs/setups.md §8)
--piece <NAME>Only this piece of a setup cut in pieces, such as A1 (docs/specs/pieces.md §7); default: every piece.
--part <ID>Only this part (as part list numbers it), as if every other part were switched off; may be given more than once (docs/specs/part-export.md) Repeatable.
--set <PATH=VALUE>For this run only: set a project value by path, e.g. stock.thickness=18 (see chipload set). The project file isn’t changed. Repeatable.

chipload simulate

Simulate the material a project’s program removes (docs/specs/simulation.md): print the numbers and write a shaded top view (PNG). The job is generated and checked as by run; no G-code is written

chipload simulate <PROJECT> [OPTIONS]
ArgumentMeaning
<PROJECT>The project (.chipload)
-o, --output <OUTPUT>Where to write the image (.png); default: the project’s name with -sim.png.
--cell <CELL>Cell size, mm (default: automatic, from the stock’s size)
--no-imagePrint the numbers only.
--after <N>The stock after only the first N moves of the program (docs/specs/playback.md §5), for tests and scripts.
--setup <ID>The setup to simulate (as setup list numbers it); default: the active one.
--piece <NAME>Only this piece of a setup cut in pieces, on its own board; default: the pieces put together (docs/specs/pieces.md §7)
--face <FACE>The face the picture shows: front, or back (the board turned over, as it lies); default: the face the setup cuts (docs/specs/two-sided.md §10.2) One of: front, back.
--set <PATH=VALUE>For this run only: set a project value by path, e.g. stock.thickness=18 (see chipload set). The project file isn’t changed. Repeatable.

chipload sheet

Write a project’s setup sheet for the operator (HTML), without the G-code. The job is generated and checked as by run

chipload sheet <PROJECT> [OPTIONS]
ArgumentMeaning
<PROJECT>The project (.chipload)
-o, --output <OUTPUT>Where to write the sheet (.html) Required.
--gcode <GCODE>The G-code the sheet is for, as given to run -o, for its file names (default: the project’s name with the post’s extension)
--tool-change <TOOL_CHANGE>For this sheet only: one file per tool, or one file with M6 tool changes. One of: files (One G-code file per tool group), program (One file with T<n> M6 at each tool change).
--units <UNITS>Its lengths and feeds: mm (default) or inch. The G-code’s units are the machine’s (machine.gcode_units) One of: mm, inch.
--lang <LANG>Its language (default: English). The command line itself stays English. One of: en, de, fr, es, it, sv, ru.
--setup <ID>Only this setup (as setup list numbers it); default: every setup for run, the active one otherwise (docs/specs/setups.md §8)
--part <ID>Only this part (as part list numbers it), as if every other part were switched off; may be given more than once (docs/specs/part-export.md) Repeatable.
--set <PATH=VALUE>For this run only: set a project value by path, e.g. stock.thickness=18 (see chipload set). The project file isn’t changed. Repeatable.

chipload render

Render a toolpath JSON (as plan --json prints under “program”) through a post-processor. For testing posts; the output is NOT checked, use run to cut

chipload render <PROGRAM> [OPTIONS]
ArgumentMeaning
<PROGRAM>Toolpath JSON file.
--post <POST>Built-in post-processor. Default: grbl.

chipload check

Check a G-code program against a project’s stock, safe height and machine limits

chipload check <GCODE> [OPTIONS]
ArgumentMeaning
<GCODE>G-code file to check.
--job <JOB>The project (or job) the program was generated from. Required.
--tool <TOOL>The project’s tool that cuts this program, for its stock margin (one file of a set written one file per tool). Default: the largest tool the project uses.

chipload op

Change an operation of a project

chipload op add

Add an operation for shapes of the drawing, with the defaults the app gives (docs/specs/styles.md §2): a profile of outlines goes outside and through with tabs, of holes inside and through; of both, two profiles, the holes first. Feeds and step downs come from the tool’s cutting data. Added at the end of the active part

chipload op add <PROJECT> <KIND> [OPTIONS]
ArgumentMeaning
<PROJECT>The project (.chipload)
<KIND>What to add. One of: profile, pocket, vcarve, path, chamfer (A V-bit breaks the top edge of closed shapes (docs/specs/chamfer.md): –width), flute (A groove along open shapes that ramps in and out at their ends (docs/specs/fluting.md): –depth, –ramp), pattern (Grooves of lines, cross-hatch, rings or waves inside closed shapes (docs/specs/fill-pattern.md): –pattern, –spacing, –angle), prism (Closed shapes left standing as roofs with a ridge along their centre (docs/specs/prism.md): –max-depth), moulding (A profile swept along paths (docs/specs/moulding.md): –profile, –radius, –side, –ends), holes, relief (A 3D model carved from the top (docs/specs/stl-relief.md): with –model, of that model within its outline), surface (The part’s top faced flat over closed shapes grown by –margin, under each of its instances (docs/specs/surface-levels.md); the whole board is op add-surface).
--layer <LAYER>Every shape on this layer (with –dxf: on that DXF’s layer)
--shapes <SHAPES>These shapes, by id (as inspect lists them) Repeatable.
--dxf <DXF>All of this DXF or SVG (an id from dxf list)
--group <GROUP>All of this group.
--text <TEXT>All of this text (an id from text list)
--image <IMAGE>All of this traced picture (an id from image list)
--model <MODEL>The outline of this 3D model (an id from model list): a relief of it carves within it (docs/specs/stl-relief.md §4)
--tool <TOOL>The tool’s id in the project; default: the one the app would take.
--depth <DEPTH>End depth instead of the default (not with –through)
--throughCut through the stock, below it by the setup’s max overcut.
--width <WIDTH>A chamfer’s width at the surface, in place of 1 mm; its depth follows from the V-bit’s angle (docs/specs/chamfer.md)
--side <SIDE>A chamfer’s waste side, in place of the one the shapes tell: around them (outside) or within them (inside) One of: outside, inside, left (A moulding’s profile left of an open path as drawn), right (A moulding’s profile right of an open path as drawn).
--ramp <RAMP>A flute’s ramp at both ends, in place of 20 mm (docs/specs/fluting.md); a pattern’s grooves’ ramps, in place of square ends (docs/specs/fill-pattern.md)
--ramp-start <RAMP_START>A flute’s ramp at the shapes’ first ends (as drawn); 0 for a square end.
--ramp-end <RAMP_END>A flute’s ramp at the shapes’ last ends; 0 for a square end.
--ramp-shape <RAMP_SHAPE>A flute’s ramps: straight (an even taper) or curved (easing into the floor) One of: straight, curved.
--pattern <pattern_kind>A pattern’s kind, in place of lines. One of: lines, cross, rings, waves, dashes, herringbone, scales, sunburst.
--spacing <SPACING>A pattern’s spacing, groove centre to centre, in place of twice the groove’s width.
--angle <ANGLE>A pattern’s lines’ angle from the X axis, degrees counter-clockwise; 45 if left out.
--between <BETWEEN>A cross-hatch’s angle between its two sets of lines, degrees (90 squares, 60 diamonds); 90 if left out.
--wavelength <WAVELENGTH>A wave pattern’s wavelength; 4 spacings if left out.
--height <HEIGHT>A wave pattern’s height, top to bottom; one spacing if left out.
--on-lineThe grooves run up to the shapes’ lines, centred on them, instead of staying inside.
--length <LENGTH>Dashes’, herringbone’s or a sunburst’s strokes’ length; three groove widths (at least 4 mm) if left out.
--gap <GAP>The gap between dashes; half a stroke if left out.
--radius <RADIUS>Scales’ radius; twice the spacing if left out.
--pitch <PITCH>A sunburst’s strokes’ distance round its rings; a stroke’s length if left out.
--clipStroke patterns: strokes the shape’s edge cuts are clipped, not left out.
--max-depth <MAX_DEPTH>A prism’s deepest edge, for bevelled shapes with a flat top; left out, as deep as the shapes and the V-bit’s cone need (docs/specs/prism.md)
--profile <PROFILE>A moulding’s profile, in place of a 6 mm cove: with –radius, or –width and –depth for an ogee, or –profile-shape for a drawn one. One of: cove, roundover, ogee, bead, flute, shape.
--profile-shape <PROFILE_SHAPE>The drawn open shape a moulding’s profile is read from (its id)
--ends <ENDS>How a moulding ends on open paths: square, round, or taper (–taper long) One of: square, round, taper.
--taper <TAPER>A tapered end’s length; 30 mm if left out.
--stepover <STEPOVER>The distance between a moulding’s passes across its profile.
--margin <MARGIN>A surface’s margin past its shapes, in place of the tool’s radius (docs/specs/surface-levels.md §3)
-o, --output <OUTPUT>Where to write the result; default: the project itself.

chipload op tool

Set the tool an operation cuts with (docs/specs/tool-per-operation.md §7). The tool must be in the project: copy one in from your library with tool use

chipload op tool <PROJECT> [OPTIONS]
ArgumentMeaning
<PROJECT>The project (.chipload)
--op <OP>The operation’s number as the app lists it (1 = first; get and set paths count from 0) Required.
--tool <TOOL>The tool’s id in the project. Required.
-o, --output <OUTPUT>Where to write the result; default: the project itself.

chipload op remove

Remove an operation, as Delete in the app does

chipload op remove <PROJECT> [OPTIONS]
ArgumentMeaning
<PROJECT>The project (.chipload)
--op <OP>The operation’s number as the app lists it (1 = first; get and set paths count from 0) Required.
-o, --output <OUTPUT>Where to write the result; default: the project itself.

chipload op duplicate

Copy an operation right after itself, in the same part, named “… (copy)”

chipload op duplicate <PROJECT> [OPTIONS]
ArgumentMeaning
<PROJECT>The project (.chipload)
--op <OP>The operation’s number as the app lists it (1 = first; get and set paths count from 0) Required.
-o, --output <OUTPUT>Where to write the result; default: the project itself.

chipload op move

Move an operation to another place in the cutting order (it joins the part of the operation that was there). A surface of the whole board stays first in its setup

chipload op move <PROJECT> [OPTIONS]
ArgumentMeaning
<PROJECT>The project (.chipload)
--op <OP>The operation’s number as the app lists it (1 = first) Required.
--to <TO>The place it goes to, numbered the same way; it joins that operation’s part. Required.
-o, --output <OUTPUT>Where to write the result; default: the project itself.

chipload op add-rest

Add a rest pass to a pocket (docs/specs/rest-passes.md): a smaller endmill that clears what the pocket’s tool (or its last pass’s) leaves, in the pocket’s shapes and depths. It is cut after the run of pockets with the pocket’s tool

chipload op add-rest <PROJECT> [OPTIONS]
ArgumentMeaning
<PROJECT>The project (.chipload)
--op <OP>The pocket’s number as the app lists it (1 = first; get and set paths count from 0) Required.
--tool <TOOL>The smaller tool’s id in the project. Required.
-o, --output <OUTPUT>Where to write the result; default: the project itself.

chipload op add-floor

Give a V-carve a floor pass (docs/specs/vcarve-wide-strokes.md §4): an end mill that clears the floor between the walls of strokes too wide to carve from their centre line, cut before the V-carve. Replaces the floor pass it had

chipload op add-floor <PROJECT> [OPTIONS]
ArgumentMeaning
<PROJECT>The project (.chipload)
--op <OP>The V-carve’s number as the app lists it (1 = first; get and set paths count from 0) Required.
--tool <TOOL>The end mill’s id in the project. Required.
-o, --output <OUTPUT>Where to write the result; default: the project itself.

chipload op add-inlay

Make a V-carve inlay of closed shapes (docs/specs/vcarve-inlay.md): a pocket V-carved in the active part, and in a new setup, the plug board, the plug: the rest of that board carved, mirrored left to right, so the shapes stand as a tapered plug that is glued face down into the pocket

chipload op add-inlay <PROJECT> [OPTIONS]
ArgumentMeaning
<PROJECT>The project (.chipload)
--layer <LAYER>Every shape on this layer (with –dxf: on that DXF’s layer)
--shapes <SHAPES>These shapes, by id (as inspect lists them) Repeatable.
--dxf <DXF>All of this DXF or SVG (an id from dxf list)
--group <GROUP>All of this group.
--text <TEXT>All of this text (an id from text list)
--image <IMAGE>All of this traced picture (an id from image list)
--model <MODEL>The outline of this 3D model (an id from model list): a relief of it carves within it (docs/specs/stl-relief.md §4)
--tool <TOOL>The V-bit’s id in the project; both are carved with it. Required.
--depth <DEPTH>The pocket’s depth; default 5 mm.
--plug-start <PLUG_START>How deep below the plug board’s surface its outline is taken: the plug’s face lies that deep in the pocket, the rest of the pocket’s depth left for glue; default 3 mm.
--gap <GAP>The glue gap: how much farther the plug board is carved, so its back stands above the base; default 1.5 mm.
--floor-tool <FLOOR_TOOL>An end mill’s id for the floor passes of both (the plug’s channel is mostly floor)
--plug-thickness <PLUG_THICKNESS>The plug board’s thickness; default 10 mm.
-o, --output <OUTPUT>Where to write the result; default: the project itself.

chipload op add-roughing

Give a relief a roughing pass (docs/specs/stl-relief.md §14): an end mill or a plain ball nose that takes the material down in levels before the relief, leaving an allowance on the model. Replaces the roughing pass it had

chipload op add-roughing <PROJECT> [OPTIONS]
ArgumentMeaning
<PROJECT>The project (.chipload)
--op <OP>The relief’s number as the app lists it (1 = first; get and set paths count from 0) Required.
--tool <TOOL>The roughing tool’s id in the project. Required.
--allowance <ALLOWANCE>What is left on the model for the finish; default 0.5 mm.
-o, --output <OUTPUT>Where to write the result; default: the project itself.

chipload op add-smoothing

Give a relief a smoothing pass (docs/specs/stl-relief.md §17), after its roughing pass: the surface followed once, lines across the roughing’s, taking the steps the roughing leaves on slopes. Replaces the smoothing pass it had

chipload op add-smoothing <PROJECT> [OPTIONS]
ArgumentMeaning
<PROJECT>The project (.chipload)
--op <OP>The relief’s number as the app lists it (1 = first; get and set paths count from 0) Required.
--tool <TOOL>The smoothing tool’s id in the project, an end mill or a plain ball nose; default the roughing tool.
--allowance <ALLOWANCE>What is left on the model for the finish; default the roughing’s.
-o, --output <OUTPUT>Where to write the result; default: the project itself.

chipload op remove-roughing <PROJECT> [OPTIONS]

chipload op remove-floor

Take a V-carve’s floor pass away (docs/specs/vcarve-wide-strokes.md §4)

chipload op remove-floor <PROJECT> [OPTIONS]
